  // Restore the saved color scheme preference, or
  // enact the browser preference if "automatic", 
  // without expecting DOM load to have completed.
  //
  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();

arXiv:2303.12592v4 [math.RT] 14 Feb 2024 
 
 
 BPS algebras and generalised Kac–Moody algebras from 2-Calabi–Yau categories 
 
 
 Ben Davison
 
 ,  
 Lucien Hennecart
 
  and  
 Sebastian Schlegel Mejia
 
 Address:  School of Mathematics, University of Edinburgh, Edinburgh, UK
 
 Email address:  Ben.Davison@ed.ac.uk 
 
 Email address:  Lucien.Hennecart@ed.ac.uk 
 
 Address:  Max Planck Institute for Mathematics, Vivatsgasse 7, 53111 Bonn, Germany
 
 Email address:  schlegel@mpim-bonn.mpg.de 
 
 Date: August 24, 2026 

 Abstract. 
    
 We determine the structure of the BPS algebra of  2 2 -Calabi–Yau Abelian categories whose stack of objects admits a good moduli space. We prove that this algebra is isomorphic to the positive part of the enveloping algebra of a generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra generated by the intersection cohomology of certain connected components (corresponding to roots) of the good moduli space. Some major examples include the BPS algebras of (1) the category of semistable coherent sheaves of given slope on a K3 surface or, more generally, quasiprojective symplectic surface, (2) semistable Higgs bundles on smooth projective curves, (3) preprojective algebras of quivers, (4) multiplicative preprojective algebras and (5) fundamental groups of (quiver) Riemann surfaces. We define the BPS Lie algebras of  2 2 -Calabi–Yau categories and prove that they coincide with the ones obtained by dimensional reduction from the critical cohomological Hall algebra in the case in which the 2-Calabi–Yau category is the category of representations of a preprojective algebra. Consequences include (1) A proof in full generality of the Bozec–Schiffmann positivity conjecture for absolutely cuspidal polynomials, a strengthening of the Kac positivity conjecture (2) A proof of the cohomological integrality conjecture for the category of semistable coherent sheaves on local K3 surfaces (3) A description of the cohomology (in all degrees) of Nakajima quiver varieties as direct sums of irreducible lowest weight representations over the BPS Lie algebra.

 1.  Introduction 

 
 In this paper, we study connections between generalised Kac–Moody (also known as Borcherds) Lie algebras, and the Borel–Moore homology of stacks of objects of  2 2 -Calabi–Yau (2CY) Abelian categories. 2CY categories feature prominently in geometric representation theory, mathematical physics, algebraic geometry and nonabelian Hodge theory. Concrete examples of such categories include representations of various flavours of preprojective algebras, categories of coherent sheaves on K3 surfaces, and categories of Higgs bundles. We prove our results via the BPS algebras defined in  [ DHS22 ] , following the sequence of papers  [ HM96 ,  KS11 ,  DM20 ] . 
 
 
 In the original paper  [ HM96 ]  on BPS algebras of Harvey and Moore, a physical argument for the appearance of generalised Kac–Moody algebras in algebras of BPS states is given. In the study of operators via correspondences on Hilbert schemes of points on  𝐀 2 \mathbf{A}^{2} , it was shown by Nakajima and Grojnowski  [ Nak97 ,  Gro96 ]  that an infinite-dimensional Heisenberg algebra, which is a very particular generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra, plays a central role. These papers in turn were inspired by the pioneering work of Nakajima  [ Nak94 ] , in which the deep connection between (usual) Kac–Moody Lie algebras and algebras of correspondences on quiver varieties was first uncovered. In this paper we explain how  every  suitably geometric 2CY category gives rise to a (generalised) Kac–Moody Lie algebra. More precisely, we show that the BPS algebra of a 2CY category  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  with good moduli stack of objects is the universal enveloping algebra of one half of a generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra, and that the Cartan datum of this Lie algebra is explicitly given in terms of intersection cohomology of certain coarse moduli spaces of objects in  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . Special cases of (the doubled versions of) these BPS Lie algebras are identified with algebras of correspondences appearing in  [ Nak94 ,  Nak97 ,  Nak98 ,  Gro96 ] ; indeed part of our motivation was to find a unified explanation for all of these results. On the other hand, our results apply to very general 2CY categories. Reflecting the range of 2CY categories in mathematics, we can then use our structural results to prove conjectures in combinatorics, representation theory, and algebraic geometry. 
 
 
 For the combinatorial applications, we consider certain functions counting isomorphism classes of representations of quivers over finite fields. A consequence of the main theorem applied to the category  Rep ⁡ Π Q \operatorname{Rep}\Pi_{Q}  of representations of the preprojective algebra of a quiver is a proof in full generality of the Bozec–Schiffmann positivity conjecture for (absolutely) cuspidal polynomials of quivers  [ BS19 ] . This is a strengthening of the positivity of Kac polynomials conjectured in  [ Kac83 ] . As in the proofs  [ HLR13 ,  Dav18 ,  DGT16 ]  of the Kac positivity conjecture, we prove the positivity of absolutely cuspidal polynomials by identifying their coefficients with dimensions of certain vector spaces, more specifically by identifying these polynomials with intersection Poincaré polynomials of singular Nakajima varieties. We also define and prove the positivity of nilpotent and  1 1 -nilpotent absolutely cuspidal polynomials, which are associated to nilpotent and  1 1 -nilpotent Kac polynomials counting representations of quivers over finite fields satisfying various nilpotency conditions  [ BSV20 ] . 
 
 
 In geometric representation theory, we apply our main result also to understanding the cohomology of Nakajima quiver varieties as representations over the BPS Lie algebra. Applying the main theorem to the category of  Rep ⁡ Π Q 𝐟 \operatorname{Rep}\Pi_{Q_{\mathbf{f}}} -modules, where  Q 𝐟 Q_{\mathbf{f}}  is a framed quiver, we describe a decomposition of the entire cohomology of Nakajima quiver varieties (i.e. in all cohomological degrees) into irreducible lowest weight modules for the (doubled) BPS Lie algebra. Since this Lie algebra contains the usual Kac–Moody Lie algebra associated to the unframed quiver  Q Q , this answers a longstanding question of Nakajima regarding the possibility of extending his famous decomposition of (part of) the cohomology of quiver varieties into irreducible lowest weight modules. This provides a unified framework for constructing the action of Kac–Moody Lie algebras and Heisenberg algebras on cohomology of various quiver varieties  [ Nak97 ,  Nak98 ] , as well as cohomology of more general moduli spaces of objects in 2CY categories. 
 
 
 Within enumerative algebraic geometry, and more specifically cohomological Donaldson–Thomas theory  [ DM20 ,  Sze14 ] , we use our results to prove the cohomological integrality theorem for general 2CY categories and their undeformed 3CY completions. The 2CY version of this conjecture states that there is an isomorphism 
 

 
 
 H BM ⁡ ( 𝔐 𝒜 , 𝐐 ¯ vir ) ≅ Sym ⁡ ( ⨁ a ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) ℱ ¯ a ⊗ H ⁡ ( B ⁡ 𝐂 ∗ , 𝐐 ) ) \HO^{\BoMo}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}},\underline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{vir}})\cong\Sym\left(\bigoplus_{a\in\K(\mathcal{A})}\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{a}\otimes\HO(\B\mathbf{C}^{*},\mathbf{Q})\right) 
 
 
 where  𝔐 𝒜 \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  is the stack of objects in a fixed 2CY category  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . Up to a cohomological shift the left hand side is the Borel–Moore homology of  𝔐 𝒜 \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}} , and each  ℱ ¯ a \underline{\mathcal{F}}_{a}  is a bounded complex of mixed Hodge structures. We prove this by proving a stronger relative version, in terms of the tensor category of mixed Hodge modules on the coarse moduli space  ℳ 𝒜 \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}} . The above isomorphism is reminiscent of a PBW-type isomorphism, and indeed we prove it by producing a (relative) PBW isomorphism for the CoHA associated to  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . Via dimensional reduction  [ Kin22 ] , the theorem implies the cohomological integrality conjecture regarding the 3CY completion of 2CY categories; a concrete consequence is the (refined) integrality theorem for the DT partition function of local K3 surfaces. In the rest of the introduction we explain, connect and motivate these results in more depth. 
 
 
 1.1.  Generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebras 

 
 Our main structural theorems in § 1.2  are expressed in terms of generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebras, and their positive halves, which we briefly introduce in this section. 
 
 
 1.1.1.  Generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebras associated to a monoid with bilinear form and weight function 

 
 Let  ( M , + ) (M,+)  be a monoid. Often, we will take  M M  such that the natural map  M → M ^ M\rightarrow\widehat{M}  to the groupification is an inclusion into a lattice (which becomes convenient when considering Weyl group actions). Let  ( − , − ) : M × M → 𝐙 (-,-)\colon M\times M\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}  be a symmetric bilinear form. In § 3.1 , we define the sets of  primitive positive roots   Σ M , ( − , − ) \Sigma_{M,(-,-)} ,  simple positive roots   Φ M , ( − , − ) + \Phi^{+}_{M,(-,-)}  and the Cartan matrix  A M , ( − , − ) = ( a m , n = ( m , n ) ) m , n ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + A_{M,(-,-)}=(a_{m,n}=(m,n))_{m,n\in\Phi^{+}_{M,(-,-)}} . We assume that  a m , m ∈ 2 ​ 𝐙 ⩽ 1 a_{m,m}\in 2\mathbf{Z}_{\leqslant 1}  and that  a m , n ⩽ 0 a_{m,n}\leqslant 0  if  a m , m = 2 a_{m,m}=2 . For a function 
 

 
 
 P : Φ M , ( − , − ) + → 𝐍 [ t ± 1 / 2 ] m ↦ P m ​ ( t ) \begin{matrix}P&\colon&\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+}&\rightarrow&\mathbf{N}[t^{\pm 1/2}]\\
&&m&\mapsto&P_{m}(t)\end{matrix} 
 
 
 such that  P m ​ ( t ) = 1 P_{m}(t)=1  if  a m , m = 2 a_{m,m}=2 , we define a  M ^ × 𝐙 \widehat{M}\times\mathbf{Z} -graded generalised Kac–Moody algebra  𝔤 M , ( − , − ) , P \mathfrak{g}_{M,(-,-),P}  (in the sense of Borcherds  [ Bor88 ] , see  [ BS19 ]  for the graded version) associated to this datum, by generators and relations. We refer to such a function as a  weight function . The Lie algebra  𝔤 M , ( − , − ) , P \mathfrak{g}_{M,(-,-),P}  has a triangular decomposition  𝔤 M , ( − , − ) , P = 𝔫 M , ( − , − ) , P − ⊕ 𝔥 M , ( − , − ) ⊕ 𝔫 M , ( − , − ) , P + \mathfrak{g}_{M,(-,-),P}=\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{M,(-,-),P}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{M,(-,-)}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{M,(-,-),P} . We also have a description by generators and relations (see § 3.2 ) and a triangular decomposition of its enveloping algebra 
 

 
 
 𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔤 M , ( − , − ) , P ) ≅ 𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔫 M , ( − , − ) , P − ) ⊗ 𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔥 M , ( − , − ) ) ⊗ 𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔫 M , ( − , − ) , P + ) . \UEA(\mathfrak{g}_{M,(-,-),P})\cong\UEA(\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{M,(-,-),P})\otimes\UEA(\mathfrak{h}_{M,(-,-)})\otimes\UEA(\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{M,(-,-),P}). 
 
 
 Both  𝔫 M , ( − , − ) , P + \mathfrak{n}^{+}_{M,(-,-),P}  and  𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔫 M , ( − , − ) , P + ) \UEA(\mathfrak{n}_{M,(-,-),P}^{+})  are  M × 𝐙 M\times\mathbf{Z} -graded. 
 
 
 In concrete situations, the polynomial  P m ​ ( t ) P_{m}(t)  is the graded dimension of some given vector space  V m V_{m} . The above algebra is the unique (up to isomorphism, and some choice regarding the Cartan subalgebra) cohomologically graded generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra with generators in the  m m th root weight space identified with  V m V_{m} . 
 
 
 
 1.1.2.  Positive halves of generalised Kac–Moody (Lie) algebras in symmetric tensor categories of perverse sheaves or mixed Hodge modules 

 
 Let  ( ℳ , ⊕ ) (\mathcal{M},\oplus)  be a commutative monoid in the category of complex schemes, for which we have a decomposition  ℳ = ∐ m ∈ M ℳ m \mathcal{M}=\coprod_{m\in M}\mathcal{M}_{m}  into finite type unions of finite type connected components  ℳ m \mathcal{M}_{m}  of  ℳ \mathcal{M}  (i.e.  ℳ m \mathcal{M}_{m}  might be disconnected but it is a finite type scheme) for some monoid  ( M , + ) (M,+)  as in § 1.1.1 . We assume that this decomposition is respected by  ⊕ \oplus  (we allow  ℳ \mathcal{M}  to have infinitely many connected components). In this case, we do not have a groupification  ℳ ^ \widehat{\mathcal{M}}  with which to define the full generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra with generators for positive roots given by some selection of sheaves on  ℳ \mathcal{M} , but we nonetheless can define the  positive half  of such a Lie algebra. 
 
 
 We let  ( − , − ) : M × M → 𝐙 (-,-)\colon M\times M\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}  be a bilinear form,  Σ M , ( − , − ) \Sigma_{M,(-,-)}  be the set of primitive positive roots,  Φ M , ( − , − ) + \Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+}  be the set of simple positive roots and  A M , ( − , − ) A_{M,(-,-)}  be the Cartan matrix as in § 1.1.1 . We assume that  ℳ m = pt \mathcal{M}_{m}=\mathrm{pt}  for any  m ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + , m\in\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\real}  and that the sum map  + : M × M → M +\colon M\times M\rightarrow M  is finite. 
 
 
 Let  𝒢 ¯ m ∈ MHM ⁡ ( ℳ m ) \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{m}\in\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{m})  be a set of mixed Hodge modules indexed by  m ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + m\in\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+} . We assume that  𝒢 ¯ m = 𝐐 ¯ ℳ m = 𝐐 ¯ pt \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{m}=\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}_{m}}=\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathrm{pt}}  is the constant mixed Hodge module if  m ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + , m\in\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\real} . We let  P P  be the weight function given by the Poincaré polynomials of these  𝒢 ¯ m \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{m} : 
 

 
 
 P : Φ M , ( − , − ) + → 𝐍 [ t ± 1 / 2 ] m ↦ P ⁡ ( 𝒢 ¯ m ) = ∑ j ∈ 𝐙 dim H j ⁡ ( 𝒢 ¯ m ) ​ t j / 2 . \begin{matrix}P\colon&\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+}&\rightarrow&\mathbf{N}[t^{\pm 1/2}]&\\
&m&\mapsto&P(\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{m})&=\sum_{j\in\mathbf{Z}}\dim\HO^{j}(\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{m})t^{j/2}.\end{matrix} 
 
 
 We define  𝔫 ℳ , 𝒢 ¯ + \mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\mathcal{M},\underline{\mathscr{G}}}  to be the free Lie algebra object generated by  𝒢 ¯ = ⨁ m ∈ M 𝒢 ¯ m \underline{\mathscr{G}}=\bigoplus_{m\in M}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{m}  in the symmetric tensor category  MHM ⁡ ( ℳ ) \mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}) , quotiented by the Lie ideal generated by generalised Serre relations (see § 3.2  for the precise construction). The Lie algebra obtained by taking derived global sections of  𝔫 ℳ , 𝒢 ¯ + \mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\mathcal{M},\underline{\mathscr{G}}}  and forgetting the mixed Hodge structures is isomorphic to  𝔫 M , ( − , − ) , P + \mathfrak{n}^{+}_{M,(-,-),P} , (half) the generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra defined as in § 1.1.1 . 
 
 
 Similarly we define  𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔫 ℳ , 𝒢 ¯ + ) \UEA(\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\mathcal{M},\underline{\mathscr{G}}})  to be the free unital associative algebra generated by  𝒢 ¯ \underline{\mathscr{G}} , quotiented by the two-sided ideal generated by the same relations. The algebra obtained by taking derived global sections and forgetting the MHS of  𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔫 ℳ , 𝒢 ¯ + ) \UEA(\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\mathcal{M},\underline{\mathscr{G}}})  is isomorphic to  𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔫 M , ( − , − ) , P + ) \UEA(\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{M,(-,-),P}) . 
 
 
 
 
 1.2.  The BPS algebra of a 2-Calabi–Yau category 

 
 We let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be an Abelian 2-Calabi–Yau category in the sense of  [ DHS22 ] . It is an Abelian subcategory of the homotopy category of some ambient dg category  𝒞 \mathscr{C} . We briefly recall in § 4  the assumptions on  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . In particular, we assume that it has a moduli stack of objects,  𝔐 𝒜 \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  which is an Artin stack with a good moduli space  𝙹𝙷 𝒜 : 𝔐 𝒜 → ℳ 𝒜 \JH_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  (as defined in  [ Alp13 ] ). The good moduli space  ℳ 𝒜 \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  is assumed to be a scheme and it has the monoid structure  ⊕ : ℳ 𝒜 × ℳ 𝒜 → ℳ 𝒜 \oplus\colon\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  induced by the direct sum  ⊕ : 𝔐 𝒜 × 𝔐 𝒜 → 𝔐 𝒜 \oplus\colon\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}} . The complex of mixed Hodge modules  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ≔ ( 𝙹𝙷 𝒜 ) ∗ ​ 𝔻 ​ 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 𝒜 vir ∈ 𝒟 + ​ ( MHM ⁡ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) ) \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq(\JH_{\mathcal{A}})_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}))  is  the relative cohomological Hall algebra , where the multiplication 
 

 
 
 𝔪 : 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ⊡ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 → 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \mathfrak{m}\colon\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\boxdot\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}} 
 
 
 has been defined in  [ DHS22 ] . The superscript  vir \mathrm{vir}  indicates a twist by a certain power of the Tate twist which we make precise in § 6.1 . The complex of mixed Hodge modules  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}  is concentrated in nonnegative cohomological degrees and the degree  0 0  cohomology,  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ≔ ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}) , carries the induced multiplication  ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝔪 ) \mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathfrak{m}) . It is called the  BPS algebra sheaf . 
 
 
 We let  ( − , − ) 𝒞 (-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}  be the Euler form of the category  𝒞 \mathscr{C} . It induces a symmetric bilinear form 
 

 
 
 ( − , − ) 𝒞 : π 0 ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) × π 0 ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) → 𝐙 (-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}\colon\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})\times\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\mathbf{Z} 
 
 
 such that  ( a , a ) 𝒞 ≡ 0 ( mod 2 ) (a,a)_{\mathscr{C}}\equiv 0\pmod{2}  for any  a ∈ π 0 ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) a\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}) . We fix a monoid  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \K(\mathcal{A}) , which we assume is a submonoid of a lattice, and a surjective morphism with finite fibres  cl : π 0 ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) → K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \mathrm{cl}\colon\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\K(\mathcal{A})  such that the Euler form factors through  cl \mathrm{cl} . We choose a bilinear form  ψ \psi  on  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \K(\mathcal{A})  such that for any  a , b ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) a,b\in\K(\mathcal{A}) , we have  ψ ⁡ ( a , b ) + ψ ⁡ ( b , a ) ≡ ( a , b ) 𝒞 ( mod 2 ) \psi(a,b)+\psi(b,a)\equiv(a,b)_{\mathscr{C}}\pmod{2} . We twist the CoHA multiplication  𝔪 \mathfrak{m}  by  ψ \psi :  𝔪 a , b ψ ≔ ( − 1 ) ψ ⁡ ( a , b ) ​ 𝔪 a , b \mathfrak{m}_{a,b}^{\psi}\coloneqq(-1)^{\psi(a,b)}\mathfrak{m}_{a,b}  for  a , b ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) a,b\in\K(\mathcal{A}) , where  𝔪 a , b \mathfrak{m}_{a,b}  is the restriction of  𝔪 \mathfrak{m}  to  ℳ 𝒜 , a × ℳ 𝒜 , b \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},b} , and  ℳ 𝒜 , a \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}  denotes the union of connected components corresponding to  cl − 1 ​ ( a ) \mathrm{cl}^{-1}(a) . This defines the  ψ \psi -twisted multiplication  𝔪 ψ \mathfrak{m}^{\psi}  on the relative algebras  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}} ,  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}  and the absolute algebras  H ∗ ⁡ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \HO^{*}\!\!\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}} ,  BPS ¯ 𝒜 , Alg \underline{\mathrm{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}} . We denote by  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\psi} ,  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ψ \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi} ,  H ∗ ⁡ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ \HO^{*}\!\!\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\psi}  and  BPS ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ψ \underline{\mathrm{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}  the algebras with their twisted multiplications. 
 
 
 We let  Σ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) ≔ Σ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 \Sigma_{\K(\mathcal{A})}\coloneqq\Sigma_{\K(\mathcal{A}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}}  and  Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + ≔ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 + \Phi^{+}_{\K(\mathcal{A})}\coloneqq\Phi^{+}_{\K(\mathcal{A}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}}  be the sets of primitive positive roots and simple positive roots respectively (for the monoid with bilinear form  ( K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 ) (\K(\mathcal{A}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}) ), as in § 3.1 . For  m ∈ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + , iso m\in\Phi_{\K(\mathcal{A})}^{+,\mathrm{iso}} , we write  m = l ​ m ′ m=lm^{\prime}  for  m ′ ∈ Σ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) m^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\K(\mathcal{A})}  and  l ∈ 𝐍 l\in\mathbf{N} . This decomposition of  m m  is unique and we have a natural closed immersion 
 

 
 
 u m : ℳ 𝒜 , m ′ → ℳ 𝒜 , m x ↦ x ⊕ l . \begin{matrix}u_{m}&\colon&\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m^{\prime}}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m}\\
&&x&\mapsto&x^{\oplus l}.\end{matrix} 
 
 
 The (connected components of the) complex scheme  ℳ 𝒜 , m ′ \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m^{\prime}}  are of dimension  2 2  (Proposition  5.4 ) and we let  𝒢 ′ ¯ m = ( u m ) ∗ ​ ( ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 , m ′ ) ) \underline{\mathscr{G}^{\prime}}_{m}=(u_{m})_{*}(\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m^{\prime}})) . Then for  m ∈ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + m\in\Phi^{+}_{\K(\mathcal{A})}  we define 
 

 
 
 𝒢 ¯ m ≔ { ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 , m ) if  ​ m ∈ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + ∖ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + , iso ( u m ) ∗ ​ ( ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 , m ′ ) ) if  m = l m ′ ∈ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + , iso , m ′ ∈ Σ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) . \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{m}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m})&\textrm{if }m\in\Phi^{+}_{\K(\mathcal{A})}\smallsetminus\Phi_{\K(\mathcal{A})}^{+,\mathrm{iso}}\\
(u_{m})_{*}(\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m^{\prime}}))&\textrm{if }m=lm^{\prime}\in\Phi_{\K(\mathcal{A})}^{+,\mathrm{iso}},\;m^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\K(\mathcal{A})}.\end{cases} 
 
 
 The definitions of the intersection complexes  ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( − ) \underline{\mathcal{IC}}(-)  are recalled in § 1.8 . For every  m m ,  𝒢 ¯ m \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{m}  is a semisimple mixed Hodge module on  ℳ 𝒜 , m \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m} , and in the isotropic case it is supported on the  small diagonal  of  ℳ 𝒜 , m \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m}  which we define to be the image of  u m u_{m} . Its restriction to each of the connected components of  ℳ 𝒜 , m \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m}  is simple. 
 
 
 1.2.1.  BPS algebras as GKM algebras 

 
 We let  𝒢 ¯ = ⨁ m ∈ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + 𝒢 ¯ m \underline{\mathscr{G}}=\bigoplus_{m\in\Phi_{\K(\mathcal{A})}^{+}}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{m}  be as defined above. Note that  𝒢 ¯ m ≅ 𝐐 ¯ pt \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{m}\cong\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathrm{pt}}  if  m ∈ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + , m\in\Phi_{\K(\mathcal{A})}^{+,\real} . We can finally state our first main theorem, proved in § 7.7 . 
 
 
 Theorem 1.1 . 
 
 There exists a canonical isomorphism of algebra objects 
 

 
 
 𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔫 ℳ 𝒜 , ( − , − ) 𝒞 , 𝒢 ¯ + ) → ≅ ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ψ \UEA(\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}},(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}},\underline{\mathscr{G}}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi} 
 
 
 in  MHM ⁡ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) \mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}) . 
 
 
 
 Recall that  BPS ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ψ ≔ H ∗ ⁡ ( ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ψ ) \underline{\mathrm{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}\coloneqq\HO^{*}(\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi})  is the algebra formed by taking the derived global sections of the BPS algebra sheaf. It is an ordinary  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) × 𝐙 \K(\mathcal{A})\times\mathbf{Z} -graded algebra (where the copy of  𝐙 \mathbf{Z}  encodes the cohomological grading) and it is called  the BPS algebra . By construction, it has a lift to an algebra object in the category of mixed Hodge structures. 
 
 
 If  ı △ : ℳ 𝒜 △ ↪ ℳ 𝒜 \imath_{\triangle}\colon\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\triangle}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  is a locally closed inclusion of a saturated submonoid, the complex  ı ! △ ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ψ \imath^{!}_{\triangle}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}  inherits the structure of an algebra object in the derived category of mixed Hodge modules on  ℳ 𝒜 △ \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\triangle}  via restriction, and we similarly define the  △ \triangle  BPS algebra  by taking derived global sections: 
 

 
 
 BPS ¯ 𝒜 , Alg △ , ψ ≔ H ∗ ( ℳ 𝒜 △ , ı △ ! ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ψ ) . \underline{\mathrm{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\triangle,\psi}\coloneqq\HO^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\triangle},\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}). 
 
 
 
 
 Applying the functor  ı △ ! \imath_{\triangle}^{!}  and taking derived global sections in Theorem  1.1  implies our main result on BPS algebras: 
 
 
 Theorem 1.2 . 
 
 There is a canonical isomorphism of algebras 
 

 
 
 𝐔 ( 𝔫 K ( 𝒜 ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 , H ∗ ( ı △ ! 𝒢 ) + ) → ≅ BPS 𝒜 , Alg △ , ψ . \UEA(\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\K(\mathcal{A}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}},\HO^{*}(\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\mathscr{G})})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{BPS}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\triangle,\psi}. 
 
 
 and thus an isomorphism of algebras 
 

 
 
 𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔫 K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 , IP 𝒜 △ + ) → ≅ BPS 𝒜 , Alg △ , ψ , \UEA(\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\K(\mathcal{A}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}},\mathrm{IP}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{BPS}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\triangle,\psi}, 
 
 
 where  IP 𝒜 △ \mathrm{IP}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}}  is the weight function 
 

 
 
 IP 𝒜 △ : Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + → 𝐍 [ t ± 1 / 2 ] m ↦ ch 𝐙 ( H ∗ ( ı △ ! 𝒢 m ) ) ≔ ∑ j ∈ 𝐙 dim H j ( ı △ ! 𝒢 m ) t j / 2 . \begin{matrix}\mathrm{IP}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}}\colon&\Phi^{+}_{\K(\mathcal{A})}&\rightarrow&\mathbf{N}[t^{\pm 1/2}]&\\
&m&\mapsto&\mathrm{ch}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\HO^{*}(\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\mathscr{G}_{m}))&\coloneqq\sum_{j\in\mathbf{Z}}\dim\HO^{j}(\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\mathscr{G}_{m})t^{j/2}.\end{matrix} 
 
 
 In particular, there is an embedding 
 

 
 
 BPS 𝒜 , Alg △ , ψ ⊆ 𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔤 K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 , IP 𝒜 △ ) \mathrm{BPS}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\triangle,\psi}\subseteq\UEA(\mathfrak{g}_{\K(\mathcal{A}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}},\mathrm{IP}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}}}) 
 
 
 of the BPS algebra as the positive half of a cohomologically graded generalised Kac–Moody algebra. 
 
 
 
 Explicitly, for  m ∈ Σ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) ⊆ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + m\in\Sigma_{\K(\mathcal{A})}\subseteq\Phi_{\K(\mathcal{A})}^{+} ,  IP 𝒜 △ ​ ( m ) \mathrm{IP}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}}(m)  is the Poincaré polynomial of  H ∗ ( ℳ 𝒜 , m △ , ı △ ! ℐ 𝒞 ( ℳ 𝒜 , m ) ) \HO^{*}\!(\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A},m},\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\mathcal{IC}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m})) , and for  m = l ​ m ′ m=lm^{\prime}  with  m ′ ∈ Σ 𝒜 m^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  isotropic,  IP 𝒜 △ ​ ( m ) \mathrm{IP}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}}(m)  is the Poincaré polynomial of  H ∗ ( ℳ 𝒜 , m ′ △ , ı △ ! ℐ 𝒞 ( ℳ 𝒜 , m ′ ) ) \HO^{*}\!(\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A},m^{\prime}},\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\mathcal{IC}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m^{\prime}})) , so that spaces of simple roots of  𝔤 𝒜 BPS , △ \mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathtt{\aBPS},\triangle}  defined below are identified with  ı △ \imath_{\triangle} -restrictions of intersection complexes on moduli spaces of objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . 
 
 
 We let  𝒜 \mathcal{A} ,  𝔐 𝒜 \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}} ,  ℳ 𝒜 \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  be as in § 1.2 . Motivated by Theorem  1.1 , we define the  relative BPS Lie algebra  of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  as 
 

 
 (1.1) 
 
 ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Lie ≔ 𝔫 ℳ 𝒜 , ( − , − ) 𝒞 , 𝒢 ¯ + , \displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Lie}}\coloneqq\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}},(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}},\underline{\mathscr{G}}}, 
 
 
 we define 
 

 
 (1.2) 
 
 𝔫 ¯ 𝒜 BPS , △ , + ≔ H ∗ ( ℳ 𝒜 △ , ı △ ! ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Lie ) \underline{\mathfrak{n}}^{\mathtt{\aBPS},\triangle,+}_{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq\HO^{*}(\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}},\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Lie}}) 
 
 
 so that 
 

 
 (1.3) 
 
 𝔫 𝒜 BPS , △ , + ≔ \displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{\mathtt{\aBPS},\triangle,+}_{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq 
 H ∗ ( ℳ 𝒜 △ , ı △ ! ℬ 𝒫 𝒮 𝒜 , Lie ) \displaystyle\HO^{*}(\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}},\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Lie}}) 
 
 
 
 ≅ \displaystyle\cong 
 𝔫 + K ( 𝒜 ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 , H ∗ ( ℳ △ 𝒜 , ı △ ! 𝒢 ) \displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\K(\mathcal{A}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}},\HO^{*}(\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}},\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\mathscr{G})} 
 
 
 and define the  full  BPS Lie algebra: 
 

 
 (1.4) 
 
 𝔤 BPS , △ 𝒜 ≔ 𝔤 K ( 𝒜 ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 , H ∗ ( ℳ △ 𝒜 , ı △ ! 𝒢 ) . \mathfrak{g}^{\mathtt{\aBPS},\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq\mathfrak{g}_{\K(\mathcal{A}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}},\HO^{*}(\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}},\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\mathscr{G})}. 
 
 
 By construction, the full BPS Lie algebra is a generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra containing the BPS Lie algebra  H ∗ ( ℳ 𝒜 △ , ı △ ! ℬ 𝒫 𝒮 𝒜 , Lie ) \HO^{*}(\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{\mathcal{A}},\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Lie}})  as its strictly positive half. 
 
 
 
 
 1.3.  Positivity for cuspidal polynomials 

 
 Cuspidal polynomials  C Q , 𝐝 ​ ( q ) C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)  of a quiver  Q Q  were introduced in  [ BS19 ] , and defined as the polynomials counting the dimensions of the graded components of the space of primitive elements of the constructible Hall algebra  H Q , 𝐅 q H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}  over a finite field. The primitive elements are defined using Green’s coproduct on  H Q , 𝐅 q H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}   [ Gre95 ]  and they form a minimal subspace generating the Hall algebra  [ SV01 ] . See  [ Sch18 ]  for motivation and background for this subsection. 
 
 
 Bozec and Schiffmann also defined a closely related family of polynomials, the absolutely cuspidal polynomials  C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( q ) C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{abs}}(q) . They are defined in a combinatorial way from cuspidal polynomials and coincide with the latter for  hyperbolic  dimension vectors. More precisely,  C Q , 𝐝 ​ ( q ) = C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( q ) C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)=C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{abs}}(q)  if  ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) Q < 0 (\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{Q}<0 ,  C Q , 𝐝 ​ ( q ) = C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( q ) = q g i C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)=C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{abs}}(q)=q^{g_{i}}  if  𝐝 = 1 i ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}=1_{i}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  is a basis vector and the vertex  i i  carries  g i g_{i}  loops, and, if  ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) Q = 0 (\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{Q}=0  with  𝐝 \mathbf{d}  indivisible, then the families  ( C Q , l ​ 𝐝 ​ ( q ) ) l ⩾ 1 (C_{Q,l\mathbf{d}}(q))_{l\geqslant 1}  and  ( C Q , l ​ 𝐝 abs ​ ( q ) ) l ⩾ 1 (C_{Q,l\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{abs}}(q))_{l\geqslant 1}  are related by the formula utilising plethystic exponentials (see for example  [ BS19 , §1.5]  for a definition of this combinatorial operation) 
 

 
 
 Exp z ⁡ ( ∑ l ⩾ 1 C Q , l ​ 𝐝 ​ ( q ) ​ z l ​ 𝐝 ) = Exp z , q ⁡ ( ∑ l ⩾ 1 C Q , l ​ 𝐝 abs ​ ( q ) ​ z l ​ 𝐝 ) . \Exp_{z}\left(\sum_{l\geqslant 1}C_{Q,l\mathbf{d}}(q)z^{l\mathbf{d}}\right)=\Exp_{z,q}\left(\sum_{l\geqslant 1}C_{Q,l\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{abs}}(q)z^{l\mathbf{d}}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 Bozec and Schiffmann formulated the following positivity conjecture for the family  ( C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( q ) ) 𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 (C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{abs}}(q))_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}} : 
 
 
 Conjecture 1.3   ( [ BS19 ] ) . 
 
 For  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} , the polynomial  C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( q ) C^{\mathrm{abs}}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)  has nonnegative coefficients. 
 
 
 
 By taking the Serre subcategories of the category  Rep ⁡ ( Q ) \operatorname{Rep}(Q)  of finite dimensional representations of  Q Q , of nilpotent representations,  Rep nil ⁡ ( Q ) \operatorname{Rep}^{\mathrm{nil}}(Q) , and of  1 1 -nilpotent representations,  Rep 1 ​ -nil ⁡ ( Q ) \operatorname{Rep}^{1\snil}(Q)  (§ 9.3.1 ), one can consider the associated constructible Hall algebras  H Q , 𝐅 q nil H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}  and  H Q , 𝐅 q 1 ​ -nil H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{1\snil} . We have inclusions of bialgebras  H Q , 𝐅 Q nil ⊆ H Q , 𝐅 q 1 ​ -nil ⊆ H Q , 𝐅 q H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{Q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}\subseteq H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{1\snil}\subseteq H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}} . As in  [ BS19 ] , we define the nilpotent cuspidal polynomials  C Q , 𝐝 nil ​ ( q ) C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{nil}}(q)  and  1 1 -nilpotent cuspidal polynomials  C Q , 𝐝 1 ​ -nil ​ ( q ) C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{1\snil}(q)  as the dimension counts of primitive elements of the twisted bialgebras  H Q , 𝐅 q nil H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}  and  H Q , 𝐅 1 ​ -nil H_{Q,\mathbf{F}}^{1\snil}  respectively, as well as their absolute versions  C Q , 𝐝 nil , abs ​ ( q ) C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{nil},\mathrm{abs}}(q)  and  C Q , 𝐝 1 ​ -nil , abs ​ ( q ) C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{1\snil,\mathrm{abs}}(q) . We may therefore formulate in the same way the positivity conjecture for these new families of polynomials. 
 
 
 We prove all these conjectures in this paper. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.4   (Theorems  9.5  and  9.12 ) . 
 
 Absolutely cuspidal polynomials have nonnegative coefficients (Conjecture  1.3  is true). Additionally, absolutely cuspidal nilpotent and  1 1 -nilpotent polynomials have nonnegative coefficients. 
 
 
 
 A key fact in the proof of the positivity conjectures is the interpretation of cuspidal polynomials as graded multiplicities of simple roots. Namely, for the monoid  M = 𝐍 Q 0 M=\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  with the symmetrised Euler form and a weight function  P P  as in § 1.1.1 , there is a combinatorial formula giving the character of the positive part of the GKM Lie algebra  𝔤 M , ( − , − ) , P \mathfrak{g}_{M,(-,-),P}  (a generalization of the Weyl character formula). In the graded context, it is described in details in  [ BS19 ] . By letting  P P  vary, we obtain a function 
 

 
 
 W Q : { P : Φ M , ( − , − ) + → 𝐍 [ q ± 1 / 2 ] } → 𝐍 [ q ± 1 / 2 ] [ [ M ] ] . W_{Q}:\{P\colon\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbf{N}[q^{\pm 1/2}]\}\rightarrow\mathbf{N}[q^{\pm 1/2}][\![M]\!]. 
 
 
 sending a weight function to the character of the corresponding positive part of the generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra. 
 
 
 Proposition 1.5   ( [ BS19 ] ) . 
 
 The map  W Q W_{Q}  is injective and if  Exp z , t ⁡ ( ∑ 𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 A Q , 𝐝 ​ ( t ) ​ z 𝐝 ) \Exp_{z,t}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}A_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(t)z^{\mathbf{d}}\right)  is in its image, then it is the image under  W Q W_{Q}  of the weight function  𝐝 ↦ C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( t ) \mathbf{d}\mapsto C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{abs}}(t)  (we denote by  A Q , 𝐝 ​ ( q ) A_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)  the Kac polynomials of  Q Q  counting absolutely indecomposable  𝐝 \mathbf{d} -dimensional representations of  Q Q  over  𝐅 q \mathbf{F}_{q} ). 
 
 
 
 This proposition is proven in [loc. cit.] by an iterative inversion of the Weyl character formula for graded GKM algebras. For the nilpotent versions of cuspidal polynomials, we have an analogue of Proposition  1.5  for the nilpotent and  1 1 -nilpotent Kac and cuspidal polynomials. Proposition  1.5  implies that the positivity of cuspidal polynomials is a strengthening of the positivity of Kac polynomials of quivers proven in  [ HLR13 ] . Simultaneously, our results prove a strengthening of the Kac constant term conjecture  [ Kac83 ] , proved originally in  [ Hau10 ] . This conjecture states that the constant terms of the Kac polynomials are equal to the root multiplicities of a certain Kac–Moody Lie algebra  𝔤 Γ \mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma} ; we show that the  entire  Kac polynomial is the characteristic function of a larger (generalised) cohomologically graded Kac–Moody Lie algebra containing  𝔤 Γ \mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma}  as its (cohomological) degree zero piece. 
 
 
 Our proof of the positivity of cuspidal polynomials also gives a cohomological interpretation of their coefficients. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.6   (Theorem  9.5 ) . 
 
 Let  𝐝 ∈ Σ Π Q \mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} . Then, 
 

 
 
 C Q , 𝐝 ( q ) = IP ( ℳ Π Q , 𝐝 ) ( q − 1 / 2 ) . C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)=\mathrm{IP}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}})(q^{-1/2}). 
 
 
 
 
 
 This interpretation gives a way to calculate the intersection Poincaré polynomials of quiver varieties. The same way that Hua’s formula  [ Hua00 ]  gives a way to calculate Kac polynomials by giving an effective formula for the plethystic exponential of their generating series, the above results enable us to recover the intersection Poincaré polynomials from the same series. 
 
 
 The intersection Poincaré polynomial of any quiver variety can be recovered using Theorem  1.6 , the canonical decomposition of dimension vectors of quivers  [ Cra02 , Theorem 1.1]  which induces a decomposition of Nakajima quiver varieties  [ Cra02 ] , and the formula for the intersection Poincaré polynomial of symmetric products (see Corollary  9.7  for the general result). 
 
 
 
 1.4.  PBW theorems and cohomological integrality in Donaldson–Thomas theory 

 
 The BPS algebra  BPS 𝒜 , Alg ψ \aBPS^{\psi}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}  is defined to be the zeroeth part of the perverse filtration on the cohomological Hall algebra  H ∗ ⁡ 𝒜 𝒜 \HO^{*}\!\mathscr{A}_{\mathcal{A}}  associated to the morphism  𝙹𝙷 𝒜 \JH_{\mathcal{A}} , defined in  [ DHS22 ] . In particular, it is a subalgebra of  H ∗ ⁡ 𝒜 𝒜 \HO^{*}\!\mathscr{A}_{\mathcal{A}} . Theorem  1.2  completely determines the structure of this algebra, up to determining the intersection Poincaré polynomials of some singular varieties associated to simple roots (and in the case  𝒜 = Rep ⁡ Π Q \mathcal{A}=\operatorname{Rep}\Pi_{Q}  this can be done algorithmically using absolutely cuspidal polynomials, as explained in § 1.3 ). 
 
 
 Although Theorem  1.2  only concerns a subalgebra of the CoHA, we can use the same techniques to write down an explicit PBW isomorphism for the  entire  CoHA. To start with, in § 8.3.2  we prove a stronger, “relative” PBW isomorphism in the category of complexes of mixed Hodge modules on the coarse moduli space  ℳ 𝒜 \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}} : 
 
 
 Theorem 1.7   (Relative PBW isomorphism) . 
 
 Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be a  2 2 -Calabi–Yau Abelian category satisfying the assumptions of § 4 . After choosing a positive determinant line bundle  det : 𝔐 𝒜 → B ⁡ 𝐂 ∗ \det\colon\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\B\mathbf{C}^{*} , we have a canonical isomorphism 
 

 
 
 Ψ 𝒜 ψ : Sym ⊡ ⁡ ( ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Lie ⊗ H ∗ ⁡ ( B ⁡ 𝐂 ∗ , 𝐐 ¯ ) ) → ≅ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ \Psi^{\psi}_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Lie}}\otimes\HO^{*}(\B\mathbf{C}^{*},\underline{\mathbf{Q}})\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\psi} 
 
 
 of complexes of mixed Hodge modules in  𝒟 + ​ ( MHM ⁡ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) ) \mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})) , with  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Lie \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Lie}}  defined as in ( 1.1 ). The isomorphism is obtained by evaluating symmetric tensors in  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Lie ⊗ H ∗ ⁡ ( B ⁡ 𝐂 ∗ , 𝐐 ¯ ) \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Lie}}\otimes\HO^{*}(\B\mathbf{C}^{*},\underline{\mathbf{Q}})  using the ( ψ \psi -twisted) Hall algebra structure on the target. 
 
 
 
 See § 8.3.2  for the definition of a positive determinant bundle. Applying the restriction functor  ı △ ! \imath_{\triangle}^{!}  and taking derived global sections, we obtain the following corollary. 
 
 
 Corollary 1.8   (PBW isomorphism) . 
 
 Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be a  2 2 -Calabi–Yau Abelian category satisfying the assumptions of § 4 . Let  𝒜 △ ⊆ 𝒜 \mathcal{A}^{\triangle}\subseteq\mathcal{A}  be a Serre subcategory. After choosing a positive determinant line bundle, we have a canonical PBW isomorphism 
 

 
 
 H ∗ ( ı △ ! Ψ 𝒜 ψ ) : Sym ( 𝔫 ¯ 𝒜 BPS , △ , + ⊗ H ∗ ( B 𝐂 ∗ , 𝐐 ¯ ) ) → ≅ H ∗ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 △ , ψ = H BM ( 𝔐 𝒜 △ , 𝐐 ¯ vir ) \HO^{*}(\imath_{\triangle}^{!}\Psi^{\psi}_{\mathcal{A}})\colon\Sym\!\left(\underline{\mathfrak{n}}^{\mathtt{\aBPS},\triangle,+}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\HO^{*}(\B\mathbf{C}^{*},\underline{\mathbf{Q}})\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\HO^{*}\!\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\triangle,\psi}=\HO^{\BoMo}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\triangle},\underline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{vir}}) 
 
 
 of  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) × 𝐙 \K(\mathcal{A})\times\mathbf{Z} -graded mixed Hodge structures, with  𝔫 ¯ 𝒜 BPS , △ , + \underline{\mathfrak{n}}^{\mathtt{\aBPS},\triangle,+}_{\mathcal{A}}  defined as in ( 1.2 ). This isomorphism is obtained via the inclusion of cohomologically graded mixed Hodge structures  𝔫 ¯ 𝒜 BPS , △ , + ⊗ H ∗ ⁡ ( B ⁡ 𝐂 ∗ , 𝐐 ) ↪ H ∗ ⁡ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 △ , ψ \underline{\mathfrak{n}}^{\mathtt{\aBPS},\triangle,+}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\HO^{*}(\B\mathbf{C}^{*},\mathbf{Q})\hookrightarrow\HO^{*}\!\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\triangle,\psi}  determined by our choice of determinant line bundle, and the evaluation of symmetric tensors using the ( ψ \psi -twisted) cohomological Hall algebra structure on the target. 
 
 
 
 As usual, we emphasize that the isomorphisms of Theorem  1.7  and Corollary  1.8  are  not  algebra morphisms, although both sides have their own algebra structure. The left-hand sides have the algebra structures given by the  Sym \Sym  construction while the right-hand sides have the cohomological Hall algebra structure. However, the isomorphism  Ψ 𝒜 ψ \Psi^{\psi}_{\mathcal{A}}   is  constructed using the algebra structure on  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\psi} . This is similar to the usual PBW theorem for enveloping algebras, which gives an isomorphism, after passing to underlying vector spaces, between a polynomial algebra and the enveloping algebra. 
 
 
 If we choose  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  to be the 2-Calabi–Yau category of  Π Q \Pi_{Q} -representations, the existence of  some  cohomological integrality isomorphism as in Theorem  1.7  and Corollary  1.8  is (via dimensional reduction  [ Dav17a ] ) a particular case of the PBW isomorphism of Davison–Meinhardt (see  [ DM20 ] , then  [ Dav23a ] ), and purity of the BPS sheaf is one of the main results of  [ Dav20 ] . However, even in this case, the precise determination of the IC complexes making up the BPS sheaf is a new result. 
 
 
 1.4.1.  Donaldson–Thomas theory 

 
 The main application of Corollary  1.8  in enumerative geometry concerns the cohomological Donaldson–Thomas (DT) theory of local K3 surfaces, which we explain here. To start with, let  X X  be a projective 3CY variety, meaning that  X X  is smooth, three-dimensional, and has trivial canonical bundle,  𝒪 X ≅ K X \mathscr{O}_{X}\cong K_{X} . Donaldson–Thomas theory, as introduced in  [ Tho00 ] , assigns numbers to certain (fine) moduli schemes  ℳ \mathcal{M}  of coherent sheaves on  X X , using perfect obstruction theories to produce a degree zero virtual fundamental class of  ℳ \mathcal{M} . Thomas then defines  # DT ​ ( ℳ ) \#_{\DT}(\mathcal{M})  to be the degree of this class. This theory was subsequently generalised in multiple directions. Firstly it was proved by Behrend  [ Beh09 ]  that the count  # DT ​ ( ℳ ) \#_{\DT}(\mathcal{M})  could be calculated as a weighted Euler characteristic of a certain constructible function  ν ℳ \nu_{\mathcal{M}}  (the Behrend function), so that it makes sense to define this number even if  ℳ \mathcal{M}  is not projective. In particular, we can replace  X X  with a local K3 surface. Secondly, via the papers of Joyce–Song  [ JS12 ]  and Kontsevich–Soibelman  [ KS08 ] , a theory was developed that removed the need to study only fine moduli spaces  ℳ \mathcal{M} , i.e. stacks  𝔐 \mathfrak{M} , possibly containing strictly semistable objects, are handled by their theory. Finally, an effective theory of DT mixed Hodge modules on stacks of objects in 3CY categories was developed in the sequence of papers  [ Bra+15 ,  BBJ19 ,  Ben+15 ] ; associated to a moduli stack  𝔐 \mathfrak{M}  of objects in a 3CY category  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , equipped with an orientation in the sense of  [ Joy15 ] , they define the mixed Hodge module  ϕ DT \phi_{\DT} , the pointwise Euler characteristic of which is equal to the Behrend function. We recover the weighted Euler characteristics (where they make sense) by taking actual Euler characteristics of derived global sections of  ϕ DT \phi_{\DT} . 
 
 
 Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be a 2CY category with a good moduli space, satisfying the assumptions of § 4 . A motivating example for us comes from setting  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  to be the category of Gieseker semistable coherent sheaves, of fixed slope, on a polarised K3 surface  S S . In order to obtain a 3CY category, we take the 3CY completion  𝒜 ~ = Π 3 ​ ( 𝒜 ) \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\Pi_{3}(\mathcal{A})  in the sense of  [ Kel11 ] . It is shown in  [ BCS20 ]  that the shifted cotangent stack  𝕸 ~ m = T ∗ ⁡ [ − 1 ] ​ 𝕸 m \bm{\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}}_{m}=\T^{*}[-1]\bm{\mathfrak{M}}_{m}  is equivalent to a substack of the (derived) stack of objects in  𝒜 ~ \widetilde{\mathcal{A}} . Being the classical truncation of a  ( − 1 ) (-1) -shifted cotangent stack,  𝔐 ~ m = t 0 ​ ( 𝕸 ~ m ) \widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}_{m}=t_{0}(\bm{\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}}_{m})  carries a canonical orientation, and indeed a canonical d-critical structure, determining the mixed Hodge module  ϕ DT \phi_{\DT}  on it; see  [ Kin22 ]  for the details. In [loc. cit] it is moreover proved that there is a canonical  dimensional reduction  isomorphism, for each  m ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) m\in\K(\mathcal{A}) : 
 

 
 
 H ⁡ ( 𝔐 ~ m , ϕ DT ) ≅ H BM ⁡ ( 𝔐 m , 𝐐 vir ) \HO(\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}_{m},\phi_{\DT})\cong\HO^{\BoMo}(\mathfrak{M}_{m},\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}) 
 
 
 where the  vir \mathrm{vir}  superscript denotes a system of Tate twists that we do not make explicit in this introduction. 
 
 
 A foundational/definitional conjecture in Donaldson–Thomas theory is the  integrality conjecture . In the enumerative version of the theory this is the claim that certain partition functions with coefficients given by DT invariants are obtained by taking plethystic exponentials of partition functions involving simpler invariants, called BPS invariants: see  [ KS08 ]  for an exact formulation in the enumerative setup. This enumerative version is implied by a stronger  cohomological  version of the integrality theorem: 
 
 
 Conjecture 1.9 . 
 
 Let  𝒞 \mathcal{C}  be a 3CY Abelian category on which the Euler form vanishes and  cl : π 0 ​ ( 𝔐 𝒞 ) → K ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) \mathrm{cl}\colon\pi_{0}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{C}})\rightarrow\K(\mathcal{C})  is a surjective morphism to a monoid. Then there is some isomorphism of  K ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) \K(\mathcal{C}) -graded, cohomologically graded mixed Hodge structures 
 

 
 
 ⨁ m ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) H ⁡ ( 𝔐 𝒞 , m , ϕ ¯ DT ) ≅ Sym ⁡ ( ⨁ 0 ≠ m ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) BPS ¯ 𝒞 , m ⊗ H ⁡ ( B ⁡ 𝐂 ∗ , 𝐐 ¯ ) ) \bigoplus_{m\in\K(\mathcal{C})}\HO(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{C},m},\underline{\phi}_{\DT})\cong\Sym\left(\bigoplus_{0\neq m\in\K(\mathcal{C})}\underline{\aBPS}_{\mathcal{C},m}\otimes\HO(\B\mathbf{C}^{*},\underline{\mathbf{Q}})\right) 
 
 
 where each  BPS ¯ 𝒞 , m \underline{\aBPS}_{\mathcal{C},m}  has bounded cohomological amplitude, and finite total dimension. 
 
 
 
 Via dimensional reduction, we obtain the following from Theorem  1.7 . 
 
 
 Theorem 1.10 . 
 
 Conjecture  1.9  is true for 3CY categories of the form  𝒜 ~ = Π 3 ​ ( 𝒜 ) \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\Pi_{3}(\mathcal{A})  for  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  a 2-Calabi–Yau category satisfying the assumptions of § 4 . In particular, if  X = Tot ⁡ ( K S ) ≅ S × 𝐀 1 X=\operatorname{Tot}(K_{S})\cong S\times\mathbf{A}^{1}  for  S S  a smooth quasiprojective surface satisfying  K S ≅ 𝒪 S \K_{S}\cong\mathscr{O}_{S} , the cohomological integrality conjecture holds for the category of Gieseker semistable 1 1 
                     1 
                    
                    
                    
                   With respect to a polarisation of a compactification of  S S .  compactly supported coherent sheaves of fixed slope on  X X . 
 
 
 
 In the case  𝒜 = 𝐇𝐢𝐠𝐠𝐬 θ ​ ( C ) \mathcal{A}=\mathbf{Higgs}_{\theta}(C) , the category of semistable Higgs sheaves of fixed slope  θ \theta  on a smooth projective curve  C C ,  𝒜 ~ = Π 3 ​ ( 𝒜 ) \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\Pi_{3}(\mathcal{A})  can be identified with the category of semistable coherent sheaves of pure dimension one on  Tot C ⁡ ( 𝒪 C ⊕ K C ) \operatorname{Tot}_{C}(\mathscr{O}_{C}\oplus K_{C}) , and Conjecture  1.9  is proved in  [ KK21 ] , building on  [ KM21 ,  MS20 ] . In the case in which  g ⁡ ( C ) ⩾ 2 g(C)\geqslant 2 , the precise determination of  BPS ¯ 𝒜 ~ \underline{\aBPS}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}  in terms of intersection cohomology was obtained in our earlier work  [ DHS22 ] , while for  g ⁡ ( C ) ⩽ 1 g(C)\leqslant 1  it can be found in  [ Dav23 ] . 
 
 
 Remark 1.11 . 
 
 We may alternatively  define   BPS ¯ 𝒞 , m ≔ H ⁡ ( ℳ 𝒞 , m , ℋ 1 ​ ( ( 𝔐 𝒞 → ℳ 𝒞 ) ∗ ​ ϕ ¯ DT ) ) \underline{\aBPS}_{\mathcal{C},m}\coloneqq\HO(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C},m},\mathcal{H}^{1}((\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}})_{*}\underline{\phi}_{\DT}))  and make the stronger conjecture that for this choice of mixed Hodge structure, there is an isomorphism as in Conjecture  1.9 . The local version of this conjecture (for Jacobi algebras) is the main result of  [ DM20 ] ; see § 1.6  for this special case. Proving this stronger version of the integrality conjecture is beyond the scope of the current paper, and forms the subject of an upcoming paper by the first-named author and Tasuki Kinjo. 
 
 
 
 
 
 1.5.  Lowest weight modules from Nakajima quiver varieties 

 
 Part of the inspiration for constructing generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebras out of 2CY categories was the famous work of Nakajima on irreducible highest/lowest weight representations constructed out of cohomology of quiver varieties, and work of Nakajima and Grojnowski on irreducible highest/lowest weight representations of Heisenberg algebras on cohomology of Hilbert schemes. In this section we explain how we recover and extend this theory. 
 
 
 Given a quiver  Q Q  without loops, and a framing dimension vector  𝐟 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{f}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} , Nakajima constructs in  [ Nak98 ]  an action of the Kac–Moody Lie algebra  𝔤 Γ \mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma}  on  ⨁ 𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 H 0 ⁡ ( N ⁡ ( Q , 𝐝 , 𝐟 ) , 𝐐 vir ) \bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}\HO^{0}(N(Q,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}),\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})  where  Γ \Gamma  is the underlying graph of  Q Q ,  𝔤 Γ \mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma}  is the associated Kac–Moody Lie algebra, and  N ⁡ ( Q , 𝐝 , 𝐟 ) N(Q,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f})  are Nakajima quiver varieties (we recall the definition in § 10.2 , along with the cohomological shifts bundled into the superscript  vir \mathrm{vir} ). Furthermore, this representation is shown to be the irreducible lowest weight representation of lowest weight  ∑ i ∈ Q 0 f i ​ δ i \sum_{i\in Q_{0}}f_{i}\delta_{i} , where the  δ i \delta_{i} s denote the fundamental weights. Note that the same correspondences considered in  [ Nak98 ]  act on the entire cohomology  𝕄 𝐟 ​ ( Q ) ≔ ⨁ 𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 H ∗ ⁡ ( N ⁡ ( Q , 𝐝 , 𝐟 ) , 𝐐 vir ) \mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}\HO^{*}(N(Q,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}),\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}) , suggesting the following question: 
 
 
 Question 1.12 . 
 
 For fixed  Q Q  and  𝐟 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{f}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} , what is the decomposition of the  entire  cohomology  𝕄 𝐟 ​ ( Q ) \mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)  into irreducible lowest weight  𝔤 Γ \mathfrak{g}_{\Gamma} -modules? 
 
 
 
 Next, let  Q Q  be instead the Jordan quiver; this is the quiver with one vertex and one loop. We fix the framing vector  f = 1 f=1 ; then there is an isomorphism  N ⁡ ( Q , d , 1 ) ≅ Hilb d ⁡ ( 𝐀 2 ) N(Q,d,1)\cong\Hilb_{d}(\mathbf{A}^{2}) . Again,  𝕄 1 ​ ( Q ) \mathbb{M}_{1}(Q)  is acted on by an algebra of correspondences (Nakajima’s raising and lowering operators), and it is proved in  [ Nak97 ,  Nak99 ]  that the resulting Lie algebra of correspondences  𝔤 \mathfrak{g}  is an infinite-dimensional Heisenberg algebra. Moreover, it is proven that  𝕄 1 ​ ( Q ) \mathbb{M}_{1}(Q)  is an irreducible lowest weight module for this Lie algebra. Note that this is a long way from being a special case of the result in the paragraph above Question  1.12 , since  Q Q  only contains a loop. On the other hand, the algebra  𝔤 \mathfrak{g}  is a (very degenerate example of a)  generalised  Kac–Moody Lie algebra, containing  only  imaginary simple roots. 
 
 
 These two central results in the geometric representation theory of quiver varieties motivate the following more ambitious question: 
 
 
 Question 1.13 . 
 
 For fixed  Q Q , is there a natural generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra  𝔤 ~ Q \widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{Q}  of correspondences, containing the Kac–Moody Lie algebra associated to the real subquiver (see § 2 ), for which, for fixed  𝐟 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{f}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} , the module  𝕄 𝐟 ​ ( Q ) \mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)  admits a concrete decomposition into irreducible lowest weight modules? 
 
 
 
 Although Question  1.13  is more subjective, we still venture to answer it with a “yes” in this paper. Firstly, we propose the (full) BPS Lie algebra  𝔤 Π Q BPS \mathfrak{g}^{\mathtt{\aBPS}}_{\Pi_{Q}}  introduced at ( 1.4 ) to be the Lie algebra  𝔤 ~ Q \widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{Q} . Then the decomposition into irreducible lowest weight representations, with lowest weight spaces identified with intersection cohomology of certain singular moduli spaces of framed representations, is provided by the following theorem, a more general version of which is proved as Theorem  10.5 : 
 
 
 Theorem 1.14 . 
 
 The  𝔫 Π Q BPS , + \mathfrak{n}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathtt{\aBPS},+} -action on  𝕄 𝐟 ​ ( Q ) \mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)  extends to a  𝔤 Π Q BPS \mathfrak{g}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathtt{\aBPS}} -action, for which  𝕄 𝐟 ​ ( Q ) \mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)  decomposes as a direct sum of cohomologically shifted simple lowest weight representations, with lowest weight spaces identified with intersection cohomology of singular quiver varieties: 
 

 
 
 𝕄 𝐟 ​ ( Q ) ≅ ⨁ ( 𝐝 , 1 ) ∈ Φ Q 𝐟 + IH^* ⁡ ( ℳ Π Q 𝐟 , ( 𝐝 , 1 ) ) ⊗ L ( ( 𝐝 , 1 ) , ( − , 0 ) ) Q 𝐟 . \mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)\cong\bigoplus_{(\mathbf{d},1)\in\Phi_{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}^{+}}\ICA\!(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q_{\mathbf{f}}},(\mathbf{d},1)})\otimes L_{((\mathbf{d},1),(-,0))_{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}}. 
 
 
 Here,  IH^* \ICA  denotes the result of the derived global section functor applied to the perverse IC complex. 
 
 
 
 We explain how to recover the above results of  [ Nak97 ,  Nak98 ,  Gro96 ]  from Theorem  10.5  in § 10.4 . 
 
 
 Remark 1.15 . 
 
 A consequence of our results is that  𝔤 Π Q BPS \mathfrak{g}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathtt{\aBPS}} acts faithfully on the direct sum of modules  𝕄 𝐟 ​ ( Q ) \mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)  provided by taking the cohomology of Nakajima quiver varieties for various framing vectors  𝐟 \mathbf{f} . In  [ MO19 ]  Maulik and Okounkov associate to each quiver  Q Q  a Lie algebra  𝔤 𝙼𝙾 , Q \mathfrak{g}_{\mathtt{MO},Q}  (after extending scalars from  𝐐 \mathbf{Q}  to an equivariant cohomology ring). By construction, their Lie algebra acts faithfully on cohomology of Nakajima quiver varieties, and admits a  𝐙 Q 0 \mathbf{Z}^{Q_{0}} -graded weight space decomposition. Furthermore,  𝔤 𝙼𝙾 , Q \mathfrak{g}_{\mathtt{MO},Q}  is conjectured to be a generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra, is conjectured to be already defined over  𝐐 \mathbf{Q} , and is known to contain the usual Kac–Moody Lie algebra associated to  Q Q . The main results of this paper, then, should be taken as strong evidence for the conjectures 2 2 
                 2 
                
                
                
              These conjectures have now been proved in  [ BD23 ] . The proof uses in an essential way the description of the BPS Lie algebra as a generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra which forms Theorem  1.2 , as well as the description of its representation theory contained in Theorem  1.14 . These conjectures in turn imply Okounkov’s conjecture equating the graded dimensions of  𝔤 𝙼𝙾 , Q \mathfrak{g}_{\mathtt{MO},Q}  with coefficients of Kac polynomials, see  [ BD23 ,  SV23 ] .  (in  [ MO19 ]  and  [ Dav20 ]  respectively) that  𝔤 𝙼𝙾 , Q \mathfrak{g}_{\mathtt{MO},Q}  can be defined over  𝐐 \mathbf{Q} , and (after extending scalars) there is an isomorphism  𝔫 Π Q BPS , + ≅ 𝔫 𝙼𝙾 , Q + \mathfrak{n}^{\mathtt{\aBPS},+}_{\Pi_{Q}}\cong\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\mathtt{MO},Q} . 
 
 
 
 
 1.6.  Comparison with the 3d BPS Lie algebra 

 
 If  𝒜 = Rep ⁡ Π Q \mathcal{A}=\operatorname{Rep}\Pi_{Q} , a definition of the BPS Lie algebra sheaf associated to the vanishing cycle cohomology of the stack of  Π Q ​ [ x ] \Pi_{Q}[x] -modules is given in  [ Dav20 ]  using the fact that  Π Q ​ [ x ] \Pi_{Q}[x]  is the  3 3 -Calabi–Yau Jacobi algebra associated to a quiver with potential. CoHAs for vanishing cycle cohomology of Jacobi algebras were defined and studied in  [ KS11 ] . We denote this BPS Lie algebra sheaf by  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 Π Q , Lie 𝟹 ​ 𝚍 , ψ ∈ Perv ⁡ ( ℳ Π Q ) \mathcal{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathtt{3d},\psi}\in\operatorname{Perv}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}) . The superscript  𝟹 ​ 𝚍 \mathtt{3d}  indicates that the definition involves dimensional reduction from the stack of representations of a Jacobi algebra. This sheaf is the direct image of the BPS sheaf defined in the context of quivers with potential in  [ DM20 ] , and this approach to defining BPS sheaves is very much inspired by the theory recalled in § 1.4.1 . 
 
 
 Although the BPS Lie algebra sheaf (for an arbitrary 2CY category) in this paper is defined very differently, for the category of  Π Q \Pi_{Q} -modules we nonetheless prove the following in § 8.2  (Proposition  8.4 ): 
 
 
 Theorem 1.16 . 
 
 Let  Q Q  be a quiver. The relative BPS Lie algebras  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 Π Q , Lie \mathcal{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}  and  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 Π Q , Lie 𝟹 ​ 𝚍 , ψ \mathcal{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathtt{3d},\psi}  are canonically isomorphic. 
 
 
 
 In particular, the BPS Lie algebra sheaf  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 Π Q , Lie 𝟹 ​ 𝚍 , ψ \mathcal{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathtt{3d},\psi}  is (up to isomorphism) independent of the chosen  ψ \psi -twist. We expect Theorem  1.16  to be true for general 3CY completions of 2CY categories (see upcoming work of the first-named author and Tasuki Kinjo).

 1.8.  Standard notations and conventions 

 
 
 (1)  
 
 If  M M  is a monoid, we denote by  M ^ \widehat{M}  its groupification. 
 
 (2)  
 
 If  M M  is a monoid and  ( l , m ) ∈ 𝐍 × M (l,m)\in\mathbf{N}\times M , then we denote by  l ​ m lm  the sum of  l l  copies of  m m . 
 
 (3)  
 
 We denote by  1 i 1_{i}  the  i i th basis vector of the monoid  𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} , where  i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} . 
 
 (4)  
 
 We denote by  H 𝐂 ∗ ∗ \HO^{*}_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}  the  𝐂 ∗ \mathbf{C}^{*} -equivariant cohomology ring of the point  pt \mathrm{pt}  with coefficients in  𝐐 \mathbf{Q} :  H 𝐂 ∗ ∗ ≔ H 𝐂 ∗ ∗ ⁡ ( pt , 𝐐 ) ≅ H ⁡ ( B ⁡ 𝐂 ∗ , 𝐐 ) \HO^{*}_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}\coloneqq\HO^{*}_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}(\mathrm{pt},\mathbf{Q})\cong\HO(\B\mathbf{C}^{*},\mathbf{Q}) . We denote  H ¯ 𝐂 ∗ ∗ ≔ H 𝐂 ∗ ∗ ⁡ ( pt , 𝐐 ¯ ) \underline{\HO}^{*}_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}\coloneqq\HO^{*}_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}(\mathrm{pt},\underline{\mathbf{Q}})  the corresponding pure mixed Hodge structure. 
 
 (5)  
 
 Stacks are usually denoted using fraktur letters:  𝔛 \mathfrak{X} . If a stack  𝔛 \mathfrak{X}  has a good moduli space  [ Alp13 ] , it will be denoted using calligraphic letters: e.g.  𝔛 → 𝒳 \mathfrak{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}  denotes the morphism to the good moduli space. 
 
 (6)  
 
 If  ℱ \mathscr{F}  is a complex of constructible sheaves on a space  X X  we will frequently abbreviate  H ∗ ⁡ ℱ ≔ H ∗ ⁡ ( X , ℱ ) \HO^{*}\!\mathscr{F}\coloneqq\HO^{*}(X,\mathscr{F}) . 
 
 (7)  
 
 If  X X  is a smooth scheme or stack, we set  𝐐 X vir = ⨁ X ′ ∈ π 0 ​ ( X ) 𝐐 X ′ ​ [ dim X ′ ] \mathbf{Q}_{X}^{\mathrm{vir}}=\bigoplus_{X^{\prime}\in\pi_{0}(X)}\mathbf{Q}_{X^{\prime}}[\dim X^{\prime}] , the constant perverse sheaf. If each connected component of  X X  has even dimension, we set  𝐐 ¯ X vir = ⨁ X ′ ∈ π 0 ​ ( X ) 𝐐 ¯ X ′ ⊗ 𝐋 − dim ( X ′ ) / 2 \underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{X}^{\mathrm{vir}}=\bigoplus_{X^{\prime}\in\pi_{0}(X)}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{-\dim(X^{\prime})/2} , where  𝐋 = H c ⁡ ( 𝐀 1 , 𝐐 ) \mathbf{L}=\HO_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbf{A}^{1},\mathbf{Q}) . This is a mixed Hodge module of pure weight zero. 
 
 (8)  
 
 If  X X  is an irreducible complex algebraic variety of even dimension, the pure weight zero mixed Hodge module  𝐐 ¯ X sm vir = 𝐐 ¯ X sm ⊗ 𝐋 − dim ( X ) / 2 \underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{X^{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathrm{vir}}=\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{X^{\mathrm{sm}}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{-\dim(X)/2}  on  X sm X^{\mathrm{sm}}  has an essentially unique extension to a pure weight zero  simple  mixed Hodge module on  X X . We denote it by  ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( X ) \underline{\mathcal{IC}}(X) . 
 
 (9)  
 
 If  X X  is a complex, separated, locally finite type scheme, we denote by  Perv ⁡ ( X ) \operatorname{Perv}(X) ,  𝒟 c b ​ ( X ) \mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X) ,  MHM ⁡ ( X ) \mathrm{MHM}(X) ,  𝒟 b ​ ( MHM ​ ( X ) ) \mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{MHM}(X))  the categories of perverse sheaves, mixed Hodge modules and their derived categories on the underlying reduced scheme  X red X_{\mathrm{red}} . We let  𝒟 c + ​ ( X ) \mathcal{D}^{+}_{\mathrm{c}}(X)  and  𝒟 + ​ ( MHM ​ ( X ) ) \mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(X))  denote the categories of locally bounded below complexes. 
 
 (10)  
 
 We have the natural truncation functor  τ ⩽ 0 : 𝒟 + ​ ( MHM ⁡ ( X ) ) → 𝒟 + ​ ( MHM ⁡ ( X ) ) \tau^{\leqslant 0}\colon\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(X))\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(X)) . 
 
 (11)  
 
 For a complex of mixed Hodge modules  ℱ ∈ 𝒟 + ​ ( MHM ​ ( X ) ) \mathscr{F}\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(X)) , we denote by  ℋ i ​ ( ℱ ) \mathcal{H}^{i}(\mathscr{F})  its  i i th cohomology. It is a mixed Hodge module on  ℳ \mathcal{M} . 
 
 (12)  
 
 If  M M  is a monoid,  A A  a  M M -graded algebra and  ψ : M × M → 𝐙 / 2 ​ 𝐙 \psi\colon M\times M\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}  a bilinear form, we let  A ψ A^{\psi}  be the  ψ \psi -twisted algebra. As  M M -graded vector spaces,  A = A ψ A=A^{\psi}  and if  m : A ⊗ A → A m\colon A\otimes A\rightarrow A  is the multiplication of  A A , then the restriction of the multiplication on  A ψ A^{\psi}  is  m a , b ψ ≔ ( − 1 ) ψ ⁡ ( a , b ) ​ m a , b : A a ⊗ A b → A a + b m_{a,b}^{\psi}\coloneqq(-1)^{\psi(a,b)}m_{a,b}\colon A_{a}\otimes A_{b}\rightarrow A_{a+b}  for any  a , b ∈ M a,b\in M . 
 
 (13)  
 
 If a statement concerning a  ψ \psi -twisted algebra  A ψ A^{\psi}  in some monoidal category does not actually depend on the algebra structure (and therefore, the  ψ \psi -twist does not play any role), we write  A ( ψ ) A^{(\psi)} . For example, the  M M -graded character of  A A  or the purity (if  A A  is a mixed Hodge module or mixed Hodge structure) are properties independent of the multiplication. 
 
 (14)  
 
 If  V V  is a  M × 𝐙 M\times\mathbf{Z}  graded vector space (i.e. for each  m ∈ M m\in M , the vector space  V m V_{m}  is cohomologically graded), we denote by  ch 𝐙 ​ ( V ) \mathrm{ch}^{\mathbf{Z}}(V)  its character:  ch 𝐙 ​ ( V ) = ∑ n ∈ 𝐙 ∑ m ∈ M dim ( V m n ) ​ q n / 2 ​ z m ∈ 𝐍 ⁡ [ [ M × 𝐙 ] ] \mathrm{ch}^{\mathbf{Z}}(V)=\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}}\sum_{m\in M}\dim(V_{m}^{n})q^{n/2}z^{m}\in\mathbf{N}[[M\times\mathbf{Z}]] . 
 
 (15)  
 
 For  l ∈ 𝐙 ⩾ 1 l\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 1} , we let  S l S^{l}  be the symmetric group on a set with  l l  elements. 
 
 (16)  
 
 If  X X  is an algebraic variety with an action of an algebraic group  G G , the stack quotient is simply denoted by  X / G X/G  (in the literature, the notation  [ X / G ] [X/G]  is also used). 
 
 (17)  
 
 If  X X  is a scheme, we let  Γ ⁡ ( X ) \Gamma(X)  be the ring of global functions on  X X . 
 
 (18)  
 
 If  A A  is a ring with an action of a group  G G  by automorphisms of ring, we let  A G A^{G}  be the ring of invariants. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 2.  Quivers and representations 

 
 A quiver is a directed graph, i.e. a pair of sets  Q 1 Q_{1}  and  Q 0 Q_{0}  of arrows and vertices, respectively, along with a pair of maps  s , t : Q 1 → Q 0 s,t\colon Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{0}  taking an arrow to its source and target, respectively. 
 
 
 We allow our quivers to have loops (i.e. 1-cycles). Let  Q 0 im ⊆ Q 0 Q_{0}^{\mathrm{im}}\subseteq Q_{0}  be the subset of vertices supporting loops, which we call  imaginary vertices . We denote by  Q ⊆ Q Q\subseteq Q  the quiver obtained by replacing  Q 0 Q_{0}  with  Q 0 ∖ Q 0 im Q_{0}\smallsetminus Q_{0}^{\mathrm{im}} , and removing all arrows from  Q 1 Q_{1}  that have source or target in  Q 0 im Q_{0}^{\mathrm{im}} . 
 
 
 We denote by 
 

 
 
 χ Q ​ ( − , − ) : \displaystyle\chi_{Q}(-,-)\colon 
 𝐙 Q 0 × 𝐙 Q 0 → 𝐙 \displaystyle\mathbf{Z}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbf{Z}^{Q_{0}}\rightarrow\mathbf{Z} 
 
 
 
 
 ( 𝐝 ′ , 𝐝 ′′ ) ↦ ∑ i ∈ Q 0 d i ′ ​ d i ′′ − ∑ a ∈ Q 1 d s ⁡ ( a ) ′ ​ d t ⁡ ( a ) ′′ \displaystyle(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime})\mapsto\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}d^{\prime}_{i}d^{\prime\prime}_{i}-\sum_{a\in Q_{1}}d^{\prime}_{s(a)}d^{\prime\prime}_{t(a)} 
 
 
 the Euler form of  Q Q , and define  ( − , − ) Q (-,-)_{Q}  to be its symmetrisation. For  𝐝 ′ , 𝐝 ′′ ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} , we define the  dot product  by  𝐝 ′ ⋅ 𝐝 ′′ = ∑ i ∈ Q 0 d i ′ ​ d i ′′ \mathbf{d}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime}=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}d^{\prime}_{i}d^{\prime\prime}_{i} . 
 
 
 Given a quiver  Q Q  we denote by  𝐂 ​ Q \mathbf{C}Q  the  free path algebra , i.e. the algebra having a  𝐂 \mathbf{C} -basis given by paths in  Q Q , including paths  𝒆 i \bm{e}_{i}  of length zero at each of the vertices  i i . The multiplication, with respect to this basis, is given by concatenation of paths. 
 
 
 If  A = 𝐂 ​ Q / R A=\mathbf{C}Q/R  is presented as the quotient of a free path algebra by a two sided ideal generated by linear combinations of paths of length of at least one, and  ρ \rho  is an  A A -module, we define the  dimension vector 
 

 
 
 dim Q 0 ( ρ ) ≔ ( dim ( 𝒆 i ⋅ ρ ) ) i ∈ Q 0 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 . \dim_{Q_{0}}(\rho)\coloneqq(\dim(\bm{e}_{i}\!\cdot\!\rho))_{i\in Q_{0}}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}. 
 
 
 For a quiver  Q Q  and dimension vector  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  we define 
 

 
 
 𝐀 Q , 𝐝 ≔ ∏ a ∈ Q 1 Hom ⁡ ( 𝐂 d s ⁡ ( a ) , 𝐂 d t ⁡ ( a ) ) \mathbf{A}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}\coloneqq\prod_{a\in Q_{1}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbf{C}^{d_{s(a)}},\mathbf{C}^{d_{t(a)}}) 
 
 
 which is acted on by the gauge group 
 

 
 
 GL 𝐝 ≔ ∏ i ∈ Q 0 GL d i ​ ( 𝐂 ) \mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}}\coloneqq\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathrm{GL}_{d_{i}}(\mathbf{C}) 
 
 
 by change of basis. We denote by  𝔐 Q , 𝐝 \mathfrak{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  the stack of  𝐝 \mathbf{d} -dimensional  𝐂 ​ Q \mathbf{C}Q -modules, and by  𝙹𝙷 𝐝 : 𝔐 Q , 𝐝 → ℳ Q , 𝐝 \JH_{\mathbf{d}}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  the canonical morphism to the affinization. There is an equivalence of stacks, and an isomorphism of affine varieties 
 

 
 
 𝔐 Q , 𝐝 ≃ \displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}\simeq 
 𝐀 Q , 𝐝 / GL 𝐝 \displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}/\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}} 
 
 
 
 ℳ Q , 𝐝 ≅ \displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}\cong 
 Spec ⁡ ( Γ ​ ( 𝐀 Q , 𝐝 ) GL 𝐝 ) . \displaystyle\operatorname{Spec}(\Gamma(\mathbf{A}_{Q,\mathbf{d}})^{\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}}}). 
 
 
 The variety  ℳ Q , 𝐝 \mathcal{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  is the coarse moduli space of  𝐝 \mathbf{d} -dimensional  𝐂 ​ Q \mathbf{C}Q -modules, and its points correspond to semisimple  𝐝 \mathbf{d} -dimensional modules. We set  𝔐 Q ≔ ∐ 𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 𝔐 Q , 𝐝 \mathfrak{M}_{Q}\coloneqq\coprod_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}\mathfrak{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  and  ℳ Q ≔ ∐ 𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 ℳ Q , 𝐝 \mathcal{M}_{Q}\coloneqq\coprod_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}\mathcal{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}} . There is a direct sum operation 
 

 
 
 ⊕ : ℳ Q × ℳ Q → ℳ Q \oplus\colon\mathcal{M}_{Q}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{Q} 
 
 
 making  ℳ Q \mathcal{M}_{Q}  into a commutative monoid in schemes. This morphism is finite  [ MR19 , Lemma 2.1] . 
 
 
 It will often be convenient to consider a submonoid  ℳ Q △ ⊆ ℳ Q \mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\triangle}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{Q} , which we will assume is saturated, meaning that the following diagram is Cartesian 
 

 
 
 ℳ Q △ × ℳ Q △ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\triangle}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\triangle}} ℳ Q △ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\triangle}} ℳ Q × ℳ Q {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q}} ℳ Q {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}} ı △ × ı △ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath_{\triangle}\times\imath_{\triangle}} i △ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\triangle}} ⊕ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus} ⊕ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus} ⌟ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner} 
 
 
 where  ı △ : ℳ Q △ ↪ ℳ Q \imath_{\triangle}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{Q}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{Q}  is the inclusion, and we denote again by  ⊕ \oplus  the restriction of  ⊕ \oplus  to  ℳ Q △ × ℳ Q △ \mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{Q}\times\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{Q} . 
 
 
 The connected components  π 0 ​ ( ℳ Q △ ) \pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{Q})  then form a monoid, and we fix a morphism of monoids 
 

 
 
 cl : π 0 ​ ( ℳ Q △ ) → K . \mathrm{cl}\colon\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{Q})\rightarrow\K. 
 
 
 where  K \K  is a finitely generated torsion-free, commutative, cancellative monoid; equivalently, the natural map  K → K ^ \K\rightarrow\widehat{\K}  to the groupification of  K \K  is injective, and the target is a lattice. We assume also that the Euler form  χ Q ​ ( − , − ) \chi_{Q}(-,-) , which automatically descends to a bilinear form on  π 0 ​ ( ℳ Q △ ) \pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}^{\triangle}_{Q}) , descends to a bilinear form on  K \K . The standard choice of  K \K  is  𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} , with  cl \mathrm{cl}  taking a connected component  ℳ ⊆ ℳ Q , 𝐝 △ \mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\triangle}  to  𝐝 \mathbf{d} . 
 
 
 Given a quiver  Q Q  we denote by  Q ¯ \overline{Q}  the  doubled quiver , i.e. the quiver obtained by adding a new arrow  a ∗ a^{*}  for each arrow  a ∈ Q 1 a\in Q_{1} , with  s ⁡ ( a ∗ ) = t ⁡ ( a ) s(a^{*})=t(a)  and  t ⁡ ( a ∗ ) = s ⁡ ( a ) t(a^{*})=s(a) . We define the  preprojective algebra 
 

 
 
 Π Q ≔ 𝐂 ​ Q ¯ / ⟨ ∑ a ∈ Q 1 [ a , a ∗ ] ⟩ . \Pi_{Q}\coloneqq\mathbf{C}\overline{Q}/\big\langle\sum_{a\in Q_{1}}[a,a^{*}]\big\rangle. 
 
 
 
 
 We denote by  Q ~ \widetilde{Q}  the  tripled quiver  obtained from  Q ¯ \overline{Q}  by adding a loop  ω i \omega_{i}  based at  i i , for each vertex  i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} .
We denote by  𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) \mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)  the  derived preprojective algebra , defined as follows. We give  𝐂 ​ Q ~ \mathbf{C}\widetilde{Q}  a cohomological grading, by putting  a a  and  a ∗ a^{*}  in cohomological degree  0 0 , and each  ω i \omega_{i}  in cohomological degree  − 1 -1 . We define a differential on  𝐂 ​ Q ~ \mathbf{C}\widetilde{Q}  by setting  d ​ ω i = 𝒆 i ⋅ ∑ a ∈ Q 1 [ a , a ∗ ] ⋅ 𝒆 i d\omega_{i}=\bm{e}_{i}\!\cdot\!\sum_{a\in Q_{1}}[a,a^{*}]\!\cdot\!\bm{e}_{i}  and extending via the Leibniz rule. Then  H 0 ⁡ ( 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) ≅ Π Q \HO^{0}(\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q))\cong\Pi_{Q}  and we identify the category of  Π Q \Pi_{Q} -modules with the full subcategory of  𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) \mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q) -modules concentrated in cohomological degree  0 0 . For  M M  and  N N  two finite-dimensional  Π Q \Pi_{Q} -modules there is an equality 
 

 
 
 ( M , N ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) = ( dim Q 0 ( M ) , dim Q 0 ( N ) ) Q (M,N)_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}=(\dim_{Q_{0}}(M),\dim_{Q_{0}}(N))_{Q} 
 
 
 between the Euler form in the category of  𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) \mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q) -modules, and the symmetrised Euler form. 
 
 
 We define  𝔤 ​ 𝔩 𝐝 ≔ ∏ i ∈ Q 0 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 d i ​ ( 𝐂 ) \mathfrak{gl}_{\mathbf{d}}\coloneqq\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathfrak{gl}_{d_{i}}(\mathbf{C}) , the Lie algebra of  GL 𝐝 \mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}} . We identify  𝐀 Q ¯ , 𝐝 = T ∗ ​ 𝐀 Q , 𝐝 \mathbf{A}_{\overline{Q},\mathbf{d}}=\mathrm{T}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  via the trace pairing. Then the  GL 𝐝 \mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}} -action is Hamiltonian, with (co)moment map 
 

 
 
 μ Q , 𝐝 : 𝐀 Q ¯ , 𝐝 → 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 𝐝 ( ρ ⁡ ( a ) , ρ ⁡ ( a ∗ ) ) a ∈ Q 1 ↦ ∑ a ∈ Q 1 [ ρ ⁡ ( a ) , ρ ⁡ ( a ∗ ) ] . \begin{matrix}\mu_{Q,\mathbf{d}}\colon&\mathbf{A}_{\overline{Q},\mathbf{d}}&\rightarrow&\mathfrak{gl}_{\mathbf{d}}\\
&(\rho(a),\rho(a^{*}))_{a\in Q_{1}}&\mapsto&\sum_{a\in Q_{1}}[\rho(a),\rho(a^{*})].\end{matrix} 
 
 
 The zero set  μ Q , 𝐝 − 1 ​ ( 0 ) ⊆ 𝐀 Q ¯ , 𝐝 \mu_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(0)\subseteq\mathbf{A}_{\overline{Q},\mathbf{d}}  is identified with the subspace of  Π Q \Pi_{Q} -modules. There is an equivalence 
 

 
 
 𝔐 Π Q , 𝐝 ≃ μ Q , 𝐝 − 1 ​ ( 0 ) / GL 𝐝 \mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}\simeq\mu_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(0)/\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}} 
 
 
 between the stack of  𝐝 \mathbf{d} -dimensional  Π Q \Pi_{Q} -modules and the stack-theoretic quotient of the zero-locus of the moment map. 
 
 
 
 3.  Generalized Kac–Moody algebras 

 
 In this section, we give the constructions of the generalised Kac–Moody algebra associated to a monoid with bilinear form and a weight function following  [ Bor88 ] , and of generalised half Kac–Moody algebras in symmetric tensor categories of mixed Hodge modules/perverse sheaves. We work with monoids with bilinear forms to allow sufficient flexibility when investigating the BPS algebras of  2 2 -Calabi–Yau categories. 
 
 
 3.1.  Roots 

 
 3.1.1.  Primitive and positive roots 

 
 Let  ( M , + ) (M,+)  be a monoid and  M × M → 𝐙 M\times M\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}  be a bilinear form. When considering Weyl group actions we assume that  M M  is finitely generated, commutative, torsion-free, and cancellative; equivalently, we require that the natural map  M → M ^ M\rightarrow\widehat{M}  to the groupification is injective, and  M ^ \widehat{M}  is a lattice. All definitions of roots make sense without these assumptions (which may not be satisfied by the monoid of connected components of the good moduli space of 2CY categories). We let 
 

 
 (3.1) 
 
 Σ M , ( − , − ) = { m ∈ M ∣ 2 − ( m , m ) ⩾ 0  and  2 − ( m , m ) > ∑ j = 1 s ( 2 − ( m j , m j ) )  for any nontrivial decomposition  m = ∑ j = 1 s m j  with  m j ∈ M ∖ { 0 } } \Sigma_{M,(-,-)}=\left\{m\in M\mid 2-(m,m)\geqslant 0\text{ and }2-(m,m)>\sum_{j=1}^{s}(2-(m_{j},m_{j}))\right.\\
\left.\text{ for any nontrivial decomposition $m=\sum_{j=1}^{s}m_{j}$ with $m_{j}\in M\smallsetminus\{0\}$}\right\} 
 
 
 be the set of  primitive positive roots , and 
 

 
 
 Φ M , ( − , − ) + = Σ M , ( − , − ) ∪ { l m ∣ l ∈ 𝐍 ⩾ 1 , m ∈ Σ M , ( − , − )  and  ( m , m ) = 0 } \Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+}=\Sigma_{M,(-,-)}\cup\{lm\mid l\in\mathbf{N}_{\geqslant 1},m\in\Sigma_{M,(-,-)}\text{ and }(m,m)=0\} 
 
 
 be the set of  simple positive roots . We assume that  ( m , m ) ∈ 2 ​ 𝐙 ⩽ 1 (m,m)\in 2\mathbf{Z}_{\leqslant 1}  for  m ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + m\in\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+} . We write the decomposition of  Φ M , ( − , − ) + \Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+}  into real and imaginary roots,  Φ M , ( − , − ) + = Φ M , ( − , − ) + , ⊔ Φ M , ( − , − ) + , im \Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+}=\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\real}\sqcup\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\mathrm{im}} , where  Φ M , ( − , − ) + , ≔ { m ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + ∣ ( m , m ) = 2 } \Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\real}\coloneqq\{m\in\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+}\mid(m,m)=2\}  and  Φ M , ( − , − ) + , im ≔ { m ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + ∣ ( m , m ) ⩽ 0 } \Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\mathrm{im}}\coloneqq\{m\in\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+}\mid(m,m)\leqslant 0\} . We can further decompose  Φ M , ( − , − ) + , im = Φ M , ( − , − ) + , iso ⊔ Φ M , ( − , − ) + , hyp \Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\mathrm{im}}=\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\mathrm{iso}}\sqcup\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\mathrm{hyp}}  where 
 

 
 
 Φ M , ( − , − ) + , iso ≔ { m ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + ∣ ( m , m ) = 0 } \displaystyle\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\mathrm{iso}}\coloneqq\{m\in\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+}\mid(m,m)=0\} 
 
 
 
 Φ M , ( − , − ) + , hyp ≔ { m ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + ∣ ( m , m ) < 0 } \displaystyle\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\mathrm{hyp}}\coloneqq\{m\in\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+}\mid(m,m)<0\} 
 
 
 are the sets of isotropic and hyperbolic roots, respectively. For  m ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + , iso ∩ Σ M , ( − , − ) m\in\Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\mathrm{iso}}\cap\Sigma_{M,(-,-)}  we call  m m  an  indivisible isotropic root . 
 
 
 We fix an Abelian category  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , and an ambient dg subcategory  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  which contains  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  as a subcategory of its homotopy category, and a fixed stack of objects  𝔐 𝒜 \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  in  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , such that the Euler form  ( − , − ) 𝒞 (-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}  is locally constant on  𝔐 𝒜 × 𝔐 𝒜 \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}} . We assume that we have a chosen monoid  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \K(\mathcal{A})  and a fixed surjective morphism of monoids 
 

 
 
 π 0 ​ ( 𝔐 𝒜 ) → K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) . \pi_{0}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\K(\mathcal{A}). 
 
 
 As in § 2  we assume that the natural morphism to the groupification  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) → K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) ^ \K(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\widehat{\K(\mathcal{A})}  is injective,  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) ^ \widehat{\K(\mathcal{A})}  is a lattice, and the Euler form  ( − , − ) 𝒞 (-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}  descends to a bilinear form on  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \K(\mathcal{A})  (and thus induces a bilinear form on  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) ^ \widehat{\K(\mathcal{A})} ). We denote by  Σ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \Sigma_{\K(\mathcal{A})}  and  Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + \Phi_{\K(\mathcal{A})}^{+}  the sets of primitive positive roots and simple positive roots, respectively, associated to the monoid  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \K(\mathcal{A})  endowed with the Euler form. 
 
 
 Remark 3.1 . 
 
 The hypotheses on  ( m , m ) (m,m)  and the definition of  Φ M , ( − , − ) + , \Phi_{M,(-,-)}^{+,\real}  are designed for the study of the BPS algebras of  2 2 -Calabi–Yau categories. It is certainly possible to weaken or generalise them to combinatorially define a broader class of GKM algebras. A possible different choice would be 
 

 
 (3.2) 
 
 Φ M , ( − , − ) + , ≔ { m ∈ M ∣ ( m , m ) > 0 } \displaystyle\Phi^{+,\real}_{M,(-,-)}\coloneqq\{m\in M\mid(m,m)>0\} 
 
 
 (3.3) 
 
 2 ​ ( m , n ) ( m , m ) ∈ 𝐙 ⩽ 0 ​  if  m ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + ,  and  n ∈ Φ M , ( − , − ) + . \displaystyle\frac{2(m,n)}{(m,m)}\in\mathbf{Z}_{\leqslant 0}\text{ if $m\in\Phi^{+,\real}_{M,(-,-)}$ and $n\in\Phi^{+}_{M,(-,-)}$}.

 Lemma 3.2 . 
 
 Let  f : M → N f\colon M\rightarrow N  be a surjective morphism of monoids, and let  ( − , − ) M (-,-)_{M}  and  ( − , − ) N (-,-)_{N}  be bilinear forms on  M , N M,N  respectively such that  f ∗ ​ ( − , − ) N = ( − , − ) M f^{*}(-,-)_{N}=(-,-)_{M} . We assume that  f ⁡ ( m ) ≠ 0 f(m)\neq 0  if  m ∈ Σ M , ( − , − ) M m\in\Sigma_{M,(-,-)_{M}} . Then,  f − 1 ​ ( Σ N , ( − , − ) N ) = Σ M , ( − , − ) M f^{-1}(\Sigma_{N,(-,-)_{N}})=\Sigma_{M,(-,-)_{M}} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This is straightforward from the definitions of the sets of primitive positive roots.
∎
