Then we assume that ( 4.2 ) is represented by a diagram of globally presented quasi-smooth morphisms (i.e., a diagram in  𝙶𝙿𝚛𝚎𝚜 \GPres ) and we assume that the global presentations in this diagram are globally equivalent to the natural pullbacks of  𝐪 ′ \bm{q}^{\prime} .
For a completely precise formulation of this assumption see  [ DHS22 , Assumption 3] . 
 
 
 
 
 4.4.  Assumptions on the good moduli space 

 
 The construction of the BPS algebra in § 6.2  requires the existence of a good moduli space, satisfying various extra assumptions. Again, in concrete applications, they are all well-known to hold; see  [ DHS22 ]  for a leisurely discussion. 
 
 
 Assumption 4 . 
 
 The stack  𝔐 \mathfrak{M}  has a good moduli space  𝔐 → 𝙹𝙷 ℳ \mathfrak{M}\xrightarrow{\JH}\mathcal{M}  in the sense of  [ Alp13 ] , and  ℳ \mathcal{M}  is a scheme. 
 
 
 
 The scheme  ℳ \mathcal{M}  is endowed with a monoid structure given by the direct sum: 
 

 
 
 ⊕ : ℳ × ℳ \displaystyle\oplus\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} 
 ⟶ ℳ \displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M} 
 
 
 
 ( x , y ) \displaystyle(x,y) 
 ⟼ x ⊕ y \displaystyle\longmapsto x\oplus y 
 
 
 obtained from the direct sum  ⊕ : 𝔐 × 𝔐 → 𝔐 \oplus\colon\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}  and universality of the good moduli space  𝙹𝙷 : 𝔐 → ℳ \JH\colon\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M} . Note that the following diagram commutes: 
 

 
 
 𝔐 × 𝔐 {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}}} 𝔈 ​ 𝔵 ​ 𝔞 ​ 𝔠 ​ 𝔱 {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Exact}}} 𝔐 {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}}} ℳ × ℳ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}} ℳ . {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}.}} 𝙹𝙷 × 𝙹𝙷 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH\times\JH} q \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q} p \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p} 𝙹𝙷 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH} ⊕ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus} 
 
 
 If  ℳ a \mathcal{M}_{a}  is a connected component of  ℳ \mathcal{M} , we define  𝔐 a ≔ 𝙹𝙷 − 1 ⁡ ( ℳ a ) \mathfrak{M}_{a}\coloneqq\JH^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{a}) , and for  a ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) a\in\K(\mathcal{A}) , where  cl : π 0 ​ ( ℳ ) → K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \mathrm{cl}\colon\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\K(\mathcal{A})  is a morphism of monoids, we define  𝔐 a \mathfrak{M}_{a}  similarly (thus  𝔐 a = ⨆ a ′ ∈ cl − 1 ​ ( a ) 𝔐 a ′ \mathfrak{M}_{a}=\bigsqcup_{a^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}^{-1}(a)}\mathfrak{M}_{a^{\prime}} ). 
 
 
 Assumption 5 . 
 
 We assume that the morphism  ⊕ \oplus  is finite. 
 
 
 
 Given that the morphism  ⊕ \oplus  is easily seen to be quasi-finite, this amounts to requesting that  ⊕ \oplus  is a proper morphism. 
 
 
 To apply the local neighbourhood theorem (Theorem  4.2 ) on closed points of the moduli stacks  𝔐 \mathfrak{M}  we make the following additional Assumption  6 . 
 
 
 Assumption 6 . 
 
 Suppose we are in the situation of § 4.1  and Assumptions  4  and  5  are satisfied. Given a collection of points  x 1 , … , x r ∈ ℳ x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\in\mathcal{M}  parametrising simple objects  ℱ ¯ = { ℱ 1 , … , ℱ r } \underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}  of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , the full dg subcategory of  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  containing  ℱ ¯ \underline{\mathcal{F}}  carries a right 2-Calabi–Yau (2CY) structure in the sense of  [ BD19 , §3.1] . 
 
 
 
 Again, this somewhat technical assumption is known to hold in examples we encounter, since by  [ BD19 , Theorem 3.1] , the existence of the required right 2CY structure will follow from the existence of a left 2CY structure on the category  𝒞 \mathscr{C} , which is constructed in the examples appearing in this paper in [loc. cit.]. 
 
 
 Making these rather general assumptions, we have the following theorem. The 2CY assumption (Assumption  6 ) then plays a crucial role in the definition of the BPS algebra appearing in § 6.2 . 
 
 
 Theorem 4.1   ( [ DHS22 , §9] ) . 
 
 Under Assumptions  1 - 5 
there is a virtual pullback along  𝐪 a , b ′ \bm{q}^{\prime}_{a,b}  and
a push-forward along  p p , yielding the cohomological Hall algebra product 
 

 
 (4.3) 
 
 𝔪 : 𝙹𝙷 ∗ 𝔻 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 vir ⊡ 𝙹𝙷 ∗ 𝔻 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 vir ≔ ⊕ ∗ ( 𝙹𝙷 × 𝙹𝙷 ) ∗ 𝔻 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 × 𝔐 vir ⟶ 𝙹𝙷 ∗ 𝔻 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 vir \mathfrak{m}\colon\JH_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\boxdot\JH_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\coloneqq\oplus_{*}(\JH\times\JH)_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}}\longrightarrow\JH_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{vir}} 
 
 
 which endows the mixed Hodge module complex  𝙹𝙷 ∗ ⁡ 𝔻 ​ 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 vir \JH_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{vir}} 
with the structure of an associative algebra in the symmetric monoidal category  ( 𝒟 + ​ ( MHM ​ ( ℳ ) ) , ⊡ ) (\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M})),\boxdot) . 
 
 
 
 
 4.5.  Serre subcategories 

 
 Let  𝔐 → 𝙹𝙷 ℳ \mathfrak{M}\xrightarrow{\JH}\mathcal{M}  be as in Assumption  4  a good moduli space for the stack of objects  𝔐 \mathfrak{M}  as in § 4.1 . Fix a locally closed submonoid  ℳ ′ ⊆ ℳ \mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}  that is stable under direct summands, i.e. saturated (if  x ⊕ y ∈ ℳ ′ x\oplus y\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime} , then  x , y ∈ ℳ ′ x,y\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime} ). We define  ℬ \mathcal{B}  to be the full Abelian subcategory of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  generated by the objects represented by the closed points of  ℳ ′ \mathcal{M}^{\prime}  under taking extensions. Typically,  ℬ \mathcal{B}  will be defined to be the full subcategory of objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  satisfying some support or nilpotency condition. Since  ℳ \mathcal{M}  parametrises semisimple objects in  𝒜 \mathcal{A} ,  ℬ \mathcal{B}  is a Serre subcategory (an Abelian subcategory stable under extensions, subobjects and quotients). 
 
 
 Let  ℬ \mathcal{B}  be a Serre subcategory of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  corresponding to a locally closed submonoid  ℳ ℬ ⊆ ℳ \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\mathcal{M} . We define  𝔐 ℬ ⊆ 𝔐 \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}  to be the substack parametrising objects of  ℬ \mathcal{B} , which we  define  via the following Cartesian square 
 

 
 
 𝔐 ℬ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}}} 𝔐 {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}}} ℳ ℬ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}}} ℳ . {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}.} 𝙹𝙷 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH} ⌟ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner} 
 
 
 The morphism  𝔐 ℬ → ℳ ℬ \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}  is a good moduli space by  [ Alp13 , Proposition 4.7 (i)] . Let  π 0 ​ ( ℳ ℬ ) \pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})  be the monoid of connected components of  ℳ ℬ \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}} . The monoid structure  ⊕ : π 0 ​ ( ℳ ℬ ) × π 0 ​ ( ℳ ℬ ) → π 0 ​ ( ℳ ℬ ) \oplus:\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})\times\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})\rightarrow\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})  is induced by the direct sum. We choose a monoid  K ⁡ ( ℬ ) \K(\mathcal{B})  and a surjective morphism of monoids  cl : π 0 ​ ( ℳ ℬ ) → K ⁡ ( ℬ ) \mathrm{cl}\colon\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})\rightarrow\K(\mathcal{B})  with finite fibres, where the target is a monoid for which the morphism  K ⁡ ( ℬ ) → K ⁡ ( ℬ ) ^ \K(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\widehat{\K(\mathcal{B})}  is an inclusion in a lattice. 
 
 
 The monoid of connected components of the stack  𝔐 ℬ \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}  coincides with  π 0 ​ ( ℳ ℬ ) \pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}) , since the fibres of  𝙹𝙷 \JH  are connected, by  [ Alp13 , Theorem 4.16 (vii)] . For  a ∈ K ⁡ ( ℬ ) a\in\K(\mathcal{B}) , we denote by  𝔐 ℬ , a \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B},a}  and  ℳ ℬ , a \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B},a}  the corresponding (finite) unions of connected components. 
 
 
 For any object  ℱ \mathcal{F}  of  ℬ \mathcal{B}  (resp.  𝐂 \mathbf{C} -point  x x  of  𝔐 ℬ \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}  or  ℳ ℬ \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}} ), we let  [ ℱ ] [\mathcal{F}]  (resp.  [ x ] [x] ) be  cl \mathrm{cl}  applied to the connected component of  x x . We will refer to  [ x ] [x]  as  the class  of  x x . The Euler form automatically factors through  π 0 ​ ( ℳ ℬ ) \pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})  to define a bilinear form: 
 

 
 
 ( − , − ) 𝒞 : π 0 ​ ( ℳ ℬ ) × π 0 ​ ( ℳ ℬ ) ⟶ 𝐙 . (-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}\colon\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})\times\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}. 
 
 
 We assume that it moreover factors through  cl \mathrm{cl} . The pair  ( K ⁡ ( ℬ ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 ) (\K(\mathcal{B}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}})  will provide the combinatorial input required in § 3.1 . 
 
 
 
 4.6.  The local neighbourhood theorem 

 
 Let  𝒞 \mathscr{C} ,  𝔐 \mathfrak{M}  and  ℳ \mathcal{M}  be as above, satisfying Assumptions  4  and  6 . Then the closed points of  ℳ \mathcal{M}  are in bijection with semisimple objects of the category  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  and the map  𝙹𝙷 \JH  sends a  𝐂 \mathbf{C} -point of  𝔐 \mathfrak{M}  corresponding to an object  ℱ \mathcal{F}  of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  to the  𝐂 \mathbf{C} -point of  ℳ \mathcal{M}  corresponding to the associated graded object of  ℱ \mathcal{F}  with respect to some Jordan–Hölder filtration. 
 
 
 4.6.1.  Σ \Sigma -collections 

 
 Let  ℱ \mathcal{F}  be an object in a  𝐂 \mathbf{C} -linear Abelian category  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , which we will assume is realised as a subcategory of the homotopy category of some dg category  𝒞 \mathscr{C} . We say that  ℱ \mathcal{F}  is a  Σ \Sigma -object  if  Ext 𝒞 ∗ ⁡ ( ℱ , ℱ ) ≅ H ∗ ⁡ ( S , 𝐂 ) \operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\cong\HO^{*}(S,\mathbf{C})  for  S S  a closed Riemann surface (of some genus  g ⩾ 0 g\geqslant 0 ). If  ℱ ¯ = { ℱ 1 , … , ℱ r } \underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}  is a collection of objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , we say that  ℱ ¯ \underline{\mathcal{F}}  is a  Σ \Sigma -collection  if each  ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t}  is a  Σ \Sigma -object and for  m ≠ n m\neq n ,  Hom ⁡ ( ℱ m , ℱ n ) = 0 \operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{m},\mathcal{F}_{n})=0 . 
 
 
 Let  ℱ ¯ = { ℱ 1 , … , ℱ r } \underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}  be a  Σ \Sigma -collection of objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . We define the Ext-quiver of  ℱ ¯ \underline{\mathcal{F}}  as the quiver  Q ¯ ℱ ¯ \overline{Q}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  having as set of vertices  ℱ ¯ \underline{\mathcal{F}} , and having  ext 1 ⁡ ( ℱ i , ℱ j ) \operatorname{ext}^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j})  arrows from  i i  to  j j . When  ext 1 ⁡ ( ℱ i , ℱ j ) = ext 1 ⁡ ( ℱ j , ℱ i ) \operatorname{ext}^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j})=\operatorname{ext}^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{j},\mathcal{F}_{i})  for any  1 ⩽ i , j ⩽ r 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant r  and this number is even if  i = j i=j , then  Q ¯ ℱ ¯ \overline{Q}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  is the double of some (usually non-unique) quiver  Q ℱ ¯ Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}} . This condition is for example satisfied for right  2 2 -Calabi–Yau categories as in § 4.1 . Accordingly, we make Assumption  6 . We will refer to  Q ℱ ¯ Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  as a  half of the Ext-quiver of  ℱ ¯ \underline{\mathcal{F}} . 
 
 
 We have a morphism of monoids 
 

 
 
 λ ℱ ¯ : 𝐍 ℱ ¯ ⟶ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) 𝐦 ⟼ ∑ i = 1 r m i ​ [ ℱ i ] . \begin{split}\lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}&\longrightarrow\K(\mathcal{A})\\
\mathbf{m}&\longmapsto\sum_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}[\mathcal{F}_{i}].\end{split} 
 
 
 We also have a morphism of monoids 
 

 
 (4.4) 
 
 ı ℱ ¯ : 𝐍 ℱ ¯ ⟶ ℳ \imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M} 
 
 
 mapping  𝐦 ∈ 𝐍 ℱ ¯ \mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  to the point of  ℳ \mathcal{M}  corresponding to the object  ⨁ i = 1 r ℱ i ⊕ m i \bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}} , i.e.  1 ℱ i ↦ x i 1_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}\mapsto x_{i}  if we denote by  x i ∈ ℳ x_{i}\in\mathcal{M}  the point corresponding to  ℱ i \mathcal{F}_{i} . 
 
 
 If  x ∈ ℳ 𝒜 x\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}} , it corresponds to a semisimple object  ℱ = ⨁ i = 1 r ℱ i ⊕ d i \mathcal{F}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\oplus d_{i}} . We define  ( Q x , 𝐝 x ) (Q_{x},\mathbf{d}_{x})  to be the pair formed by a half of the Ext-quiver of  ℱ ¯ ≔ { ℱ 1 , … , ℱ r } \underline{\mathcal{F}}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}  and the dimension vector  𝐝 x = ( d 1 , … , d r ) \mathbf{d}_{x}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r}) . Although this involves making an irrelevant choice for  Q x Q_{x} , the doubled quiver  Q x ¯ \overline{Q_{x}}  only depends on the simple direct summands of  ℱ \mathcal{F}  and not on their multiplicities. 
 
 
 
 4.6.2.  The local neighbourhood theorem 

 
 We recall here the local neighbourhood theorem for  2 2 -Calabi–Yau categories  [ Dav21 , Theorem 5.11] , which we will use in the next section to investigate the geometry of components of moduli spaces corresponding to roots for general 2CY categories. 
 
 
 Theorem 4.2 . 
 
 Let  𝙹𝙷 : 𝔐 → ℳ \JH\colon\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}  be a good moduli space, locally of finite type with reductive stabilizer groups. We assume that  𝔐 = t 0 ​ ( 𝕸 ) \mathfrak{M}=t_{0}(\bm{\mathfrak{M}})  is an Artin stack, where  𝕸 ⊆ 𝕸 𝒞 \bm{\mathfrak{M}}\subseteq\bm{\mathfrak{M}}_{\mathscr{C}}  is an open substack of the stack of objects of a dg-category  𝒞 \mathscr{C} . Let  x x  be a closed  𝐂 \mathbf{C} -valued point of  𝔐 \mathfrak{M}  corresponding to an object  ℱ = ⨁ 1 ⩽ i ⩽ r ℱ i ⊕ m i \mathcal{F}=\bigoplus_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}}  and assume that the full dg-subcategory of  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  containing  ℱ i \mathcal{F}_{i} ,  1 ⩽ i ⩽ r 1\leqslant i\leqslant r  carries a right 2CY structure and  ℱ ¯ = { ℱ i } 1 ⩽ i ⩽ r \underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{i}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}  is a  Σ \Sigma -collection. We let  Q Q  be a half of the Ext-quiver of the collection  ℱ ¯ = { ℱ i : 1 ⩽ i ⩽ r } \underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r\} . Then, there is a pointed affine  GL 𝐦 \mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{m}} -variety  ( H , y ) (H,y) , and an analytic neighbourhood  U ⊆ H U\subseteq H  of  y y  such that if  𝒰 x \mathcal{U}_{x}  (resp.  𝔘 x \mathfrak{U}_{x} ) denotes the image of  U U  by the canonical map  H → H / / GL 𝐦 H\rightarrow H/\!\!/\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{m}}  (resp.  H → H / GL 𝐦 H\rightarrow H/\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{m}} ), there is a commutative diagram 
 

 
 
 ( 𝔐 Π Q , 𝐦 , 0 𝐦 ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}},0_{\mathbf{m}})}} ( 𝔘 x , y ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathfrak{U}_{x},y)}} ( 𝔐 , x ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathfrak{M},x)}} ( ℳ Π Q , 𝐦 , 0 𝐦 ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}},0_{\mathbf{m}})}} ( 𝒰 x , p ⁡ ( y ) ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{U}_{x},p(y))}} ( ℳ , 𝙹𝙷 ⁡ ( x ) ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{M},\JH(x))}} 𝙹𝙷 𝐦 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH_{\mathbf{m}}} p 𝐦 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbf{m}}} 𝙹𝙷 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH} ȷ ~ x \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\jmath}_{x}} ȷ x \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath_{x}} ȷ 0 𝐦 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath_{0_{\mathbf{m}}}} ȷ ~ 0 𝐦 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\jmath}_{0_{\mathbf{m}}}} ⌟ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner} ⌟ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner} 
 
 
 in which the horizontal morphisms are analytic open immersions and the two squares are Cartesian. 
 
 
 
 The original version of this theorem gives an étale local description of the map  ( 𝔐 , x ) → ( ℳ , 𝙹𝙷 ⁡ ( x ) ) (\mathfrak{M},x)\rightarrow(\mathcal{M},\JH(x)) . For applications to calculating perverse sheaves (as opposed to objects in the derived category of perverse sheaves), working in the analytic topology has some advantages. Under the assumptions given in § 4.6 , the theorem applies for any  𝐂 \mathbf{C} -valued closed point  x x  of  𝔐 \mathfrak{M} . 
 
 
 
 
 
 5.  Roots and the geometry of the good moduli space 

 
 5.1.  Roots for preprojective algebras 

 
 Let  Q Q  be a quiver and let  Π Q \Pi_{Q}  be its preprojective algebra. We let  ( − , − ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) : 𝐍 Q 0 × 𝐍 Q 0 → 𝐙 (-,-)_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}\colon\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}  be the Euler form of the derived preprojective algebra  𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) \mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q) . It is the symmetrization of the Euler form of  Q Q  and, when  Q Q  is not Dynkin, it coincides with the Euler form of  Π Q \Pi_{Q}  itself. We let 
 

 
 
 Σ Π Q ≔ { 𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 ∖ { 0 } ∣  for any nontrivial decomposition  𝐝 = ∑ j = 1 r 𝐝 j , 𝐝 j ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 ∖ { 0 }  one has  ( 2 − ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) > ∑ j = 1 r ( 2 − ( 𝐝 j , 𝐝 j ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) } . \Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\smallsetminus\{0\}\mid\text{ for any nontrivial decomposition }\mathbf{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\mathbf{d}_{j},\mathbf{d}_{j}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\smallsetminus\{0\}\right.\\
\left.\text{ one has }(2-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)})>\sum_{j=1}^{r}(2-(\mathbf{d}_{j},\mathbf{d}_{j})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)})\right\}. 
 
 
 We let  Q 0 Q_{0}  be the set of  real  vertices (loop-free vertices of  Q Q ). For  i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} , we have a reflection 
 

 
 
 s i : 𝐙 Q 0 → 𝐙 Q 0 𝐝 ↦ 𝐝 − ( 1 i , 𝐝 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ​ 1 i . \begin{matrix}s_{i}&\colon&\mathbf{Z}^{Q_{0}}&\rightarrow&\mathbf{Z}^{Q_{0}}\\
&&\mathbf{d}&\mapsto&\mathbf{d}-(1_{i},\mathbf{d})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}1_{i}.\end{matrix} 
 
 
 These reflections generate the  Weyl group   W Q W_{Q}  of  Q Q . 
 
 
 The fundamental cone is 
 

 
 
 C Π Q ≔ { 𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 ∖ { 0 } ∣ supp ⁡ ( 𝐝 ) ​  is connected and  ​ ( 𝐝 , 1 i ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ⩽ 0 ​  for every  i ∈ Q 0 } , C_{\Pi_{Q}}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\smallsetminus\{0\}\mid\operatorname{supp}(\mathbf{d})\text{ is connected and }(\mathbf{d},1_{i})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}\leqslant 0\text{ for every $i\in Q_{0}$}\}, 
 
 
 where the support  supp ⁡ ( 𝐝 ) \operatorname{supp}(\mathbf{d})  of the dimension vector  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  is defined as the full subquiver containing the vertices  i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0}  such that  d i ≠ 0 d_{i}\neq 0 .
In the definition of the fundamental cone, one can replace  Q 0 Q_{0}  by  Q 0 Q_{0}  since if  i i  is a vertex with at least one loop,  ( 1 i , 𝐝 ) ⩽ 0 (1_{i},\mathbf{d})\leqslant 0  automatically for any  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} . We embed  Q 0 Q_{0}  inside  𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  by sending  i ↦ 1 i i\mapsto 1_{i} . Following  [ Cra01 ] , we let 
 

 
 
 R Π Q ≔ W Q ⋅ Q 0 ∪ ( ± W Q ⋅ C Π Q ) R_{\Pi_{Q}}\coloneqq W_{Q}\cdot Q_{0}\cup(\pm W_{Q}\cdot C_{\Pi_{Q}}) 
 
 
 be the set of roots of the quiver  Q Q . We let  R Π Q + ≔ R Π Q ∩ 𝐍 Q 0 R_{\Pi_{Q}}^{+}\coloneqq R_{\Pi_{Q}}\cap\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  be the set of positive roots. As in [loc. cit] we define the set 
 

 
 
 Σ ′ Π Q ≔ { 𝐝 ∈ R Π Q + ∣  for any nontrivial decomposition  𝐝 = ∑ j = 1 r 𝐝 j , 𝐝 j ∈ R Π Q + ∖ { 0 }  one has  ( 2 − ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) > ∑ j = 1 r ( 2 − ( 𝐝 j , 𝐝 j ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) } . \Sigma^{\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{d}\in R_{\Pi_{Q}}^{+}\mid\text{ for any nontrivial decomposition }\mathbf{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\mathbf{d}_{j},\mathbf{d}_{j}\in R_{\Pi_{Q}}^{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}\right.\\
\left.\text{ one has }(2-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)})>\sum_{j=1}^{r}(2-(\mathbf{d}_{j},\mathbf{d}_{j})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)})\right\}. 
 
 
 
 
 We now prove that the set  Σ Π Q ′ \Sigma^{\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}  (which by  [ Cra01 , Theorem 1.2]  characterises dimension vectors for which the preprojective algebra  Π Q \Pi_{Q}  admits simple representations) coincides with  Σ Π Q \Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}  (whose definition does not require the knowledge of roots of the quiver). 
 
 
 Lemma 5.1 . 
 
 The sets  Σ Π Q \Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}  and  Σ Π Q ′ \Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\prime}  coincide. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By changing the conditions on the dimension vectors and on the decompositions allowed, we define a third set 
 

 
 
 Σ ′′ Π Q ≔ { 𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 ∖ { 0 } ∣  for any nontrivial decomposition  𝐝 = ∑ j = 1 r 𝐝 j , 𝐝 j ∈ R Π Q + ∖ { 0 }  one has  ( 2 − ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) > ∑ j = 1 r ( 2 − ( 𝐝 j , 𝐝 j ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) } \Sigma^{\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\smallsetminus\{0\}\mid\text{ for any nontrivial decomposition }\mathbf{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\mathbf{d}_{j},\mathbf{d}_{j}\in R_{\Pi_{Q}}^{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}\right.\\
\left.\text{ one has }(2-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)})>\sum_{j=1}^{r}(2-(\mathbf{d}_{j},\mathbf{d}_{j})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)})\right\} 
 
 
 and a fourth one 
 

 
 
 Σ ′ ′ ′ Π Q ≔ { 𝐝 ∈ R + Π Q ∣  for any nontrivial decomposition  𝐝 = ∑ j = 1 r 𝐝 j , 𝐝 j ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 ∖ { 0 }  one has  ( 2 − ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) > ∑ j = 1 r ( 2 − ( 𝐝 j , 𝐝 j ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) } . \Sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{d}\in R^{+}_{\Pi_{Q}}\mid\text{ for any nontrivial decomposition }\mathbf{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\mathbf{d}_{j},\mathbf{d}_{j}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\smallsetminus\{0\}\right.\\
\left.\text{ one has }(2-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)})>\sum_{j=1}^{r}(2-(\mathbf{d}_{j},\mathbf{d}_{j})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)})\right\}. 
 
 
 
 
 Clearly,  Σ Π Q ′ ⊆ Σ Π Q ′′ ⊇ Σ Π Q ⊇ Σ Π Q ′′′ \Sigma^{\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}\supseteq\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}\supseteq\Sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}  and  Σ Π Q ′′′ ⊆ Σ Π Q ′ \Sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}} . We eventually prove that these sets all coincide. 
 
 
 We prove that  Σ Π Q = Σ Π Q ′′ \Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}=\Sigma^{\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}} . We let  𝐝 ∈ Σ Π Q ′′ \mathbf{d}\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}} . Let  𝐝 = ∑ j = 1 r 𝐝 j \mathbf{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\mathbf{d}_{j} ,  𝐝 j ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}_{j}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  be a nontrivial decomposition of  𝐝 \mathbf{d} . For  1 ⩽ j ⩽ r 1\leqslant j\leqslant r , we let  𝐝 j = ∑ u = 1 s j 𝐝 j , u \mathbf{d}_{j}=\sum_{u=1}^{s_{j}}\mathbf{d}_{j,u}  be the canonical decomposition of  𝐝 j \mathbf{d}_{j}  (see  [ Cra01 ]  for the definition of this decomposition). Then, by definition,  𝐝 j , u ∈ R Π Q + \mathbf{d}_{j,u}\in R^{+}_{\Pi_{Q}}  and by  [ Cra01 , Lemma 4.5] 
 

 
 
 2 − ( 𝐝 j , 𝐝 j ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ⩽ ∑ u = 1 s j ( 2 − ( 𝐝 j , u ​ 𝐝 j , u ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) 2-(\mathbf{d}_{j},\mathbf{d}_{j})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}\leqslant\sum_{u=1}^{s_{j}}(2-(\mathbf{d}_{j,u}\mathbf{d}_{j,u})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}) 
 
 
 for  1 ⩽ j ⩽ r 1\leqslant j\leqslant r . The decomposition  𝐝 = ∑ j , u 𝐝 j , u \mathbf{d}=\sum_{j,u}\mathbf{d}_{j,u}  is such that  𝐝 j , u ∈ Σ Π Q ′′ ⊆ R Π Q + \mathbf{d}_{j,u}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq R^{+}_{\Pi_{Q}} , and is nontrivial. So by definition of  Σ Π Q ′′ \Sigma^{\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}} , we have 
 

 
 
 2 − ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) > ∑ j , u ( 2 − ( 𝐝 j , u ​ 𝐝 j , u ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) ⩾ ∑ j = 1 r ( 2 − ( 𝐝 j , 𝐝 j ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ) . 2-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}>\sum_{j,u}(2-(\mathbf{d}_{j,u}\mathbf{d}_{j,u})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)})\geqslant\sum_{j=1}^{r}(2-(\mathbf{d}_{j},\mathbf{d}_{j})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}). 
 
 
 Therefore,  𝐝 ∈ Σ Π Q \mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} . 
 
 
 Now, we prove the equality  Σ Π Q ′′′ = Σ Π Q \Sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}=\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} . Let  𝐝 ∈ Σ Π Q \mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} . All what we have to prove is  𝐝 ∈ R Π Q + \mathbf{d}\in R^{+}_{\Pi_{Q}} . We will actually prove that  𝐝 \mathbf{d}  is in the fundamental cone  C Π Q C_{\Pi_{Q}} . First, the support of  𝐝 \mathbf{d}  has to be connected, as otherwise we can write  𝐝 = 𝐝 1 + 𝐝 2 \mathbf{d}=\mathbf{d}_{1}+\mathbf{d}_{2}  with  𝐝 1 , 𝐝 2 ≠ 0 \mathbf{d}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{2}\neq 0  and  ( 𝐝 1 , 𝐝 2 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) = 0 (\mathbf{d}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{2})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}=0 . We then obtain 
 

 
 
 2 − ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) = 2 − ( 𝐝 1 , 𝐝 1 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) − ( 𝐝 2 , 𝐝 2 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) < 2 − ( 𝐝 1 , 𝐝 1 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) + 2 − ( 𝐝 2 , 𝐝 2 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) , 2-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}=2-(\mathbf{d}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{1})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}-(\mathbf{d}_{2},\mathbf{d}_{2})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}<2-(\mathbf{d}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{1})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}+2-(\mathbf{d}_{2},\mathbf{d}_{2})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}, 
 
 
 contradicting the condition defining  Σ Π Q \Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} .
Let  i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} . If  d i = 0 d_{i}=0 , then clearly,  ( 𝐝 , 1 i ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ⩽ 0 (\mathbf{d},1_{i})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}\leqslant 0 . If  𝐝 = d i ​ 1 i \mathbf{d}=d_{i}1_{i} , then since  ( 1 i , 1 i ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) = 2 (1_{i},1_{i})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}=2 , we must have  d i = 1 d_{i}=1  and clearly,  1 i ∈ Σ Π Q ′′′ 1_{i}\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}} . Otherwise, we write  𝐝 = 1 i + 𝐝 ′ \mathbf{d}=1_{i}+\mathbf{d}^{\prime} , with  𝐝 ′ = 𝐝 − 1 i ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 ∖ { 0 } \mathbf{d}^{\prime}=\mathbf{d}-1_{i}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\smallsetminus\{0\} . By definition of  Σ Π Q \Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} , we have 
 

 
 
 2 − ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) > 2 − ( 1 i , 1 i ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) + 2 − ( 𝐝 ′ , 𝐝 ′ ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) 2-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}>2-(1_{i},1_{i})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}+2-(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)} 
 
 
 and this simplifies to 
 

 
 
 ( 1 i , 𝐝 ′ ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) < − 1 . (1_{i},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}<-1. 
 
 
 From this, we obtain 
 

 
 
 ( 𝐝 , 1 i ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) = 2 + ( 1 i , 𝐝 ′ ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ) ⩽ 0 . (\mathbf{d},1_{i})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}=2+(1_{i},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q)}\leqslant 0. 
 
 
 
 
 We conclude that  𝐝 \mathbf{d}  is in the fundamental cone. 
 
 
 We deduce that  Σ Π Q ′ ⊆ Σ Π Q ′′′ \Sigma^{\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}  and the obvious inclusion  Σ Π Q ′′′ ⊆ Σ Π Q ′ \Sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}  allows us to conclude that all four sets  Σ Π Q , Σ Π Q ′ , Σ Π Q ′′ \Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}},\Sigma^{\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}},\Sigma^{\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}  and  Σ Π Q ′′′ \Sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\Pi_{Q}}  coincide.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5.2.  Ext-quivers and roots 

 
 Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be a 2-Calabi–Yau Abelian category. We let  ℱ ¯ = { ℱ 1 , … , ℱ r } \underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}  be a collection of simple objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  and let  Q ¯ ℱ ¯ \overline{Q}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  be the Ext-quiver of  ℱ ¯ \underline{\mathcal{F}} . Recall the morphism of monoids  λ ℱ ¯ : 𝐍 ℱ ¯ → K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\rightarrow\K(\mathcal{A}) .
(see § 4.6.1 ). 
 
 
 Lemma 5.2 . 
 
 The pull-back by  λ ℱ ¯ \lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  of the Euler form of  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  is the Euler form of  𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ℱ ¯ ) \mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}) : for any  𝐝 , 𝐞 ∈ 𝐍 ℱ ¯ \mathbf{d},\mathbf{e}\in\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}} ,  ( 𝐝 , 𝐞 ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( Q ℱ ¯ ) = ( λ ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝐝 ) , λ ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝐞 ) ) 𝒞 (\mathbf{d},\mathbf{e})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}})}=(\lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbf{d}),\lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbf{e}))_{\mathscr{C}} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This is a direct calculation using the definition of the Ext-quiver (§ 4.6.1 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 We now define the sets of primitive positive roots  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  and simple positive roots  Φ 𝒜 + \Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}  for  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  by 
 

 
 (5.1) 
 
 Σ 𝒜 ≔ Σ π 0 ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 \displaystyle\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq\Sigma_{\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}} 
 
 
 (5.2) 
 
 Φ 𝒜 + ≔ Φ π 0 ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 + \displaystyle\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}\coloneqq\Phi_{\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}}^{+} 
 
 
 according to § 3.1  for the monoid with bilinear form  ( π 0 ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) , ( − , − ) 𝒞 ) (\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}),(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}) . We also have the roots for  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \K(\mathcal{A}) ,  Σ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \Sigma_{\K(\mathcal{A})}  and  Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + \Phi^{+}_{\K(\mathcal{A})} . 
 
 
 Proposition 5.3 . 
 
 
 (1)  
 
 Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  is the set of connected components  m ∈ π 0 ​ ( ℳ ) m\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M})  such that  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  has simple objects of class  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , or equivalently such that  𝙹𝙷 𝒜 , m \JH_{\mathcal{A},m}  is a  𝐆 m \mathbf{G}_{m} -gerbe over an open subset of  ℳ 𝒜 , a \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a} , 
 
 (2)  
 
 λ ℱ ¯ − 1 ​ ( Σ 𝒜 ) = Σ Π Q ℱ ¯ \lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{-1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}})=\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}} , 
 
 (3)  
 
 λ ℱ ¯ − 1 ​ ( Φ 𝒜 + ) = Φ Π Q ℱ ¯ + \lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{-1}(\Phi^{+}_{\mathcal{A}})=\Phi^{+}_{\Pi_{Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}} . This last equality is compatible with the decompositions of simple positive roots in real, isotropic and hyperbolic roots, that is  λ ℱ ¯ − 1 ​ ( Φ 𝒜 + , ⋆ ) = Φ Π Q ℱ ¯ + , ⋆ \lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{-1}(\Phi^{+,\star}_{\mathcal{A}})=\Phi^{+,\star}_{\Pi_{Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}}  for  ⋆ ∈ { , iso , hyp } \star\in\{\real,\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{hyp}\} . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 
 (1)  
 
 This is known for  𝒜 = Rep ⁡ Π Q \mathcal{A}=\operatorname{Rep}\Pi_{Q}  by  [ Cra01 , Theorem 1.2] . Since the stabilizer of a simple representation is  𝐆 m \mathbf{G}_{m} ,  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  has a simple object of class  m m  if and only if  𝙹𝙷 m \JH_{m}  is a  𝐆 m \mathbf{G}_{m} -gerb over an open subset of  ℳ m \mathcal{M}_{m} , this condition can be checked analytically locally and so (1) is a consequence of the local neighbourhood theorem and Lemma  5.2 
 
 (2)  
 
 This is a consequence of Lemma  3.2 . 
 
 (3)  
 
 This is a consequence of (1) and Lemma  5.2 . 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5.3.  Geometry of the good moduli space 

 
 Using the local neighbourhood theorem (Theorem  4.2 ) and the geometric properties of the moduli spaces of representations of preprojective algebras  [ Cra01 ] , we derive geometric properties of the good moduli space of  2 2 -Calabi–Yau categories. 
 
 
 We define the function 
 

 
 
 p : π 0 ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) → 𝐙 a ↦ 2 − ( a , a ) 𝒞 . \begin{matrix}p&\colon&\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})&\rightarrow&\mathbf{Z}\\
&&a&\mapsto&2-(a,a)_{\mathscr{C}}.\end{matrix} 
 
 
 
 
 Proposition 5.4 . 
 
 
 (1)  
 
 Let  a ∈ Σ 𝒜 a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Then,  ℳ 𝒜 , a \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}  is irreducible of dimension  p ⁡ ( a ) p(a) . In particular, for  a ∈ Σ 𝒜 a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  such that  ( a , a ) = 0 (a,a)=0 ,  dim ℳ 𝒜 , a = 2 \dim\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}=2  and for  a ∈ Φ 𝒜 + , a\in\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{+,\real} ,  ℳ 𝒜 , a = pt \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}=\mathrm{pt} , 
 
 (2)  
 
 Let  a ∈ Φ 𝒜 + , iso a\in\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{+,\mathrm{iso}} ,  a = l ​ a ′ a=la^{\prime}  with  l ∈ 𝐍 l\in\mathbf{N}  and  a ′ ∈ Σ 𝒜 a^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Then  ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la}  is irreducible of dimension  2 ​ l 2l . 
 
 (3)  
 
 Let  a ∈ Σ 𝒜 a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  be an isotropic class. Then, for any  l ∈ 𝐍 l\in\mathbf{N} , the map 
 

 
 
 S l ​ ℳ 𝒜 , a → ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a S^{l}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la} 
 
 
 given by the direct sum induces an isomorphism between the underlying reduced schemes. 
 
 (4)  
 
 Let  a ∈ Φ 𝒜 + , iso a\in\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{+,\mathrm{iso}} . We write  a = l ​ a ′ a=la^{\prime}  for  a ′ ∈ Σ 𝒜 a^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  and  l ∈ 𝐍 ⩾ 1 l\in\mathbf{N}_{\geqslant 1} . Then, the stabilizer of a general element  x ∈ ℳ 𝒜 , a x\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}  is isomorphic to  ( 𝐂 ∗ ) l (\mathbf{C}^{*})^{l} . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The property ( 1 ) is true for  𝒜 = Rep ⁡ Π Q \mathcal{A}=\operatorname{Rep}\Pi_{Q}  by  [ Cra01 , Theorem 1.2]  and is easily deduced for any  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  by the local neighbourhood theorem (Theorem  4.2 ). Similarly, ( 2 ) is true for preprojective algebras of quivers and since irreducibility and dimension of a connected component can be checked in the neigbourhoods of closed points, we obtain the statement for any  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  by Theorem  4.2 . ( 4 ) is a direct consequence of ( 3 ). Therefore, we finish by proving ( 3 ). The direct sum gives a finite map  ⊕ : ℳ 𝒜 , a l → ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a \oplus\colon\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}^{l}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la}  (by Assumption  5 ). It is  S l S_{l} -invariant and the induced map  ⊕ : S l ​ ℳ 𝒜 , a → ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a \oplus\colon S^{l}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la}  is proper and injective. By Proposition  5.4 , the algebraic varieties  ( S l ​ ℳ 𝒜 , a ) red (S^{l}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a})_{\mathrm{red}}  and  ( ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a ) red (\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la})_{\mathrm{red}}  are of the same dimension  2 ​ l 2l . Therefore,  ⊕ \oplus  induces a homeomorphism  ( S l ​ ℳ 𝒜 , a ) red → ( ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a ) red (S^{l}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a})_{\mathrm{red}}\rightarrow(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la})_{\mathrm{red}}  between these two algebraic varieties. By  [ Cra02 ] , the map of the Proposition  S l ​ ℳ Π Q , 𝐝 → ℳ Π Q , l ​ 𝐝 S^{l}\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},l\mathbf{d}}  induces an isomorphism between the underlying reduced schemes when  𝒜 = Rep ⁡ Π Q \mathcal{A}=\operatorname{Rep}\Pi_{Q}  and  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  is an indivisible imaginary root and so, by the local neighbourhood theorem, the morphism  ( S l ​ ℳ 𝒜 , a ) red → ( ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a ) red (S^{l}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a})_{\mathrm{red}}\rightarrow(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la})_{\mathrm{red}}  induces isomorphisms between the completions of local rings at all closed points. Therefore, it is an isomorphism.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Let  a ∈ Σ 𝒜 ∩ Φ 𝒜 + , iso a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\cap\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{+,\mathrm{iso}}  be an indivisible isotropic root. We let  ℳ 𝒜 , a simp \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}^{\mathrm{simp}}  be the locus of simple objects. For  l ⩾ 1 l\geqslant 1  we let  ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a ′ ⊆ ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la}  be the locus of semisimple objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  whose simple factors are of class  a a  (so  ℳ 𝒜 , a simp = ℳ 𝒜 , a ′ \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}^{\mathrm{simp}}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}^{\prime} ). By Proposition  5.4  ( 3 ), it is open in  ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la} . We let  𝔐 𝒜 , l ​ a ′ ⊆ 𝔐 𝒜 , l ​ a \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A},la}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A},la}  be the open substack parametrising objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  whose simple subquotients are of class  a a : we have a Cartesian diagram 
 

 
 
 𝔐 𝒜 , l ​ a ′ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},la}}} 𝔐 𝒜 , l ​ a {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A},la}}} ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a ′ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},la}}} ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la}}} 𝙹𝙷 𝒜 , la \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH_{\mathcal{A},la}} 𝙹𝙷 𝒜 , la ′ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},la}} ȷ ~ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\jmath}} ȷ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath} ⌟ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner} 
 
 
 and the isomorphism of Proposition  5.4  ( 3 ) induces an isomorphism of reduced schemes  ( ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a ′ ) red ≅ ( S l ​ ℳ 𝒜 , a ′ ) red (\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},la})_{\mathrm{red}}\cong(S^{l}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}^{\prime})_{\mathrm{red}} . 
 
 
 
 
 6.  The cohomological Hall algebra and the BPS algebra 

 
 We let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be a  2 2 -Calabi–Yau Abelian category, and  𝙹𝙷 : 𝔐 𝒜 → ℳ 𝒜 \JH\colon\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  be its moduli stack of objects and good moduli space as in § 4 . 
 
 
 6.1.  The cohomological Hall algebra 

 
 In  [ DHS22 , §9] , we define the  relative cohomological Hall algebra  of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  as an algebra structure on the complex of mixed Hodge modules  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ≔ ( 𝙹𝙷 𝒜 ) ∗ ​ 𝔻 ​ 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 𝒜 vir \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq(\JH_{\mathcal{A}})_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathrm{vir}} , given by the morphism (c.f. ( 4.3 )) 
 

 
 
 𝔪 : 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ⊡ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 → 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \mathfrak{m}\colon\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\boxdot\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}} 
 
 
 satisfying the standard axioms of associativity and unitality in monoidal categories, where the tensor structure  ⊡ \boxdot  on  𝒟 + ​ ( MHM ​ ( ℳ ) ) \mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}))  is defined as in § 3.5 . The product is defined via (virtual) pullback and pushforward from the stack of short exact sequences of objects in  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . The mixed Hodge module  𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 𝒜 vir ∈ 𝒟 + ​ ( MHM ⁡ ( 𝔐 𝒜 ) ) \underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}))  is the twisted constant mixed Hodge module  𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 𝒜 , a ⊗ 𝐋 ( a , a ) 𝒞 / 2 \underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{(a,a)_{\mathscr{C}}/2}  on the connected component  𝔐 𝒜 , a \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}  of  𝔐 𝒜 \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}} . We are committing here a standard abuse of notation: while we do not define anywhere the derived category of mixed Hodge modules on the stack  𝔐 𝒜 \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}} , the direct image  ( 𝙹𝙷 𝒜 ) ∗ ​ 𝔻 ​ 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 𝒜 vir (\JH_{\mathcal{A}})_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathrm{vir}}  is still defined, as an unbounded complex of mixed Hodge modules: see  [ Dav20 , §2.1]  for details. 
 
 
 The  absolute cohomological Hall algebra  is obtained by taking the derived global sections  H ∗ ⁡ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) \HO^{*}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}) , with the multiplication  H ∗ ⁡ ( 𝔪 ) \HO^{*}(\mathfrak{m}) . If  ℬ ⊆ 𝒜 \mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}  is a Serre subcategory and  ı : ℳ ℬ ↪ ℳ 𝒜 \imath\colon\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  is the inclusion of the submonoid parametrising semisimple objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  contained in  ℬ \mathcal{B} , we let  𝔐 ℬ \mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}  be the stack defined by the following Cartesian diagram: 
 

 
 
 𝔐 ℬ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}}} 𝔐 𝒜 {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}}} ℳ ℬ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}}} ℳ 𝒜 . {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}.}} 𝙹𝙷 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH} ı \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath} ⌟ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner} 
 
 
 Then, by base change, the morphism  ı ! 𝔪 \imath^{!}\mathfrak{m}  gives  ı ! 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ∈ 𝒟 + ( MHM ( ℳ ℬ ) ) \imath^{!}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}))  an algebra structure. It is the  relative cohomological Hall algebra  of  ℬ \mathcal{B} , and  H ∗ ( ı ! 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) \HO^{*}(\imath^{!}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})  is the  absolute cohomological Hall algebra  of  ℬ \mathcal{B} . By base change in the diagram 
 

 
 
 ℳ ℬ × ℳ ℬ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}}} ℳ ℬ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}}} ℳ 𝒜 × ℳ 𝒜 {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}}} ℳ 𝒜 , {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}},} ı \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath} ⊕ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus} ⊕ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus} ı × ı \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath\times\imath} ⌟ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner} 
 
 
 which we implicitly assumed to be Cartesian (we say that  ℳ ℬ \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}  is a  saturated submonoid  of  ℳ 𝒜 \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}} ) the functor  ı ! : 𝒟 + ( MHM ( ℳ 𝒜 ) ) → 𝒟 + ( MHM ( ℳ ℬ ) ) \imath^{!}\colon\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}))\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}))  is strictly monoidal. 
 
 
 The cases most relevant to this paper are the following. 
 
 (1)  
 
 ℬ \mathcal{B}  is the Serre subcategory of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  generated by a collection of pairwise non-isomorphic simple objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} ,  ℱ ¯ = { ℱ 1 , … , ℱ r } \underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\} . Then,  ℳ ℬ ≅ 𝐍 ℱ ¯ \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}\cong\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  is a discrete submonoid of  ℳ 𝒜 \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}} . Therefore we may identify  H ∗ ( ı ! 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) = ı ! 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \HO^{*}(\imath^{!}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})=\imath^{!}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}} . It is called the cohomological Hall algebra of  ℱ ¯ \underline{\mathcal{F}} . If  𝒜 = Rep ⁡ Π Q \mathcal{A}=\operatorname{Rep}\Pi_{Q}  and  ℱ ¯ \underline{\mathcal{F}}  is the collection of nilpotent one-dimensional representations of  Π Q \Pi_{Q} ,  ℱ ¯ = 𝒩 = { S i : i ∈ Q 0 } \underline{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{N}=\{S_{i}\colon i\in Q_{0}\} , we obtain the  nilpotent cohomological Hall algebra ,  𝒜 ¯ Π Q 𝒩 \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{N}} . 
 
 (2)  
 
 𝒜 = Rep ⁡ Π Q \mathcal{A}=\operatorname{Rep}\Pi_{Q}  and  ℬ \mathcal{B}  is the category of  strictly semi-nilpotent representations  of  Π Q \Pi_{Q} 4 4 
                     4 
                    
                    
                    
                  A representation  M M  of  Π Q \Pi_{Q}  is called  strictly semi-nilpotent  if there exists a filtration  0 = M 0 ⊆ M 1 ⊆ … ⊆ M r = M 0=M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq M_{r}=M  with consecutive subquotients supported at a single vertex, such that for  α ∈ Q 1 \alpha\in Q_{1} ,  x α ​ M i ⊆ M i x_{\alpha}M_{i}\subseteq M_{i}  and for  α ∈ Q 1 ∗ \alpha\in Q_{1}^{*} , or  x α x_{\alpha}  not a loop,  x α ​ M i ⊆ M i − 1 x_{\alpha}M_{i}\subseteq M_{i-1} .  (in the sense of  [ BSV20 ] , see also  [ DHS22 , §8.3.2] ). Then,  ℳ Π Q 𝒮 ​ 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 ≔ ℳ ℬ \mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}  is the closed submonoid of  ℳ 𝒜 \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  of semisimple representations of  Π Q \Pi_{Q}  such that the only arrows of  Q ¯ \overline{Q}  acting possibly nontrivially are the loops in  Q 1 ⊆ Q ¯ 1 = Q 1 ⊔ Q 1 ∗ Q_{1}\subseteq\overline{Q}_{1}=Q_{1}\sqcup Q_{1}^{*} . We call  𝒜 ¯ Π Q 𝒮 ​ 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 ≔ ı ! 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}\coloneqq\imath^{!}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}  the  relative strictly semi-nilpotent cohomological Hall algebra  and  H ∗ ⁡ ( 𝒜 ¯ Π Q 𝒮 ​ 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 ) \HO^{*}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SSN}})  is the  strictly semi-nilpotent cohomological Hall algebra . 
 
 
 The proof of Theorem  1.1  uses in a crucial way that one can describe fully explicitly the  degree zero strictly semi-nilpotent cohomological Hall algebra   H 0 ⁡ ( 𝒜 ¯ Π Q 𝒮 ​ 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 ) \HO^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}) , see Theorem  7.9 . 
 
 
 
 6.2.  The BPS algebra 

 
 We let  𝒜 \mathcal{A} ,  𝙹𝙷 𝒜 : 𝔐 𝒜 → ℳ 𝒜 \JH_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}} ,  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 = ( 𝙹𝙷 𝒜 ) ∗ ​ 𝔻 ​ 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 𝒜 vir ∈ 𝒟 + ​ ( MHM ⁡ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) ) \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}=(\JH_{\mathcal{A}})_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}))  and  𝔪 : 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ⊡ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 → 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \mathfrak{m}\colon\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\boxdot\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}  be as in § 4.1  and § 6.1 . In particular, we make assumptions  1 – 6 . 
 
 
 Lemma 6.1 . 
 
 The complex of mixed Hodge modules  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}  is concentrated in nonnegative cohomological degrees:  ℋ i ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) = 0 \mathcal{H}^{i}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})=0  for  i < 0 i<0 . The mixed Hodge module  ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) \mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})  is semisimple. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This is part of  [ Dav20 , Theorem A] , which shows, in particular, that the mixed Hodge module  ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) \mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})  is pure of weight zero.
∎ 
 
 
 
 It follows that  τ ⩽ 0 ​ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ≅ ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) \tau^{\leqslant 0}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\cong\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}) . Since  ⊡ \boxdot  is bi-exact, it also follows that  τ ⩽ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ⊡ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) ≅ ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ⊡ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) ≅ ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) ⊡ ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) \tau^{\leqslant 0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\boxdot\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})\cong\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\boxdot\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})\cong\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})\boxdot\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}) . 
 
 
 Corollary 6.2 . 
 
 The map  ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝔪 ) : ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) ⊡ ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) → ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) \mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathfrak{m})\colon\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})\boxdot\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})  is an associative algebra in the monoidal category  ( MHM ⁡ ( ℳ ) , ⊡ ) (\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}),\boxdot) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This comes from the fact that  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}  is concentrated in nonnegative cohomological degrees (Lemma  6.1 ). The operation  ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝔪 ) \mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathfrak{m})  is associative because  𝔪 \mathfrak{m}  is.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We set  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg := ℋ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}:=\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})  with its algebra structure given above. This algebra object is called  the relative BPS algebra . Its cohomology (derived global sections) is denoted  BPS ¯ 𝒜 , Alg := H ∗ ⁡ ( ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ) \underline{\aBPS}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}:=\HO^{*}(\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}})  and called  the BPS algebra . 
 
 
 The next proposition follows from the decomposition theorem for 2CY categories  [ Dav21 ] . 
 
 
 Proposition 6.3 . 
 
 The natural morphism of algebras  BPS ¯ 𝒜 , Alg → H ∗ ⁡ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) \underline{\aBPS}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}\rightarrow\HO^{*}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})  is injective. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The decomposition theorem for 2CY categories of  [ Dav21 , Theorem B]  asserts in particular that the underlying complex of mixed Hodge modules  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}  is the direct sum of its shifted cohomology mixed Hodge modules and so the map  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg → 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}  admits a retraction. Therefore, the map  BPS ¯ 𝒜 , Alg → H ∗ ⁡ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ) \underline{\mathrm{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}\rightarrow\HO^{*}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}})  obtained by taking cohomology also admits a retraction and is injective.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 6.3.  ψ \psi -twists 

 
 6.3.1.  Twisted multiplication 

 
 In  [ DHS22 ] , we introduce a twist of the multiplication of the cohomological Hall algebra by a bilinear form  ψ \psi . This twist is necessary to have the structure of an enveloping algebra on the BPS algebra (it appears already in the PBW theorem for CoHAs of Jacobi algebras, see  [ DM20 , §1.6] ). In vague terms (made precise in  [ Hen22 ] ), this twist exchanges the specialisation at  q = − 1 q=-1  of the quantum group  𝐔 q ⁡ ( 𝔤 ) \UEA_{q}(\mathfrak{g})  with the enveloping algebra  𝐔 ⁡ ( 𝔤 ) \UEA(\mathfrak{g})  (the specialisation  q = 1 q=1 ). In this section, we explain this twist. 
 
 
 As in § 1.2  we choose a monoid  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \K(\mathcal{A})  and fix a morphism of monoids  cl : π 0 ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) → K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \mathrm{cl}\colon\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\K(\mathcal{A})  through which the Euler form factors. We assume again that  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \K(\mathcal{A})  can be embedded in a lattice and that  cl \mathrm{cl}  has finite fibres. We let  ψ : K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) × K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) → 𝐙 / 2 ​ 𝐙 \psi\colon\K(\mathcal{A})\times\K(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}  be a bilinear form such that for any  a , b ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) a,b\in\K(\mathcal{A}) , 
 

 
 
 ψ ⁡ ( a , b ) + ψ ⁡ ( b , a ) ≡ ( a , b ) 𝒞 ( mod 2 ) . \psi(a,b)+\psi(b,a)\equiv(a,b)_{\mathscr{C}}\pmod{2}. 
 
 
 Such a bilinear form exists since for any  a ∈ π 0 ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) a\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}) ,  ( a , a ) 𝒞 ≡ 0 ( mod 2 ) (a,a)_{\mathscr{C}}\equiv 0\pmod{2} . Indeed, it follows that any matrix  M M  representing the form induced by  ( − , − ) 𝒞 (-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}  on  K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \K(\mathcal{A})  has even entries on the diagonal, and we may define  ψ ⁡ ( − , − ) \psi(-,-)  via the matrix obtained by replacing all entries in  M M  above the diagonal by zeros. 
 
 
 For such a bilinear form  ψ \psi , we let  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\psi}  be the relative cohomological Hall algebra with the multiplication  𝔪 a , b ψ ≔ ( − 1 ) ψ ⁡ ( a , b ) ​ 𝔪 a , b : 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ ⊠ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ → 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ \mathfrak{m}_{a,b}^{\psi}\coloneqq(-1)^{\psi(a,b)}\mathfrak{m}_{a,b}\colon\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\psi}\boxtimes\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\psi}\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\psi} . As complexes of mixed Hodge modules on  ℳ 𝒜 \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}} ,  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ = 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\psi}=\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}} . The twisted multiplication induces twisted multiplications  𝔪 ψ \mathfrak{m}^{\psi}  on the relative BPS algebra  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}} , on the absolute CoHA  H ∗ ⁡ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 \HO^{*}\!\!\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}  and the absolute BPS algebra  BPS ¯ 𝒜 , Alg \underline{\mathrm{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}} . We denote by  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ψ \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi} ,  H ∗ ⁡ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ \HO^{*}\!\!\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\psi}  and  BPS ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ψ \underline{\mathrm{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}  the algebra objects with the twisted multiplications. 
 
 
 
 6.3.2.  Induced twist for Ext-quivers 

 
 Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be an Abelian subcategory of a 2CY category as in § 4 . If  ℱ ¯ \underline{\mathcal{F}}  is a collection of simple objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , we have the Ext-quiver  Q ¯ ℱ ¯ \overline{Q}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  (§ 4.6.1 ) and the preprojective algebra  Π Q ℱ ¯ \Pi_{Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}} . Using the morphism of monoids ( 4.4 ), we obtain by pullback a bilinear form  ψ \psi  on  𝐍 ( Q ℱ ¯ ) 0 \mathbf{N}^{(Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}})_{0}} . By Lemma  5.2 , for any  𝐝 , 𝐝 ′ ∈ 𝐍 ( Q ℱ ¯ ) 0 \mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}^{(Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}})_{0}} ,  ψ ⁡ ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ′ ) + ψ ⁡ ( 𝐝 ′ , 𝐝 ) ≡ ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ′ ) 𝒢 2 ​ ( 𝐂 ​ Q ℱ ¯ ) ( mod 2 ) \psi(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})+\psi(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{d})\equiv(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})_{\mathscr{G}_{2}(\mathbf{C}Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}})}\pmod{2} . 
 
 
 
 
 6.4.  Primitive summands 

 
 6.4.1.  Real and hyperbolic primitive summands 

 
 In this section, we define the Chevalley generators for primitive positive roots  m ∈ Σ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) m\in\Sigma_{\K(\mathcal{A})} . 
 
 
 Lemma 6.4   ( [ Dav21 , Theorem 6.6] ) . 
 
 For any  m ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) m\in\K(\mathcal{A})  such that  𝙹𝙷 𝒜 , m \JH_{\mathcal{A},m}  is a  𝐆 m \mathbf{G}_{m} -gerb over an open subset (that is,  m ∈ Σ 𝒜 m\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} , see § 5.1 ), we have a canonical monomorphism 
 

 
 
 ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 , m ) → ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ( ψ ) . \underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m})\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{(\psi)}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 6.4.2.  Isotropic primitive summands 

 
 In this section, we describe the generators for isotropic positive roots. 
 
 
 Let  a ∈ Σ 𝒜 a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  be isotropic. We let 
 

 
 
 Δ l : ℳ 𝒜 , a ′ → ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a ′ \Delta_{l}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},a}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},la} 
 
 
 be the diagonal inclusion (see § 5.3  for the meaning of the prime symbols). 
 
 
 For  a ∈ Σ 𝒜 a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} , such that  ( a , a ) 𝒞 = 0 (a,a)_{\mathscr{C}}=0  ( a a  is isotropic), we let  𝔐 ′ 𝒜 , ∙ a = ⨆ l ⩾ 0 𝔐 ′ 𝒜 , l ​ a \mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a}=\bigsqcup_{l\geqslant 0}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},la}  and similarly for  ℳ ′ 𝒜 , ∙ a \mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a} . We let  𝙹𝙷 ′ 𝒜 , ∙ a : 𝔐 ′ 𝒜 , ∙ a → ℳ ′ 𝒜 , ∙ a \JH^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a}\colon\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a}  be the restriction of the map  𝙹𝙷 𝒜 \JH_{\mathcal{A}} . We define  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ′ ψ ≔ ȷ ~ ∗ ​ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ \underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\psi}_{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq\widetilde{\jmath}^{*}\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\psi}_{\mathcal{A}} , with  ȷ \jmath  defined as in § 5.3 . The mixed Hodge module complex  𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ′ ψ \underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\psi}_{\mathcal{A}}  inherits a multiplication morphism given by restricting  𝔪 ψ : 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ ⊡ 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ → 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ψ \mathfrak{m}^{\psi}\colon\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\psi}_{\mathcal{A}}\boxdot\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\psi}_{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\psi}_{\mathcal{A}} . 
 
 
 Theorem 6.5 . 
 
 Let  a ∈ Σ 𝒜 a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  be isotropic. We have a PBW isomorphism of mixed Hodge module complexes 
 

 
 (6.1) 
 
 Sym ⊡ ( ⨁ l ⩾ 1 ( Δ l ) ∗ 𝐐 ¯ ℳ 𝒜 , a ′ ⊗ 𝐋 − 1 ⊗ H 𝐂 ∗ ∗ ) ≅ ( 𝙹𝙷 𝒜 , ∙ a ′ ) ∗ 𝔻 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 ′ 𝒜 , ∙ a vir = 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ′ ψ , \Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\bigoplus_{l\geqslant 1}(\Delta_{l})_{*}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},a}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{-1}\otimes\HO^{*}_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}\right)\cong(\JH^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a})_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a}}^{\mathrm{vir}}=\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\psi}_{\mathcal{A}}, 
 
 
 i.e. a morphism  ⨁ l ⩾ 1 ( Δ l ) ∗ ​ 𝐐 ¯ ℳ 𝒜 , a ′ ⊗ 𝐋 − 1 ⊗ H 𝐂 ∗ ∗ → 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ′ \bigoplus_{l\geqslant 1}(\Delta_{l})_{*}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},a}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{-1}\otimes\HO^{*}_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}  inducing the above morphism via the product structure on the target. The morphism ( 6.1 ) induces an isomorphism of algebra objects in  ( MHM ( ℳ 𝒜 , ∙ a ′ ) , ⊡ ) (\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a}),\boxdot) : 
 

 
 
 ℋ 0 ( ( 𝙹𝙷 𝒜 , ∙ a ′ ) ∗ 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 ′ 𝒜 , ∙ a ) = τ ⩽ 0 ( 𝙹𝙷 𝒜 , ∙ a ′ ) ∗ 𝔻 𝐐 ¯ 𝔐 ′ 𝒜 , ∙ a vir ≅ Sym ⊡ ( ⨁ l ⩾ 1 ( Δ l ) ∗ 𝐐 ¯ ℳ 𝒜 , a ′ ⊗ 𝐋 − 1 ) . \mathcal{H}^{0}((\JH^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a})_{*}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a}})=\tau^{\leqslant 0}(\JH^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a})_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},\bullet a}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\cong\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\bigoplus_{l\geqslant 1}(\Delta_{l})_{*}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},a}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{-1}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We have a Cartesian diagram 
 

 
 
 𝔐 𝒜 , a ′ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},a}}} 𝔐 𝒜 , l ​ a ′ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},la}}} ℳ 𝒜 , a ′ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},a}}} ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a ′ . {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},la}}.} Δ l \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{l}} 𝙹𝙷 𝒜 , la ′ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\JH^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},la}} q l \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{l}} Δ l ~ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Delta_{l}}} ⌟ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner} 
 
 
 The objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  parametrised by the closed points  x x  in the image of  Δ l \Delta_{l}  are of the form  S ⊕ l S^{\oplus l}  where  S S  is a simple object of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  of class  a a . The Ext-quiver of  S S  is the double of the Jordan quiver (it has one vertex – corresponding to  S S , and  1 = 1 2 ​ ( 2 − ( a , a ) 𝒞 ) 1=\frac{1}{2}(2-(a,a)_{\mathscr{C}})  arrows) and therefore, by the local neighbourhood theorem (Theorem  4.2 ), the map  q l q_{l}  is analytically locally around  x x  modeled on the map  𝔸 2 × 𝔑 l → 𝔸 2 \mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathfrak{N}_{l}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{2}  where  𝔑 l \mathfrak{N}_{l}  is the stack of pairs of commuting nilpotent  l × l l\times l  matrices. 
 
 
 For the first statement, we can now run the arguments of the proof of  [ Dav23 , Proposition A.4] . That the analogue in our situation of the morphism  Ψ X \Psi_{X}  of  loc.cit.  is an isomorphism follows from the local neighbourhood theorem and the case of the Jordan quiver (or torsion sheaves on  𝔸 2 \mathbb{A}^{2} , see  [ Dav23 , Proposition A.1]  and the references therein). For the second statement, again we appeal to  [ Dav23 , Proposition A.4] , except in [loc. cit] the  ψ \psi -twist is not considered, or equivalently we consider the  ψ \psi -twist for which  ψ ⁡ ( a , a ) = 0 \psi(a,a)=0 , and not  ψ ′ \psi^{\prime}  satisfying  ψ ′ ​ ( a , a ) = 1 \psi^{\prime}(a,a)=1  (the only nontrivial choice). On the other hand, the commutator for  τ ⩽ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ′ ψ ) \tau^{\leqslant 0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\psi}_{\mathcal{A}})  vanishes if and only if the commutator for  τ ⩽ 0 ​ ( 𝒜 ¯ 𝒜 ′ ψ ′ ) \tau^{\leqslant 0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\psi^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{A}})  vanishes, and the twisted case follows from the untwisted case.
∎ 
 
 
 
 The following two corollaries are direct consequences of Theorem  6.5 . 
 
 
 Corollary 6.6 . 
 
 For any  a ∈ Σ 𝒜 a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  such that  ( a , a ) 𝒞 = 0 (a,a)_{\mathscr{C}}=0  and any  r ⩾ 1 r\geqslant 1 , we have 
 

 
 
 ȷ ~ ∗ ​ ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg , r ​ a ( ψ ) ≅ ( Sym ⊡ ⁡ ( ⨁ l ⩾ 1 ( Δ l ) ∗ ​ 𝐐 ¯ ℳ 𝒜 , a ′ ⊗ 𝐋 − 1 ) ) r ​ a . \widetilde{\jmath}^{*}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg},ra}^{(\psi)}\cong\left(\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\bigoplus_{l\geqslant 1}(\Delta_{l})_{*}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A},a}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{-1}\right)\right)_{ra}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Corollary 6.7 . 
 
 Choose  m ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) m\in\K(\mathcal{A})  such that  m = r ​ m ′ m=rm^{\prime}  with  r ∈ 𝐍 ⩾ 1 r\in\mathbf{N}_{\geqslant 1} ,  ( m ′ , m ′ ) = 0 (m^{\prime},m^{\prime})=0  and  m ′ ∈ Σ 𝒜 m^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Then, we have a canonical monomorphism  ( u m ) ∗ ​ ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 , m ′ ) → ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ( ψ ) (u_{m})_{*}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m^{\prime}})\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{(\psi)} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows from Corollary  6.6  and the fact that  ȷ ~ ! ∗ ȷ ~ ∗ ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg , r ​ m ′ ( ψ ) \widetilde{\jmath}_{!*}\widetilde{\jmath}^{*}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg},rm^{\prime}}^{(\psi)}  is a direct summand of  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg , r ​ m ′ ( ψ ) \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg},rm^{\prime}}^{(\psi)}  by semisimplicity of  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg , r ​ m ( ψ ) \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg},rm}^{(\psi)}  (Lemma  6.1 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 6.4.3.  Generators 

 
 In this section, we define a mixed Hodge module  𝒢 𝒜 \mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{A}}  on  ℳ 𝒜 \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  which will be used for the study of the cohomological Hall algebra of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , eventually playing the role of the Chevalley generators of (half) a generalised Kac–Moody algebra. More precisely,  𝒢 ¯ ≔ ⨁ m ∈ Φ 𝒜 + 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , m \underline{\mathscr{G}}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{m\in\Phi^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},m}  where 
 

 
 
 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , m ≔ { ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 , m )  if  m ∈ Σ 𝒜 ( u m ) ∗ ​ ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( ℳ 𝒜 , m ′ )  if  m = l ​ m ′  with  m ′ ∈ Σ 𝒜  isotropic and  l ⩾ 2   , \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},m}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m})&\text{ if $m\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}$}\\
(u_{m})_{*}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m^{\prime}})&\text{ if $m=lm^{\prime}$ with $m^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}$ isotropic and $l\geqslant 2$ },\end{cases} 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 u m \displaystyle u_{m} 
 ≔ ℳ 𝒜 , m ′ → ℳ 𝒜 , m \displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m} 
 
 
 
 
 x ↦ x ⊕ l \displaystyle x\mapsto x^{\oplus l} 
 
 
 is the direct sum map. We implicitly use that the decomposition  m = l ​ m ′ m=lm^{\prime}  is unique as by Proposition  5.4 ( 3 ),  l l  is the dimension of the stabilizer of a general element of  ℳ 𝒜 , l ​ a \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},la}  and  ( ℳ 𝒜 , a ) red (\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a})_{\mathrm{red}}  can be identified with the small diagonal inside  ℳ 𝒜 , a \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a} , that is the closure of the locus of points having  GL l ​ ( 𝐂 ) \mathrm{GL}_{l}(\mathbf{C})  as stabilizer. 
 
 
 For  m ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) m\in\K(\mathcal{A}) , we let  𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , m ≔ ⨁ m ′ ∈ cl − 1 ​ ( m ) 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , m ′ \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},m}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{m^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}^{-1}(m)}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},m^{\prime}} . We have  𝒢 ¯ = ⨁ m ∈ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , m \underline{\mathscr{G}}=\bigoplus_{m\in\Phi_{\K(\mathcal{A})}^{+}}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},m} . 
 
 
 
 6.4.4.  Primitivity in the preprojective case 

 
 We finish this section by showing that, at least in the case  𝒜 = Rep ⁡ ( Π Q ) \mathcal{A}=\operatorname{Rep}(\Pi_{Q}) , the summands  𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , m \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},m}  of the BPS algebra that we have defined deserve to be called primitive. 
 
 
 Proposition 6.8 . 
 
 Let  Q Q  be a quiver. We let 
 

 
 
 𝒢 ¯ 𝐝 ≔ 𝒢 ¯ Π Q , 𝐝 = { ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( ℳ Π Q , 𝐝 )  if  𝐝 ∈ Σ Π Q ( u 𝐝 ) ∗ ​ ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( ℳ Π Q , δ )  if  𝐝 = l ​ δ  with  l ⩾ 1  and  δ ∈ Σ Π Q  is isotropic. \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathbf{d}}\coloneqq\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}=\begin{cases}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}})&\text{ if $\mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}$}\\
(u_{\mathbf{d}})_{*}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\delta})&\text{ if $\mathbf{d}=l\delta$ with $l\geqslant 1$ and $\delta\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}$ is isotropic.}\end{cases} 
 
 
 Here,  u 𝐝 : ℳ Π Q , δ → ℳ Π Q , l ​ δ u_{\mathbf{d}}\colon\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},l\delta} ;  x ↦ x ⊕ l x\mapsto x^{\oplus l} . We let  𝒢 ¯ = ⨁ 𝐝 ∈ Φ Π Q + 𝒢 ¯ 𝐝 \underline{\mathscr{G}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}^{+}}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathbf{d}} . Then, there exists a canonical decomposition  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg ≅ ℱ ¯ ⊕ 𝒢 ¯ \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}\cong\underline{\mathscr{F}}\oplus\underline{\mathscr{G}}  with  ℱ ¯ ∈ MHM ⁡ ( ℳ Π Q ) \underline{\mathscr{F}}\in\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}})  such that for any  𝐝 , 𝐝 ′ ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 ∖ { 0 } \mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\smallsetminus\{0\} , the multiplication 
 

 
 
 𝔪 ψ : ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 ψ ⊡ ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 ′ ψ → ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 + 𝐝 ′ ψ \mathfrak{m}^{\psi}\colon\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg},\mathbf{d}}^{\psi}\boxdot\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}^{\psi}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg},\mathbf{d}+\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}^{\psi} 
 
 
 factors through the natural inclusion of  ℱ ¯ \underline{\mathscr{F}} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 If  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 ∖ Φ Π Q + \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\smallsetminus\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}^{+} , we set  𝒢 ¯ 𝐝 = 0 \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathbf{d}}=0 , and  ℱ ¯ 𝐝 = ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 ( ψ ) \underline{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{d}}=\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg},\mathbf{d}}^{(\psi)} . 
 
 
 If  𝐝 ∈ Σ Π Q \mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} , by Lemma  6.4 ,  ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( ℳ Π Q , 𝐝 ) \underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}})  is a direct summand appearing with multiplicity one in the mixed Hodge module  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 ( ψ ) \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg},\mathbf{d}}^{(\psi)} . Therefore, it has a canonical complement  ℱ ¯ 𝐝 \underline{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{d}} . 
 
 
 If  𝐝 = l ​ δ \mathbf{d}=l\delta  with  l ⩾ 1 l\geqslant 1  and  δ ∈ Σ Π Q \delta\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}  isotropic, then the support of  δ \delta  is an affine subquiver of  Q Q . By the description of the BPS sheaf for affine quivers  [ Dav20 , §7.2] , and the relative cohomological integrality theorem, we have a canonical isomorphism of mixed Hodge modules  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 ( ψ ) = ( Sym ⁡ ( ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Lie ) ) 𝐝 \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg},\mathbf{d}}^{(\psi)}=(\Sym(\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}))_{\mathbf{d}} . It follows from Theorem  6.5  that  ( u 𝐝 ) ∗ ​ ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ​ ( ℳ Π Q , δ ) (u_{\mathbf{d}})_{*}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\delta})  is the unique direct summand of  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 ( ψ ) \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg},\mathbf{d}}^{(\psi)}  supported on the small diagonal. This proves that it has a canonically defined complement  ℱ ¯ 𝐝 ⊆ ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 ( ψ ) \underline{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{d}}\subseteq\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg},\mathbf{d}}^{(\psi)} . 
 
 
 By the same arguments as in  [ Dav20 , Theorem 7.1]  for hyperbolic positive roots and  [ Dav20 , §7.2]  for  Q Q  affine (which amount to support considerations), the multiplication map 
 

 
 
 𝔪 ψ : ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 ψ ⊡ ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 ′ ψ → ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 + 𝐝 ′ ψ \mathfrak{m}^{\psi}\colon\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg},\mathbf{d}}^{\psi}\boxdot\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}^{\psi}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg},\mathbf{d}+\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}^{\psi} 
 
 
 factors through the inclusion of  ℱ ¯ 𝐝 + 𝐝 ′ \underline{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{d}+\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}  for any pair of nonzero  𝐝 , 𝐝 ′ ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 ∖ { 0 } \mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\smallsetminus\{0\} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 7.  The BPS (Lie) algebra for 2-Calabi–Yau categories – Proof of Theorem  1.1 

 
 We fix a bilinear form  ψ \psi  as in § 6.3.1  and we denote by the same letter  ψ \psi  the induced bilinear form on  𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  if  Q = ( Q 0 , Q 1 ) Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})  is an Ext-quiver of a collection of simple objects of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . 
 
 
 7.1.  Local neighbourhoods and  Σ \Sigma -collections 

 
 Let  ℱ ¯ = { ℱ 1 , … , ℱ r } \underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}  be a  Σ \Sigma -collection in  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , satisfying the assumptions of Theorem  4.2 . Define the classes  a i = [ ℱ i ] ∈ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) a_{i}=[\mathcal{F}_{i}]\in\K(\mathcal{A})  and let  x i ∈ ℳ 𝒜 , a i x_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a_{i}}  be the  𝐂 \mathbf{C} -point corresponding to  ℱ i \mathcal{F}_{i} . The inclusions  x i ↪ ℳ 𝒜 x_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  induce a monoid morphism  ı ℱ ¯ : 𝐍 ℱ ¯ ↪ ℳ 𝒜 \imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  sending  1 ℱ i ↦ x i 1_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}\mapsto x_{i}  (see § 4.6.1 ). 
 
 
 Let  Q Q  be half of the Ext-quiver of the collection  ℱ ¯ \underline{\mathcal{F}} .
The inclusions  { S i } ↪ ℳ Π Q , 1 i \{S_{i}\}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},1_{i}}  of the simple nilpotent representations of  Π Q \Pi_{Q}  induce a monoid morphism  ı 𝒩 : 𝐍 ℱ ¯ ↪ ℳ Π Q \imath_{\mathcal{N}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}} .
By  [ DHS22 , Lemma 5.2] ,  ı 𝒩 ! \imath_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}  and  ı ℱ ¯ ! \imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}  are strictly monoidal. 
 
 
 For  𝐦 ∈ 𝐍 ℱ ¯ \mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  we write  a 𝐦 ≔ ∑ i m i ​ a i a_{\mathbf{m}}\coloneqq\sum_{i}m_{i}a_{i} . Pick for every  𝐦 ∈ 𝐍 ℱ ¯ \mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  an analytic Ext-quiver neighbourhood
 𝒰 𝐦 \mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{m}}  of the point  x 𝐦 ∈ ℳ 𝒜 , a 𝐦 x_{\mathbf{m}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a_{\mathbf{m}}}  as in Theorem  4.2 ,
corresponding to the semisimple object  ⨁ i ℱ i ⊕ m i \bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}}  of
class  a 𝐦 a_{\mathbf{m}} . Set  𝒰 = ⨆ 𝐦 ∈ 𝐍 I 𝒰 𝐦 \mathcal{U}=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{N}^{I}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{m}} .
By Theorem  4.2  we have a commutative diagram of analytic spaces 
 

 
 (7.1) 
 
 𝐍 ℱ ¯ {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}} ℳ Π Q {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}} 𝒰 {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}} ℳ 𝒜 . {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}.} ı 𝒩 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath_{\mathcal{N}}} y \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y} ı ℱ ¯ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}} ȷ 𝒩 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath_{\mathcal{N}}} ȷ ℱ ¯ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}} 
 
 
 The horizontal morphisms  ȷ 𝒩 , ȷ ℱ ¯ \jmath_{\mathcal{N}},\jmath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  are analytic-open embeddings such that  ȷ 𝒩 ​ ( 𝒰 ) \jmath_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{U})  (resp.  ȷ ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝒰 ) \jmath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathcal{U}) ) is an analytic neighbourhood of  ı 𝒩 ​ ( 𝐍 ℱ ¯ ) \imath_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}})  (resp.  ı ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝐍 ℱ ¯ ) \imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}) ). 
 
 
 
 7.2.  Restriction of the BPS algebra sheaf 

 
 Lemma 7.1 . 
 
 If  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg ( ψ ) \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}^{(\psi)}  denotes the BPS algebra sheaf on  ℳ Π Q \mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}  and  ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ( ψ ) \underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{(\psi)}  the BPS algebra sheaf on  ℳ 𝒜 \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}} , we have a canonical isomorphism  ι 𝒩 !  Proof. 
 
 With notation as in ( 7.1 ) we have isomorphisms 
 

 
 
 ı 𝒩 ! ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 ψ ≅ y ! ȷ 𝒩 ! ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg , 𝐝 ψ ≅ y ! ℋ 0 ( ( p 𝐝 ) ∗ 𝔻 𝐐 ¯ 𝔘 vir ) ≅ y ! ȷ ℱ ¯ ! ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg , a 𝐝 ψ ≅ ı ℱ ¯ !  with  p 𝐝 p_{\mathbf{d}}  as in Theorem  4.2  and since cohomology functors commute with open immersions.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 7.3.  Compatibility of generators with local neighbourhoods 

 
 Recall the definition of  𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , m \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},m}  for  m ∈ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + m\in\Phi^{+}_{\K(\mathcal{A})}  and  𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A}}  in § 6.4.3 . We consider 
 

 
 
 Free ⊡ -Alg ⁡ ( 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 ) ≔ Free ⊡ -Alg ⁡ ( ⨁ m ∈ Φ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) + 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , m ) . \operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot\sAlg}(\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A}})\coloneqq\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot\sAlg}\left(\bigoplus_{m\in\Phi^{+}_{\K(\mathcal{A})}}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},m}\right). 
 
 
 We let  ℐ 𝒜 , 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg \mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A},\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathrm{Alg}}  be the Serre ideal, as defined in § 3.5.2 , for the monoid  ℳ 𝒜 \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  and the mixed Hodge modules  𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , m \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},m} . 
 
 
 We let  ı ℱ ¯ : 𝐍 ℱ ¯ → ℳ 𝒜 \imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}  and  ı 𝒩 : 𝐍 ℱ ¯ → ℳ Π Q ℱ ¯ \imath_{\mathcal{N}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}}  be as in § 7.1 . 
 
 
 Lemma 7.2 . 
 
 Let  𝐦 ∈ Φ Π Q + , iso \mathbf{m}\in\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}^{+,\mathrm{iso}} . We let  𝐦 = l ​ 𝐦 ′ \mathbf{m}=l\mathbf{m^{\prime}}  with  𝐦 ′ ∈ Σ Π Q \mathbf{m^{\prime}}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} . Then  λ ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝐦 ) = l ​ λ ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝐦 ′ ) \lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbf{m})=l\lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbf{m^{\prime}})  is the decomposition of  λ ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝐦 ) \lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbf{m})  of the form  l ′ ​ a l^{\prime}a  with  a ∈ Σ 𝒜 a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Moreover, for  m ∈ Φ 𝒜 + , iso m\in\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{+,\mathrm{iso}} ,  m = l ​ m ′ m=lm^{\prime}  with  l l  maximal, the map  u m u_{m}  is a closed immersion whose image is the closure of the subset of  ℳ 𝒜 , m \mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},m}  of semisimple objects whose stabilizer is isomorphic to  GL l ​ ( 𝐂 ) \mathrm{GL}_{l}(\mathbf{C}) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The first assertion, regarding the decomposition of  λ ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝐦 ) \lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbf{m}) , comes from Proposition  5.4 . By properness of  ⊕ \oplus , the image of the morphism  u m u_{m}  from § 6.4.3  is closed. The second statement is true for  𝒜 = Rep ⁡ Π Q \mathcal{A}=\operatorname{Rep}\Pi_{Q}  by  [ Cra02 , Theorem 3.4] . Then the local neighbourhood theorem (Theorem  4.2 ) implies that for any  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , the closure of the set of points with stabiliser  GL l ​ ( 𝐂 ) \mathrm{GL}_{l}(\mathbf{C})  is irreducible and  2 2 -dimensional. The second claim follows.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 7.3 . 
 
 Let  𝐝 ∈ Σ Π Q \mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}  be isotropic,  ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) = 0 (\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})=0 . Then, 
 

 
 
 ı 𝒩 ! 𝒢 ¯ Π Q , l ​ 𝐝 ≅ ι ℱ ¯ ! 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , l ​ λ ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝐝 ) . \imath_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\Pi_{Q},l\mathbf{d}}\cong\iota_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},l\lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbf{d})}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This is a consequence of Lemma  7.2 . We let  𝒰 Δ ⊆ 𝒰 l ​ 𝐝 \mathcal{U}_{\Delta}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{l\mathbf{d}}  be the closure of the locus of closed points having  GL l ​ ( 𝐂 ) \mathrm{GL}_{l}(\mathbf{C})  as stabilizer. In diagram ( 7.1 ), we have 
 

 
 
 ȷ 𝒩 ! 𝒢 ¯ Π Q , l ​ 𝐝 ≅ ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ( 𝒰 Δ ) ≅ ȷ ℱ ¯ ! 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , l ​ λ ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝐝 ) \jmath_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\Pi_{Q},l\mathbf{d}}\cong\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Delta})\cong\jmath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},l\lambda_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbf{d})} 
 
 
 and so by applying  y ! y^{!} , we obtain the lemma.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proposition 7.4 . 
 
 We have a canonical isomorphism of algebras 
 

 
 
 ι ℱ ¯ ! Free ⊡ -Alg ( 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 ) ≅ ι 𝒩 ! Free ⊡ -Alg ( 𝒢 ¯ Π Q ) \iota_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot\sAlg}(\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A}})\cong\iota_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot\sAlg}(\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\Pi_{Q}}) 
 
 
 in  𝒟 + ​ ( MHM ⁡ ( 𝐍 ℱ ¯ ) ) \mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}})) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This comes from the natural isomorphism of  𝐍 ℱ ¯ \mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}} -graded vector spaces 
 

 
 
 ( ı 𝒩 ) ! ( ⨁ 𝐦 ∈ Φ Π Q + ⊔ Φ Π Q + , hyp ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ( ℳ Π Q , 𝐦 ) ) ≅ ı ℱ ¯ ! ( ⨁ a ∈ Φ 𝒜 + , ⊔ Φ 𝒜 + , hyp ℐ ​ 𝒞 ¯ ( ℳ 𝒜 , a ) ) (\imath_{\mathcal{N}})^{!}\left(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{m}\in\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}^{+\real}\sqcup\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}^{+,\mathrm{hyp}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}})\right)\cong\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\left(\bigoplus_{a\in\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{+,\real}\sqcup\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{+,\mathrm{hyp}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a})\right) 
 
 
 obtained using Proposition  5.3  and the diagram ( 7.1 ), the isomorphism of  𝐍 ℱ ¯ \mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}} -graded vector spaces 
 

 
 
 ( ı 𝒩 ) ! ( ⨁ 𝐦 ∈ Φ Π Q + iso 𝒢 ¯ Π Q ​ 𝐦 ) ≅ ı ℱ ¯ ! ( ⨁ a ∈ Φ 𝒜 + , iso 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , a ) (\imath_{\mathcal{N}})^{!}\left(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{m}\in\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}^{+\mathrm{iso}}}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\Pi_{Q}\mathbf{m}}\right)\cong\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\left(\bigoplus_{a\in\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{+,\mathrm{iso}}}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},a}\right) 
 
 
 obtained using Lemma  7.3  and diagram ( 7.1 ), and the fact that  ı ! ℱ ¯ \imath^{!}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}  and  ı ! 𝒩 \imath^{!}_{\mathcal{N}}  are strictly monoidal functors (§ 6.1 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 7.4.  Compatibility of Serre relations with local neighbourhoods 

 
 Recall the ideals of Serre relations for  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  and  Π Q \Pi_{Q} ,  ℐ 𝒜 , 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg \mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A},\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathrm{Alg}}  and  ℐ Π Q , 𝒢 ¯ Π Q , Alg \mathcal{I}_{\Pi_{Q},\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\Pi_{Q}},\mathrm{Alg}} , defined in § 3.5 . We shorten the notation:  ℐ ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ≔ ℐ 𝒜 , 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg \underline{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}\coloneqq\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A},\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathrm{Alg}}  and  ℐ ¯ Π Q , Alg ≔ ℐ Π Q , 𝒢 ¯ Π Q , Alg \underline{\mathcal{I}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}\coloneqq\mathcal{I}_{\Pi_{Q},\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\Pi_{Q}},\mathrm{Alg}} . Likewise, we shorten  𝔫 ¯ 𝒜 + ≔ 𝔫 ℳ 𝒜 , 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 + \underline{\mathfrak{n}}^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}},\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A}}}  and  𝔫 ¯ Π Q + ≔ 𝔫 ℳ Π Q , 𝒢 ¯ Π Q + \underline{\mathfrak{n}}^{+}_{\Pi_{Q}}\coloneqq\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}},\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\Pi_{Q}}} . 
 
 
 Proposition 7.5 . 
 
 We have a canonical isomorphism 
 

 
 
 ι ℱ ¯ ! ℐ ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ≅ ι 𝒩 ! ℐ ¯ Π Q , Alg . \iota_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}\cong\iota_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{I}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 ι ! ℱ ¯ ℐ ¯ 𝒜 , Alg \iota^{!}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\underline{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}  is the ideal of  Free ⊡ -Alg ⁡ ( 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 ) \operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot\sAlg}(\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A}})  generated by Serre relations relative to  ι ! ℱ ¯ 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 , m \iota^{!}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A},m} ,  m ∈ Φ 𝒜 + m\in\Phi^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}  while  ι 𝒩 ! ℐ ¯ Π Q ℱ ¯ , Alg \iota_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{I}}_{\Pi_{Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}},\mathrm{Alg}}  is the ideal of  Free ⊡ -Alg ⁡ ( 𝒢 ¯ Π Q ) \operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot\sAlg}(\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\Pi_{Q}})  generated by Serre relations relative to  ι 𝒩 ! 𝒢 ¯ Π Q , 𝐝 \iota_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}} ,  𝐝 ∈ Φ Π Q + \mathbf{d}\in\Phi^{+}_{\Pi_{Q}} . By Lemma  5.2 , Proposition  5.3  and Proposition  7.4 , for  𝐝 , 𝐞 ∈ Φ Π Q + \mathbf{d},\mathbf{e}\in\Phi^{+}_{\Pi_{Q}} , we have  ι 𝒩 ! ℛ ¯ 𝐝 , 𝐞 ≅ ι ! ℱ ¯ ℛ ¯ ψ ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝐝 ) , ψ ℱ ¯ ​ ( 𝐞 ) \iota_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\underline{\mathscr{R}}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{e}}\cong\iota^{!}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\underline{\mathscr{R}}_{\psi_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathbf{d})},\psi_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbf{e})}  (with notation as in § 3.5.2 ). This proves the lemma.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 7.6 . 
 
 We have a canonical isomorphism 
 

 
 
 ι ℱ ¯ ! 𝐔 ( 𝔫 ¯ 𝒜 + ) ≅ ι 𝒩 ! 𝐔 ( 𝔫 ¯ Π Q + ) . \iota_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\UEA(\underline{\mathfrak{n}}^{+}_{\mathcal{A}})\cong\iota_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\UEA(\underline{\mathfrak{n}}^{+}_{\Pi_{Q}}). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This is a straightforward combination of Propositions  7.4  and  7.5 , by taking quotients of free algebras by their respective Serre ideals.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We let  Υ 𝒜 : Free ⊡ -Alg ⁡ ( 𝒢 ¯ 𝒜 ) → ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ψ \Upsilon_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot\sAlg}(\underline{\mathscr{G}}_{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}  be the morphism of algebra objects in  MHM ⁡ ( ℳ 𝒜 ) \mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})  and  MHM ⁡ ( ℳ Π Q ) \mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}})  extending the morphisms  𝒢 ¯ m → ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ 𝒜 , Alg ψ \underline{\mathscr{G}}_{m}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}  ( m ∈ Φ 𝒜 + m\in\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{+} ) given in Lemma  6.4  (for real and hyperbolic simple positive roots) and Corollary  6.7  (for isotropic simple positive roots).
