(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})=0\\
l\geqslant 2\end{subarray}}\mathrm{IP}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}},q)z^{l\mathbf{d}}, 
 
 
 the intersection Poincaré polynomials of  ℳ Π Q , 𝐝 \mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}  for various  𝐝 \mathbf{d} . 
 
 
 We finally come to the main combinatorial consequence of Theorem  1.2 : 
 
 
 Theorem 9.5 . 
 
 Conjecture  9.3  is true: for any  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} ,  C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( q ) ∈ 𝐍 ⁡ [ q ] C^{\mathrm{abs}}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)\in\mathbf{N}[q] . Moreover, for any  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} ,  𝐝 ∈ Σ Q \mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{Q} , 
 

 
 
 C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( q − 2 ) = IP ⁡ ( ℳ Π Q , 𝐝 , q ) . C^{\mathrm{abs}}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q^{-2})=\mathrm{IP}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}},q). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This comes from the comparison of Equations ( 9.4 ) and ( 9.6 ), given that the character of the GKM Lie algebra  𝔫 Π Q BPS , + \mathfrak{n}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\aBPS,+}  is given by Equation ( 9.5 ), which is ( 9.3 ) up to the change of variables  q ↔ q − 2 q\leftrightarrow q^{-2} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 9.6 . 
 
 
 (1)  
 
 C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( q ) ≠ 0 C^{\mathrm{abs}}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)\neq 0  if and only if  𝐝 ∈ Φ Π Q + \mathbf{d}\in\Phi^{+}_{\Pi_{Q}} , 
 
 (2)  
 
 If  𝐝 ∈ Φ Π Q + \mathbf{d}\in\Phi^{+}_{\Pi_{Q}} ,  deg ⁡ C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( q ) = 1 − χ Q ​ ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) \deg C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{abs}}(q)=1-\chi_{Q}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})  and  C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( q ) C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{abs}}(q)  is a monic polynomial. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The first statement follows immediately from Theorem  9.5 . The second statement follows from the fact that for  𝐝 ∈ Σ Π Q \mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} , the singular Nakajima quiver variety  ℳ Π Q , 𝐝 \mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}  is irreducible of dimension  2 − ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) Q 2-(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{Q}  by  [ Cra01 ]  and for  𝐝 ∈ Σ Π Q \mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} ,  ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) Q = 0 (\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})_{Q}=0  and  l ⩾ 1 l\geqslant 1 ,  ℳ Π Q , l ​ 𝐝 \mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},l\mathbf{d}}  is irreducible of dimension  2 ​ l 2l  by  [ Cra02 ] .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 9.2.  Intersection Poincaré polynomials of singular quiver varieties 

 
 The canonical decomposition of a dimension vector  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  is the unique decomposition of  𝐝 \mathbf{d}  as a sum of primitive positive roots (elements of  Σ Π Q \Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} ) such that any other decomposition as a sum of primitive positive roots is a refinement of it. By  [ Cra02 ] , it is well-defined and unique. Moreover, it gives a product decomposition of the corresponding quiver variety  [ Cra02 ] . 
 
 
 Corollary 9.7 . 
 
 Let  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  be a dimension vector and  𝐝 = ∑ j = 1 r m j ​ 𝐝 j \mathbf{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}m_{j}\mathbf{d}_{j}  be the canonical decomposition of  𝐝 \mathbf{d} , where the  𝐝 j \mathbf{d}_{j}  are pairwise distinct,  𝐝 j ∈ Σ Π Q \mathbf{d}_{j}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}  and  m j ⩾ 0 m_{j}\geqslant 0 . We have 
 

 
 
 IP ⁡ ( ℳ Π Q , 𝐝 ) ​ ( t ) = ∏ j = 1 r 1 m j ! ​ ( ( d d ​ u ) m j ​ Exp t , u ⁡ ( C Q , 𝐝 j abs ​ ( t − 2 ) ​ u ) ) u = 0 \mathrm{IP}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}})(t)=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\frac{1}{m_{j}!}\left(\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}u}\right)^{m_{j}}\Exp_{t,u}(C_{Q,\mathbf{d}_{j}}^{\mathrm{abs}}(t^{-2})u)\right)_{u=0} 
 
 
 where  u u  is an additional formal variable and  Exp t , u \Exp_{t,u}  the plethystic exponential. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows from the product decomposition of quiver varieties  [ Cra02 , Theorem 1.1]  and the formula for intersection Poincaré polynomials of symmetric products.
∎ 
 
 
 
 The canonical decomposition of a dimension vector  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  can be determined algorithmically. Indeed, for a given  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} , it is possible to determine the set of primitive positive roots  𝐝 ′ ⩽ 𝐝 \mathbf{d}^{\prime}\leqslant\mathbf{d}  (this amounts to checking a certain finite number of inequalities for all decompositions of  𝐝 ′ \mathbf{d}^{\prime} ). Then, Crawley-Boevey proves that the canonical decomposition is such that any other decomposition of  𝐝 \mathbf{d}  as a sum of elements of  Σ Π Q \Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}  refines it. Therefore, we can start from the trivial decomposition  𝐝 = ∑ j = 1 r 𝐝 j ≔ ∑ i ∈ Q 0 d i ​ 1 i \mathbf{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\mathbf{d}_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}d_{i}1_{i}  where each  𝐝 j \mathbf{d}_{j}  is equal to  1 i 1_{i}  for some vertex  i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} . We can then iteratively combine some of the dimension vectors  𝐝 j \mathbf{d}_{j}  when their sum is in  Σ Π Q \Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}} . In conclusion, intersection Poincaré polynomials of all quiver varieties can be algorithmically computed using our results. 
 
 
 
 9.3.  (Semi)-nilpotent Kac and cuspidal polynomials 

 
 Bozec, Schiffmann and Vasserot defined in  [ BSV20 ]  various families of counting polynomials for quiver representations over finite fields, obtained by imposing appropriate nilpotency conditions. They related the polynomials obtained this way to the count of points of some quiver varieties. In this section, we recall these definitions and we define the corresponding  cuspidal  and  absolutely cuspidal  polynomials, following  [ BS19 ] . We use the general results from this paper to prove the positivity for these polynomials. 
 
 
 9.3.1.  Nilpotent and semi-nilpotent representations of quivers 

 
 We recall various notions of nilpotency for quiver representations from  [ BSV20 ] . Let  Q = ( Q 0 , Q 1 ) Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})  be a quiver. A representation  M M  of  Q Q  is called 
 
 (1)  
 
 nilpotent  if there exists a flag of  Q 0 Q_{0} -graded vector spaces  { 0 } = M 0 ⊆ M 1 ⊆ … ⊆ M r = M \{0\}=M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq M_{r}=M  such that the action of arrows of  Q Q  sends  M i M_{i}  to  M i − 1 M_{i-1} ; 
 
 (2)  
 
 1 1 -nilpotent  if there exists a flag of  Q 0 Q_{0} -graded vector spaces  { 0 } = M 0 ⊆ M 1 ⊆ … ⊆ M r = M \{0\}=M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq M_{r}=M  such that loops of  Q Q  send  M i M_{i}  to  M i − 1 M_{i-1}  (no conditions for other arrows). 
 
 
 
 
 Let  Q ¯ = ( Q 0 , Q ¯ 1 ) \overline{Q}=(Q_{0},\overline{Q}_{1})  be the double of the quiver  Q Q . We have  Q ¯ 1 = Q 1 ⊔ Q 1 ∗ \overline{Q}_{1}=Q_{1}\sqcup Q_{1}^{*} , and each arrow  α ∈ Q 1 \alpha\in Q_{1}  has an opposite arrow  α ∗ ∈ Q 1 ∗ \alpha^{*}\in Q_{1}^{*} . A representation  M M  of  Q ¯ \overline{Q}  is called 
 
 (1)  
 
 semi-nilpotent  if there exists a flag of  Q 0 Q_{0} -graded vector spaces  { 0 } = M 0 ⊆ M 1 ⊆ … ⊆ M r = M \{0\}=M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq M_{r}=M  such that arrows  α ∈ Q 1 \alpha\in Q_{1}  send  M i M_{i}  to  M i − 1 M_{i-1}  and arrows  α ∈ Q 1 ∗ \alpha\in Q_{1}^{*}  send  M i M_{i}  to  M i M_{i} . 
 
 (2)  
 
 strictly semi-nilpotent  if there exists a flag of  Q 0 Q_{0} -graded vector spaces  { 0 } = M 0 ⊆ M 1 ⊆ … ⊆ M r = M \{0\}=M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq M_{r}=M  such that consecutive subquotients are supported at a single vertex, each arrow  α ∈ Q 1 \alpha\in Q_{1}  sends  M i M_{i}  to  M i − 1 M_{i-1}  and arrows  α ∈ Q 1 ∗ \alpha\in Q_{1}^{*}  send  M i M_{i}  to  M i M_{i} . 
 
 (3)  
 
 ∗ * -semi-nilpotent if the condition semi-nilpotent ( 1 ) is satisfied with  Q 1 Q_{1}  and  Q 1 ∗ Q_{1}^{*}  exchanged. 
 
 (4)  
 
 ∗ * -strictly semi-nilpotent if the condition strictly semi-nilpotent ( 2 ) with  Q 1 Q_{1}  and  Q 1 ∗ Q_{1}^{*}  exchanged is satisfied. 
 
 
 Since the involution of  Π Q \Pi_{Q}  swapping each arrow  a a  with  a ∗ a^{*}  swaps (3) with (1) and (4) with (2), the BPS cohomology for the  ∗ * -versions is isomorphic to the non- ∗ * -versions, which we concentrate on exclusively from now on. 
 
 
 
 9.3.2.  Hall algebras 

 
 Recall from § 9.1  that we have the Hall algebra of  Q Q  over a finite field  𝐅 q \mathbf{F}_{q} ,  H Q , 𝐅 q H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}} , which is a bialgebra.
We define similarly 
 

 
 
 H Q , 𝐅 q nil ≔ ⨁ [ M ] ∈ Rep Q ( 𝐅 q ) ) / ∼ M ​  is nilpotent 𝐂 ⁡ [ M ] ; H Q , 𝐅 q 1 ​ -nil ≔ ⨁ [ M ] ∈ Rep Q ( 𝐅 q ) ) / ∼ M ​  is  1 -nilpotent 𝐂 ⁡ [ M ] . H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}[M]\in\operatorname{Rep}_{Q}(\mathbf{F}_{q}))/\sim\\
M\text{ is nilpotent}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{C}[M];\quad\quad H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{1\snil}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}[M]\in\operatorname{Rep}_{Q}(\mathbf{F}_{q}))/\sim\\
M\text{ is $1$-nilpotent}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{C}[M]. 
 
 
 Since the categories  Rep Q nil ⁡ ( 𝐅 q ) \operatorname{Rep}_{Q}^{\mathrm{nil}}(\mathbf{F}_{q})  and  Rep Q 1 ​ -nil ⁡ ( 𝐅 q ) \operatorname{Rep}_{Q}^{1\snil}(\mathbf{F}_{q})  of nilpotent and  1 1 -nilpotent representations of  Q Q  are  Serre subcategories  (i.e. stable under extension, subobjects and quotients),  H Q , 𝐅 nil ⊆ H Q , 𝐅 q 1 ​ -nil ⊆ H Q , 𝐅 q H_{Q,\mathbf{F}}^{\mathrm{nil}}\subseteq H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{1\snil}\subseteq H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}  are inclusions of biagebras. 
 
 
 
 9.3.3.  Kac polynomials 

 
 In  [ BSV20 ]  Bozec, Schiffmann and Vasserot defined and studied Kac polynomials for the categories  Rep Q nil ⁡ ( 𝐅 q ) \operatorname{Rep}_{Q}^{\mathrm{nil}}(\mathbf{F}_{q})  and  Rep Q 1 ​ -nil ⁡ ( 𝐅 q ) \operatorname{Rep}_{Q}^{1\snil}(\mathbf{F}_{q})  (in particular by relating them to point counts of Nakajima quiver varieties). Namely, one may define as in § 9.1  the counting functions  M Q , 𝐝 nil ​ ( q ) M_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{nil}}(q) ,  I Q , 𝐝 nil ​ ( q ) I_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{nil}}(q)  and  A Q , 𝐝 nil ​ ( q ) A_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{nil}}(q) ,  M Q , 𝐝 1 ​ -nil ​ ( q ) M_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{1\snil}(q) ,  I Q , 𝐝 1 ​ -nil ​ ( q ) I_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{1\snil}(q)  and  A Q , 𝐝 1 ​ -nil ​ ( q ) A_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{1\snil}(q)  for any  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  by considering respectively nilpotent and  1 1 -nilpotent representations of  Q Q , and all, or indecomposable, or absolutely indecomposable isomorphism classes of such representations. 
 
 
 In both the cases of nilpotent and of  1 1 -nilpotent representations, we have the sequence of equalities analogous to ( 9.1 ) and by  [ BSV20 ] , all these counting functions are polynomials in  q q . The analogue of the Kac positivity conjecture was proved in  [ Dav20 ] : 
 
 
 Proposition 9.8   ( [ Dav20 , §4.2] ) . 
 
 The nilpotent versions of Kac polynomials  A Q , 𝐝 nil ​ ( q ) A_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{nil}}(q)  and  A Q , 𝐝 1 ​ -nil ​ ( q ) A_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{1\snil}(q)  have nonnegative coefficients. 
 
 
 
 
 9.3.4.  Cuspidal polynomials 

 
 We define cuspidal functions in  H Q , 𝐅 q nil H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}  and  H Q , 𝐅 q 1 ​ -nil H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{1\snil}  as 
 

 
 
 H Q , 𝐅 q nil , cusp = H Q , 𝐅 q nil ∩ H Q , 𝐅 q cusp , H Q , 𝐅 q 1 ​ -nil , cusp = H Q , 𝐅 q 1 ​ -nil ∩ H Q , 𝐅 q cusp . H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{\mathrm{nil},\mathrm{cusp}}=H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}\cap H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{\mathrm{cusp}},\quad H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{1\snil,\mathrm{cusp}}=H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{1\snil}\cap H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{\mathrm{cusp}}. 
 
 
 We define the functions  C Q , 𝐝 nil ​ ( q ) C^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)  and  C Q , 𝐝 1 ​ -nil ​ ( q ) C^{1\snil}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q) ,  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  as 
 

 
 
 C Q , 𝐝 nil ​ ( q ) ≔ dim 𝐂 H Q , 𝐅 q nil , cusp , C Q , 𝐝 1 ​ -nil ​ ( q ) ≔ dim 𝐂 H Q , 𝐅 q 1 ​ -nil , cusp . C^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)\coloneqq\dim_{\mathbf{C}}H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{\mathrm{nil},\mathrm{cusp}},\quad C^{1\snil}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)\coloneqq\dim_{\mathbf{C}}H_{Q,\mathbf{F}_{q}}^{1\snil,\mathrm{cusp}}. 
 
 
 The inductive description of cuspidal functions  C Q , 𝐝 C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  in  [ BS19 ]  works without modification for nilpotent and  1 1 -nilpotent representations and this together with the polynomiality result for the numbers of isoclasses of nilpotent and  1 1 -nilpotent representations provides the polynomiality of the nilpotent versions of the functions  C Q , 𝐝 nil C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{nil}}  and  C Q , 𝐝 1 ​ -nil C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{1\snil} . One of the key points is the validity of the Sevenhant–Van den Bergh theorem  [ SV01 , Theorem 1.1]  describing the Hall algebra of a quiver as a quantum group for quivers with loops and the categories of representations with or without nilpotency conditions. Sevenhant and Van den Bergh assume that the quiver under consideration is loop-free and do not consider the case of nilpotent representations, but their methods adapt in a straightforward way. 
 
 
 We define  absolutely cuspidal polynomials  by the same combinatorial procedure as in  [ BS19 ] . That is, for  □ ∈ { nil , 1 ​ -nil } \square\in\{\mathrm{nil},1\snil\} , we define  C Q , 𝐝 □ , abs ​ ( q ) C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\square,\mathrm{abs}}(q)  as 
 

 
 
 
 C Q , 𝐝 □ , abs ​ ( q ) = C Q , 𝐝 □ ​ ( q ) \displaystyle C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\square,\mathrm{abs}}(q)=C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\square}(q) 
 if  χ Q ​ ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) < 0 \displaystyle\text{ if $\chi_{Q}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})<0$}

 if  𝐝 ∈ ( 𝐍 Q 0 ) prim ,  χ Q ​ ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ) = 0 . \displaystyle\text{ if $\mathbf{d}\in(\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}})_{\prim}$, \;$\chi_{Q}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d})=0$}. 
 
 
 Using Proposition  9.4  and the explicit description of isotropic cuspidal functions in  [ Hen21 , Theorems 5.2 and 5.4] , the following is easily obtained.

 9.3.5.  The (strictly) semi-nilpotent BPS Lie algebras 

 
 We let  ℳ Π Q 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 ⊆ ℳ Π Q \mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SN}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}  be the closed submonoid parametrising semisimple representations of  Π Q \Pi_{Q}  such that the only arrows of  Q ¯ \overline{Q}  that may act nontrivially are the arrows  α ∈ Q 1 ∗ \alpha\in Q_{1}^{*}  (i.e. semisimple semi-nilpotent representations of  Π Q \Pi_{Q} ). We let  ℳ Π Q 𝒮 ​ 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 ⊆ ℳ Π Q 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 \mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SN}}  be the submonoid parametrising semisimple representations  M M  of  Π Q \Pi_{Q}  such that the only arrows of  Q ¯ \overline{Q}  that may act nontrivially are the  loops   α ∈ Q 1 ∗ \alpha\in Q_{1}^{*}  (i.e. semisimple strictly semi-nilpotent representations). 
 
 
 For  △ ∈ { 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 , 𝒮 ​ 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 } \triangle\in\{\mathcal{SN},\mathcal{SSN}\} , the stacks of  △ \triangle  representations of  Π Q \Pi_{Q}  are defined by the Cartesian squares

 (9.7)

 Remark 9.11 .

 Theorem 9.12 . 
 
 For  □ ∈ { nil , 1 ​ -nil } \square\in\{\mathrm{nil},1\snil\}  and any  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}} , we have  C Q , 𝐝 □ , abs ​ ( q ) ∈ 𝐍 ⁡ [ q ] C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\square,\mathrm{abs}}(q)\in\mathbf{N}[q] .  If  𝐝 ∈ Σ Π Q \mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}  is isotropic and  l ∈ 𝐍 > 0 l\in\mathbf{N}_{>0} , then 
 

 
 
 C Q , l ​ 𝐝 nil , abs ​ ( q ) = C Q , 𝐝 nil , abs ​ ( q ) , C Q , l ​ 𝐝 1 ​ -nil , abs ​ ( q ) = C Q , 𝐝 1 ​ -nil , abs ​ ( q ) . C_{Q,l\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{nil},\mathrm{abs}}(q)=C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{nil},\mathrm{abs}}(q),\quad C_{Q,l\mathbf{d}}^{1\snil,\mathrm{abs}}(q)=C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{1\snil,\mathrm{abs}}(q). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By Proposition  9.10  and Equations ( 9.7 ), it suffices to prove that  𝔫 Π Q 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 , + \mathfrak{n}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SN},+}  and  𝔫 Π Q 𝒮 ​ 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 , + \mathfrak{n}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SSN},+}  are positive halves of generalised Kac–Moody Lie algebra associated with the monoid  𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  with the symmetrised Euler form and with cohomologically graded spaces of roots given by  H ∗ ı △ , 𝐝 ! 𝒢 Π Q , 𝐝 \HO^{*}\!\!\imath_{\triangle,\mathbf{d}}^{!}\mathscr{G}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}  for  △ ∈ { 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 , 𝒮 ​ 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 } \triangle\in\{\mathcal{SN},\mathcal{SSN}\}  respectively. This is precisely what is obtained by applying the strictly monoidal functor  H ∗ ı △ ! \HO^{*}\!\!\imath_{\triangle}^{!}  to Theorem  1.1 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 9.4.  The fully nilpotent CoHA and BPS algebras 

 
 Let  Q = ( Q 0 , Q 1 ) Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})  be a quiver. Recall the embedding of monoids  ı 𝒩 : 𝐍 Q 0 → ℳ Π Q \imath_{\mathcal{N}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}  sending  𝐝 ↦ 0 𝐝 \mathbf{d}\mapsto 0_{\mathbf{d}} , where  0 𝐝 0_{\mathbf{d}}  is the  𝐝 \mathbf{d} -dimensional representation of  Π Q \Pi_{Q}  on which all arrows of  Q ¯ \overline{Q}  act by  0 0 . The  fully nilpotent cohomological Hall algebra  is  𝒜 ¯ Π Q 𝒩 ≔ ı 𝒩 ! 𝒜 ¯ Π Q \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{N}}\coloneqq\imath_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}} . This is a (cohomologically graded) mixed Hodge module on  𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  and it carries the CoHA product given by  ı 𝒩 ! 𝔪 \imath_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\mathfrak{m} . It has sub-mixed Hodge modules given by  BPS 𝒩 Π Q ≔ ı 𝒩 ! ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Alg \mathrm{BPS}^{\mathcal{N}}_{\Pi_{Q}}\coloneqq\imath_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}  (the fully nilpotent BPS algebra) and  BPS Π Q , Lie 𝒩 ≔ ı 𝒩 ! ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 ¯ Π Q , Lie \mathrm{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathcal{N}}\coloneqq\imath_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}  (the fully nilpotent BPS Lie algebra). As usual, we let  𝒜 ¯ Π Q 𝒩 , ψ \underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{N},\psi}  and  BPS Π Q , Alg 𝒩 , ψ \mathrm{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathcal{N},\psi}  be the corresponding  ψ \psi -twisted algebras. We have an inclusion of algebras  BPS Π Q , Alg 𝒩 , ψ ⊆ 𝒜 ¯ Π Q 𝒩 , ψ \mathrm{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathcal{N},\psi}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{N},\psi}  and an inclusion of Lie algebras  BPS Π Q , Lie 𝒩 ⊆ BPS Π Q , Alg 𝒩 , ψ \mathrm{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathcal{N}}\subseteq\mathrm{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathcal{N},\psi} . We can summarize in the following proposition the properties satisfied by these algebras. 
 
 
 Proposition 9.13 . 
 
 We have the following: 
 
 (1)  
 
 BPS Π Q , Alg 𝒩 , ψ ≅ 𝐔 ⁡ ( BPS Π Q , Lie 𝒩 ) \mathrm{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathcal{N},\psi}\cong\UEA(\mathrm{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathcal{N}}) , 
 
 (2)  
 
 ch 𝐙 ​ ( BPS Π Q , Lie 𝒩 ) = ∑ 𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 A Q , 𝐝 ​ ( q ) ​ z 𝐝 \mathrm{ch}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathrm{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathcal{N}})=\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}A_{Q,\mathbf{d}}(q)z^{\mathbf{d}} , 
 
 (3)  
 
 BPS Π Q , Lie 𝒩 ≅ 𝔫 Π Q 𝒩 , + \mathrm{BPS}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathcal{N}}\cong\mathfrak{n}^{\mathcal{N},+}_{\Pi_{Q}}  is the GKM Lie algebra associated to the monoid  𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  with the symmetrised Euler form of  Q Q  and the weight function 
 

 
 
 Φ Π Q + → 𝐍 [ t ± 1 / 2 ] 𝐝 ↦ ∑ j ∈ 𝐙 dim H j ( 𝔻 𝒢 𝐝 ) t − j / 2 = C Q , 𝐝 abs ​ ( t ) . \begin{matrix}\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}^{+}&\rightarrow&\mathbf{N}[t^{\pm 1/2}]&\\
\mathbf{d}&\mapsto&\sum_{j\in\mathbf{Z}}\dim\HO^{j}(\mathbb{D}\mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{d}})t^{-j/2}&=C_{Q,\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{abs}}(t).\end{matrix} 
 
 
 where  𝒢 𝐝 \mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{d}}  is as in § 6.4.4 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows from the application of the strictly monoidal functor  ı 𝒩 ! \imath_{\mathcal{N}}^{!}  to Theorem  1.1 , and the fact that the perverse sheaves  𝒢 𝐝 \mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{d}}  are Verdier self-dual, Proposition  3.9  and Theorem  9.5 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 10.  Nakajima quiver varieties and lowest weight modules 

 
 10.1.  Semistable modules 

 
 Let  Q = ( Q 1 , Q 0 , s , t : Q 1 → Q 0 ) Q=(Q_{1},Q_{0},s,t\colon Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{0})  be a quiver. A (King)  stability condition  for  Q Q  is a tuple  ζ ∈ 𝐐 Q 0 \zeta\in\mathbf{Q}^{Q_{0}} . For  𝐝 ∈ 𝐙 Q 0 ∖ { 0 } \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{Z}^{Q_{0}}\smallsetminus\{0\}  we define the  slope 
 

 
 
 μ ζ ( 𝐝 ) = ζ ⋅ 𝐝 / | 𝐝 | \mu_{\zeta}(\mathbf{d})=\zeta\!\cdot\!\mathbf{d}/\lvert\mathbf{d}\lvert 
 
 
 and for  ρ \rho  a nonzero  𝐂 ​ Q \mathbf{C}Q -module, we define  μ ζ ​ ( ρ ) = μ ζ ​ ( dim Q 0 ( ρ ) ) \mu_{\zeta}(\rho)=\mu_{\zeta}(\dim_{Q_{0}}(\rho)) . Then  ρ \rho  is called  ζ \zeta - semistable  if for all nonzero proper submodules  ρ ′ ⊆ ρ \rho^{\prime}\subseteq\rho  we have  μ ζ ​ ( ρ ′ ) ⩽ μ ζ ​ ( ρ ) \mu_{\zeta}(\rho^{\prime})\leqslant\mu_{\zeta}(\rho) , and is called  ζ \zeta - stable  if the inequality is strict. A semistable module is called  polystable  if it is a direct sum of stable modules. 
 
 
 Fix a quiver  Q Q , a potential  W ∈ 𝐂 ​ Q / [ 𝐂 ​ Q , 𝐂 ​ Q ] W\in\mathbf{C}Q/[\mathbf{C}Q,\mathbf{C}Q] , a stability condition  ζ ∈ 𝐐 Q 0 \zeta\in\mathbf{Q}^{Q_{0}} , and a slope  θ ∈ 𝐐 \theta\in\mathbf{Q}  such that for all dimension vectors  𝐝 , 𝐝 ′ \mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}  of  ζ \zeta -slope  θ \theta  we have  χ Q ​ ( 𝐝 , 𝐝 ′ ) = χ Q ​ ( 𝐝 ′ , 𝐝 ) \chi_{Q}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})=\chi_{Q}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{d}) . This condition is called  genericity , and will be automatic for all the quivers that we encounter, since they will be symmetric. 
 
 
 We denote by  𝔐 Q , 𝐝 ζ ​ sst \mathfrak{M}^{\zeta\sstab}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  the moduli stack of  𝐝 \mathbf{d} -dimensional  ζ \zeta -semistable  𝐂 ​ Q \mathbf{C}Q -modules, and by  ℳ Q , 𝐝 ζ ​ sst \mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sstab}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  the moduli scheme, constructed via GIT in  [ Kin94 ] . Points of  ℳ Q , 𝐝 ζ ​ sst \mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sstab}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  are in bijection with isomorphism classes of  𝐝 \mathbf{d} -dimensional polystable  𝐂 ​ Q \mathbf{C}Q -modules. We denote by  𝙹𝙷 ζ : 𝔐 Q , 𝐝 ζ ​ sst → ℳ Q , 𝐝 ζ ​ sst \JH^{\zeta}\colon\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta\sstab}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sstab}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  the morphism taking a semistable module to the associated polystable module. The potential  W W  defines a function  Tr ⁡ ( W ) \Tr(W)  on  𝔐 Q , 𝐝 ζ ​ sst \mathfrak{M}^{\zeta\sstab}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  defined as in § 8.1 , factoring through  ℳ Q , 𝐝 ζ ​ sst \mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sstab}_{Q,\mathbf{d}} . 
 
 
 The BPS sheaf is defined in  [ DM20 ]  by setting 
 

 
 
 ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 Q , W , 𝐝 ζ ≔ ( τ ⩽ 1 𝔭 ​ 𝙹𝙷 ∗ ζ ​ ϕ Tr ⁡ ( W ) 𝔭 ​ 𝐐 𝔐 Q , 𝐝 ζ ​ sst vir ) ​ [ 1 ] . \mathcal{BPS}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}^{\zeta}\coloneqq({{}^{\mathfrak{p}}\tau}^{\leqslant 1}\!\JH^{\zeta}_{*}{}^{\mathfrak{p}}\!\phi_{\Tr(W)}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta\sstab}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathrm{vir}})[1]. 
 
 
 It is a perverse sheaf (not just a complex of perverse sheaves). It also admits a canonical upgrade to a monodromic mixed Hodge module, which we will ignore in this section. 
 
 
 Now assume that  Q Q  is symmetric, so the stability condition  ζ ′ = ( 0 , … , 0 ) \zeta^{\prime}=(0,\ldots,0)  is generic. Since every  𝐂 ​ Q \mathbf{C}Q -module is then semistable, we can identify  𝔐 Q , 𝐝 ζ ′ ​ sst = 𝔐 Q , 𝐝 \mathfrak{M}^{\zeta^{\prime}\sstab}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}=\mathfrak{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}  and  ℳ Q , 𝐝 ζ ′ ​ sst = ℳ Q , 𝐝 \mathcal{M}^{\zeta^{\prime}\sstab}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}=\mathcal{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}} . We denote by 
 

 
 
 q ζ , 𝐝 : ℳ Q , 𝐝 ζ ​ sst → ℳ Q , 𝐝 q_{\zeta,\mathbf{d}}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sstab}_{Q,\mathbf{d}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{Q,\mathbf{d}} 
 
 
 the affinization/forgetful map, taking a polystable module to its semisimplification. Where there is no ambiguity we omit the subscript  Q Q . The following is proved in  [ Tod17 , Lemma 4.7] . 
 
 
 Proposition 10.1 . 
 
 Let  Q Q  be a symmetric quiver with potential  W ∈ 𝐂 ​ Q / [ 𝐂 ​ Q , 𝐂 ​ Q ] W\in\mathbf{C}Q/[\mathbf{C}Q,\mathbf{C}Q] , let  ζ ∈ 𝐐 Q 0 \zeta\in\mathbf{Q}^{Q_{0}}  be a stability condition. Then there is a natural isomorphism 
 

 
 
 ( q ζ , 𝐝 ) ∗ ​ ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 Q , W , 𝐝 ζ ≅ ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 Q , W , 𝐝 . (q_{\zeta,\mathbf{d}})_{*}\mathcal{BPS}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}^{\zeta}\cong\mathcal{BPS}_{Q,W,\mathbf{d}}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 10.2.  From Nakajima quiver varieties to BPS cohomology 

 
 Let  𝐟 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{f}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  be a dimension vector. We form the framed quiver  Q 𝐟 Q_{\mathbf{f}}  by setting  ( Q 𝐟 ) 0 = Q 0 ∪ { ∞ } (Q_{\mathbf{f}})_{0}=Q_{0}\cup\{\infty\}  and setting  ( Q 𝐟 ) 1 = Q 1 ∪ I (Q_{\mathbf{f}})_{1}=Q_{1}\cup I  where  I = { α i , n | i ∈ Q 0 , 1 ⩽ n ⩽ f i } I=\{\alpha_{i,n}\;\lvert\;i\in Q_{0},1\leqslant n\leqslant f_{i}\} . We set  s ⁡ ( α i , n ) = ∞ s(\alpha_{i,n})=\infty  and  t ⁡ ( α i , n ) = i t(\alpha_{i,n})=i . Given a dimension vector  𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 \mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}  and a number  m ∈ 𝐍 m\in\mathbf{N}  we denote by  ( 𝐝 , m ) ∈ 𝐍 ( Q 𝐟 ) 0 = 𝐍 ( Q 𝐟 ¯ ) 0 (\mathbf{d},m)\in\mathbf{N}^{(Q_{\mathbf{f}})_{0}}=\mathbf{N}^{(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}})_{0}}  the dimension vector extending  𝐝 \mathbf{d}  by assigning  m m  to the vertex  ∞ \infty . We say a  ( 𝐝 , 1 ) (\mathbf{d},1) -dimensional  Q 𝐟 ¯ \overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}} -module is  stable  if it does not contain a  ( 𝐝 ′ , 1 ) (\mathbf{d}^{\prime},1) -dimensional submodule with  𝐝 ′ ≠ 𝐝 \mathbf{d}^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{d} . This is the same as  ζ \zeta -stability, for  ζ = ( 0 , … , 0 , 1 ) \zeta=(0,\ldots,0,1) . We denote by  𝐀 Q 𝐟 ¯ , ( 𝐝 , 1 ) s ⊆ 𝐀 Q 𝐟 ¯ , ( 𝐝 , 1 ) \mathbf{A}_{\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}},(\mathbf{d},1)}^{\stab}\subseteq\mathbf{A}_{\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}},(\mathbf{d},1)}  the stable locus, and define  μ Q 𝐟 , 𝐝 − 1 ​ ( 0 ) s ⊆ μ Q 𝐟 , 𝐝 − 1 ​ ( 0 ) \mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(0)^{\stab}\subseteq\mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(0)  similarly, where  μ Q 𝐟 , 𝐝 ≔ μ Q 𝐟 , ( 𝐝 , 1 ) \mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},\mathbf{d}}\coloneqq\mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},(\mathbf{d},1)}  is the moment map (§ 2 ). The gauge group  GL 𝐝 \mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}}  acts scheme-theoretically freely on  μ Q 𝐟 , 𝐝 − 1 ​ ( 0 ) s \mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(0)^{\stab}  and we define the  Nakajima quiver variety 
 

 
 
 N ⁡ ( Q , 𝐝 , 𝐟 ) ≔ μ Q 𝐟 , 𝐝 − 1 ​ ( 0 ) s / GL 𝐝 . N(Q,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f})\coloneqq\mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(0)^{\stab}/\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}}. 
 
 
 We define the singular Nakajima quiver variety by 
 

 
 
 N ′ ( Q , 𝐝 , 𝐟 ) = μ Q 𝐟 , 𝐝 − 1 ( 0 ) / / GL 𝐝 = Spec ( Γ ( μ Q 𝐟 , 𝐝 − 1 ( 0 ) ) GL 𝐝 ) ⊆ ℳ Q 𝐟 ¯ , ( 1 , 𝐝 ) . N^{\prime}(Q,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f})=\mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(0)/\!\!/\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}}=\operatorname{Spec}(\Gamma(\mu_{Q_{\mathbf{f}},\mathbf{d}}^{-1}(0))^{\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{d}}})\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}},(1,\mathbf{d})}. 
 
 
 For  △ ∈ { 𝒩 , 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 , 𝒮 ​ 𝒮 ​ 𝒩 } \triangle\in\{\mathcal{N},\mathcal{SN},\mathcal{SSN}\} , we denote by  N △ ​ ( Q , 𝐝 , 𝐟 ) ⊆ N ⁡ ( Q , 𝐝 , 𝐟 ) N^{\triangle}(Q,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f})\subseteq N(Q,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f})  the subvariety corresponding to representations of  Π Q 𝐟 \Pi_{Q_{\mathbf{f}}}  satisfying  △ \triangle . 
 
 
 We set  𝒬 = ( 𝒬 0 , 𝒬 1 ) ≔ Q 𝐟 ~ \mathscr{Q}=(\mathscr{Q}_{0},\mathscr{Q}_{1})\coloneqq\widetilde{Q_{\mathbf{f}}} , the tripled quiver of the framed quiver defined as in § 2 , and give this quiver the canonical cubic cubic potential 
 

 
 
 W ~ = ( ∑ i ∈ ( Q 𝐟 ) 0 ω i ) ​ ( ∑ a ∈ ( Q 𝐟 ) 1 [ a , a ∗ ] ) . \widetilde{W}=\left(\sum_{i\in(Q_{\mathbf{f}})_{0}}\omega_{i}\right)\left(\sum_{a\in(Q_{\mathbf{f}})_{1}}[a,a^{*}]\right). 
 
 
 We fix the stability condition  ( 0 , … , 0 , 1 ) ∈ 𝐐 𝒬 0 (0,\ldots,0,1)\in\mathbf{Q}^{\mathscr{Q}_{0}} . In words, a  ( 𝐝 , 1 ) (\mathbf{d},1) -dimensional  𝒬 \mathscr{Q} -module is semistable, equivalently stable, if it is generated by the one-dimensional vector space assigned to the vertex  ∞ \infty . There is a closed embedding of varieties 
 

 
 
 l : N ⁡ ( Q , 𝐝 , 𝐟 ) × 𝐀 1 ↪ ℳ 𝒬 , ( 𝐝 , 1 ) ζ ​ sst l\colon N(Q,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f})\times\mathbf{A}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{Q},(\mathbf{d},1)}^{\zeta\sstab} 
 
 
 which extends a  Q 𝐟 ¯ \overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}} -module to a  𝒬 \mathscr{Q} -module by assigning the operator of multiplication by  z ∈ 𝐀 1 z\in\mathbf{A}^{1}  to all the loops  ω i ∈ 𝒬 1 ∖ ( Q 𝐟 ¯ ) 1 \omega_{i}\in\mathscr{Q}_{1}\smallsetminus(\overline{Q_{\mathbf{f}}})_{1} . 
 
 
 Proposition 10.2   ( [ Dav20 ]  Proposition 6.3) . 
 
 Let  𝒬 \mathscr{Q} ,  W ~ \widetilde{W}  and  ζ \zeta  be as above. Then 
 

 
 
 ℬ ​ 𝒫 ​ 𝒮 𝒬 , W ~ , ( 𝐝 , 1 ) ζ ≅ l ∗ ​ 𝐐 N ⁡ ( Q , 𝐝 , 𝐟 ) × 𝐀 1 vir . \mathcal{BPS}^{\zeta}_{\mathscr{Q},\widetilde{W},(\mathbf{d},1)}\cong l_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{N(Q,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f})\times\mathbf{A}^{1}}^{\mathrm{vir}}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof in [loc.cit.] concerns the quiver  Q + Q^{+}  obtained after removing the loop  ω ∞ \omega_{\infty}  from  𝒬 \mathscr{Q} , but adapts without modification to our setup.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Combining Propositions  10.1  and  10.2  yields the following:

 Set 
 

 
 
 𝕄 𝐟 △ ( Q ) = ⨁ 𝐝 ∈ 𝐍 Q 0 H ∗ ( N △ ( Q , 𝐝 , 𝐟 ) , ı △ ! 𝐐 N ⁡ ( Q , 𝐝 , 𝐟 ) vir ) . \mathbb{M}^{\triangle}_{\mathbf{f}}(Q)=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}\HO^{*}(N^{\triangle}(Q,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f}),\imath^{!}_{\triangle}\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{N(Q,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{f})}).
